Biological control

Parasitoid
parasitoids
Often free-living
Biological control

Predator=Many prey 
Parasitoid=One host per developing parasitoid 

18. Important Parasitoid Groups
Major parasitoid groups include:
- Trichogrammatidae
- Braconidae
- Ichneumonidae
- Aphelinidae
- Encyrtidae
- Eulophidae
These families contain many species used naturally or commercially in biological control.

## 19. Trichogramma
Trichogramma are very small egg parasitoid wasps.
They attack insect eggs, especially eggs of certain moths and butterflies.
The general mechanism is:
Trichogramma→Pest egg→Parasitism→Pest embryo fails 
This is particularly useful because killing the pest at the egg stage prevents the damaging larva from
developing.

20. Braconid Wasps
Braconid wasps include many parasitoid species.
Different species attack:

- Caterpillars
- Aphids
- Beetle larvae
- Other insects
Some lay eggs inside hosts, where their immature offspring develop.

21. Ichneumonid Wasps
Ichneumonidae contains many parasitoid species.
They attack various insect hosts, including:
- Caterpillars
- Pupae
- Larvae of other insects
They are important components of natural ecosystems and biological control.

22. Aphid Parasitoids
Some parasitoid wasps attack aphids.
A characteristic result of parasitism is an aphid that becomes a:
Mummy  
The parasitized aphid is transformed internally by the developing parasitoid.
Mummies are therefore an important field indicator of parasitoid activity.

23. Pollinating Insects
Pollinators transfer pollen between flowers and contribute to sexual reproduction in flowering plants.
Important insect pollinators include:
- Honey bees
- Bumble bees
- Solitary bees
- Some flies
- Butterflies

- Moths
- Some beetles
The basic process is:
Anther→Pollen transfer→Stigma→Pollination 

24. Honey Bees
Honey bees are among the most economically important insect pollinators.
They visit flowers to collect:
- Nectar
- Pollen
During flower visits, pollen can be transferred between flowers.
This supports fruit and seed production in many crops.

## 25. Butterflies
Butterflies can contribute to pollination while feeding on floral nectar.
They are generally less specialized as crop pollinators than some managed bee systems, but they are
important components of biodiversity and pollination networks.

## 26. Moths
Many moths are nocturnal pollinators.
Their importance can be overlooked because they are active at night.
Thus:
Nocturnal insect≠unimportant insect    

27. Beetles as Pollinators
Some beetles visit flowers and transfer pollen.
Beetle pollination is particularly important in some plant groups.
Their role demonstrates that pollination is not limited to bees and butterflies.

28. Decomposer Insects
Some insects help break down:
- Dead plant material
- Animal material
- Organic residues
- Manure
- Other organic matter
This contributes to nutrient cycling.
Conceptually:
Organic material→Fragmentation→Microbial decomposition→Nutrient cycling 
Insects can accelerate the physical breakdown and redistribution of organic material.

29. Dung Beetles
Dung beetles use animal dung as food or reproductive material.
They can:
- Break up dung
- Incorporate organic matter into soil
- Redistribute nutrients
- Improve nutrient cycling
Thus:
Dung→Dung beetle activity→Soil nutrient cycling 

30. Beneficial Insects and Soil
Some insects contribute indirectly to soil health through:
- Organic matter breakdown
- Bioturbation

- Nutrient redistribution
- Food-web interactions
However, insects are only one component of soil biological activity.
Soil microorganisms, earthworms and other organisms also play major roles.

31. Beneficial Insects and Ecosystem Balance
A healthy agricultural ecosystem contains multiple trophic levels:
Plants→Herbivores→Predators/Parasitoids→Higher trophic organisms 
Beneficial insects can help prevent herbivore populations from increasing unchecked.

32. Natural Biological Control
When naturally occurring beneficial organisms suppress pests, this is called natural biological
control.
Conceptually:
Natural enemies→Pest mortality→Pest population regulation 
Farm management can protect and enhance this natural control.

33. Conservation Biological Control
Conservation biological control means modifying agricultural practices to protect naturally occurring
natural enemies.
Examples include:
- Reducing unnecessary broad-spectrum insecticide use
- Maintaining flowering resources where appropriate
- Providing refuge/habitat
- Maintaining crop diversity
- Avoiding unnecessary destruction of natural enemies
- Using selective control measures where appropriate
The objective is:

Natural enemy survival↑→Natural control↑ 

34. Augmentative Biological Control
In augmentative biological control, beneficial organisms are released into the crop to increase their
population.
Examples include releases of:
- Trichogramma
- Certain parasitoid wasps
- Predatory insects
- Other biological control agents
The basic concept is:
Mass production→Release→Natural enemy population↑→Pest suppression 

35. Beneficial Insects in IPM
Beneficial insects are an important component of Integrated Pest Management (IPM).
The basic IPM approach is:
IDENTIFY→MONITOR→ASSESS→DECIDE→MANAGE 
Before applying an insecticide, farmers should consider whether natural enemies are already
suppressing the pest.

36. Natural Enemy to Pest Relationship
The effectiveness of natural enemies depends on:
- Pest density
- Natural enemy density
- Crop structure
- Weather
- Pesticide exposure
- Availability of alternative prey

- Habitat
- Timing
- Synchronization between predator/parasitoid and pest
A simplified relationship is:
Natural enemy abundance↑→Potential pest mortality↑ 
But this is not necessarily a linear relationship.

37. Why Beneficial Insects Should Be Protected
Unnecessary killing of beneficial insects can cause:
Natural enemies↓→Pest regulation↓→Pest resurgence risk↑ 
This is one reason indiscriminate pesticide use can disrupt IPM.

38. Pesticides and Beneficial Insects
Pesticides can affect beneficial insects through:
- Direct toxicity
- Residues
- Contaminated prey
- Loss of habitat
- Reduced food resources
Therefore, pesticide selection should consider:
- Target pest
- Natural enemies present
- Product label
- Mode of action
- Application timing
- Exposure
- Crop stage

A pesticide being effective against a pest does not mean it is harmless to all beneficial organisms.

39. Selectivity in IPM
The goal is often:
Maximum pest suppression+Minimum natural-enemy disruption  +    
This is one reason selective pest-management approaches are important.

40. Beneficial Insect Monitoring
Farmers should monitor beneficial insects just as they monitor pests.
Record:
- Species/group
- Life stage
- Number
- Location
- Feeding behavior
- Pest species present
- Pest density
- Crop stage
For example:
Predator:pest ratio=Number of predatorsNumber of pests  Number\ of\ pests   
This ratio can provide useful context, but there is no universal predator:pest ratio that guarantees
control. Thresholds are pest- and crop-specific.

41. How to Identify a Beneficial Insect
Do not identify an insect as beneficial simply because it looks unusual.
Use:
Identification=Morphology+Behavior+Life stage+Host/prey+Field context 

Ask:
1. What insect is it?
2. What life stage is it?
3. What is it feeding on?
4. Is it feeding on the crop or another insect?
5. Is it parasitizing another insect?
6. Is it visiting flowers?
7. Is it associated with decomposing material?

42. Beneficial Insect vs Pest
Feature
Relationship with
crop
Food source
Agricultural effect
Role in IPM
Example
Management goal

Beneficial insect
Often positive/neutral

Pest insect
Harmful

Pests, nectar, pollen, organic material etc. Crop/plant/animal products
Pest
Economic damage
suppression/pollination/decomposition
Often protected/enhanced
Monitored/managed
Ladybird beetle
Aphid
Suppress when economically
Conserve
justified

43. Beneficial Insects vs Natural Enemies
These terms are related but not identical.

Beneficial insect
Broad category.
Can include:
- Predators
- Parasitoids
- Pollinators
- Decomposers

Natural enemy
More specifically refers to organisms that suppress another organism, especially pests.

Thus:
Natural enemies⊆Beneficial organisms 
But not every beneficial insect is a natural enemy.
For example:
Honey bee = beneficial pollinator
but it is not normally considered a natural enemy of crop pests.

44. Beneficial Insects vs Biological Control
Agents
A biological control agent is an organism deliberately or naturally used to suppress pests.
Beneficial insects may become biological control agents when they are used for pest suppression.
For example:
Predatory insect→Biological control agent   But a pollinator such as a honey bee is beneficial without being a pest-control agent.

45. Major Beneficial Insect Examples
Beneficial insect/group
Main service
Ladybird beetles
Predation
Lacewings
Predation
Syrphid fly larvae
Predation
Assassin bugs
Predation
Ground beetles
Predation
Rove beetles
Predation
Mantises
Predation
Trichogramma
Egg parasitism
Braconid wasps
Parasitoid activity
Ichneumonid wasps
Parasitoid activity
Aphid parasitoids
Aphid suppression
Honey bees
Pollination
Bumble bees
Pollination
Butterflies
Pollination
Moths
Pollination

Beneficial insect/group
Main service
Some beetles
Pollination
Dung beetles
Decomposition/nutrient cycling

46. Important Concept: Beneficial Insect ≠
Always Beneficial
An insect's role depends on:
- Species
- Life stage
- Food source
- Crop
- Environment
- Ecological interaction
For example, an insect may be:
- Beneficial as a larva
- Neutral as an adult
- r:
- Pollinating as an adult
- Pest-related at another stage
Therefore:
Identify the insect AND its behavior   

47. Field Example
Suppose aphids are found on a crop.
You observe:
- Aphids
- Ladybird larvae
- Adult ladybirds
- Aphid mummies
A simplistic approach would see only the aphids and recommend immediate spraying.

An IPM approach recognizes:
Aphids=Pest    Ladybirds=Predators   
Aphid mummies=Evidence of parasitoid activity   
Therefore, the natural-enemy population should be considered before choosing a management action.

48. Beneficial Insects and Pollination
Pollination is essential for sexual reproduction in many crops.
The simplified sequence is:
Flower→Insect visit→Pollen transfer→Stigma→Fertilization→Fruit/seed development 
Therefore, protecting pollinators can support crop production where insect pollination is important.

49. Beneficial Insects and Biodiversity
Greater habitat diversity can provide:
- Nectar
- Pollen
- Shelter
- Alternative prey
- Overwintering sites
- Breeding sites
This can support beneficial insect communities.
However, habitat management should be designed around the target crop, pest and beneficial species.

50. Beneficial Insects and Agroecosystem Design
A simplified ecological model is:
Crop diversity+Flowering resources+Habitat+Reduced unnecessary pesticide pressure→Beneficial inse
ct conservation 

This supports the broader IPM goal of managing the agroecosystem, rather than simply killing insects.

51. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1: Killing every insect
Incorrect.
Not every insect is a pest  

Mistake 2: Killing unfamiliar larvae
Some beneficial insects have larvae that look very different from adults.

Mistake 3: Assuming all beetles are pests
False. Many beetles are important predators or decomposers.

Mistake 4: Assuming all wasps are harmful
False. Many parasitoid wasps are valuable biological-control agents.

Mistake 5: Assuming every insect visiting flowers is a pest
Many are pollinators.

Mistake 6: Assuming natural enemies always eliminate pests
Natural enemies can suppress pest populations, but their effectiveness depends on ecological
conditions.

52. Beneficial Insects in Sustainable Agriculture
Beneficial insects contribute to sustainable agriculture by reducing dependence on chemical pest
control and supporting ecosystem functions.
Conceptually:
Beneficial insects→Natural pest control+Pollination+Nutrient cycling→Agroecosystem resilience ed  Beneficial\ insects  Natural\ pest\ control + Pollination + Nutrient\ cycling  Agroecosystem\ resilience  
They are therefore an important part of:
- IPM
- Ecological agriculture
- Conservation agriculture

- Biodiversity management
- Sustainable crop production

53. Master Classification
The easiest way to remember beneficial insects is:
BENEFICIAL INSECTS   

## 1. Predators
Ladybird + Lacewing + Assassin bug + Ground beetle  

## 2. Parasitoids
Trichogramma + Braconids + Ichneumonids  

## 3. Pollinators
Honey bee + Bumble bee + Butterfly + Moth  

## 4. Decomposers
Dung beetles + Other decomposer-associated insects  

54. Final One-Read Summary
Beneficial insects are insects that provide useful services to agriculture and ecosystems.
The major categories are:
Predators+Parasitoids+Pollinators+Decomposers  +   +
  +    

Predators
They consume multiple pest individuals:
Predator→Many prey 
Examples:
- Ladybird beetles
- Lacewings

- Assassin bugs
- Ground beetles
- Syrphid larvae

Parasitoids
Their immature stages develop on or inside a host and generally kill that host:
Parasitoid→Host→Host death 
Examples:
- Trichogramma
- Braconid wasps
- Ichneumonid wasps
- Aphid parasitoids

Pollinators
They transfer pollen:
Flower visit→Pollen transfer→Pollination 
Examples:
- Honey bees
- Bumble bees
- Butterflies
- Moths
- Some flies and beetles

Decomposers
They assist in breaking down organic material and nutrient cycling.
A key IPM principle is:
IDENTIFY BEFORE YOU KILL   
because:
Insect found in crop≠Pest automatically    
The ultimate relationship is:

BENEFICIAL INSECTS→PEST SUPPRESSION + POLLINATION + DECOMPOSITION→HEALT
HIER AGROECOSYSTEM     
Ultimate memory line
Predators eat pests, parasitoids kill hosts from within/on them, pollinators move
pollen, and decomposer insects help recycle organic matter.

## Module 15 — Insect & Pest
## Management
## 10. Predatory Insects
## 1. Introduction
Predators are one of the most important groups of beneficial insects in agriculture. They naturally
suppress pest populations by capturing and consuming other organisms, particularly insect pests.
The basic relationship is:
Predator→Prey→Prey mortality    
Unlike parasitoids, which generally develop in association with one host, a predator typically consumes
many prey individuals during its lifetime.
Therefore:
Predator = Many prey  
Predatory insects are an important component of biological control and Integrated Pest Management
(IPM).

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
A predator is an organism that captures and consumes other organisms called prey.

Agricultural definition
A predatory insect is a beneficial insect that actively captures and consumes one or
more other organisms, usually pests, during its development or adult life.

Exam-ready definition
Predatory insects are natural enemies that kill and consume multiple prey individuals
during their lifetime and thereby contribute to the biological suppression of
agricultural pests.

3. Predator vs Prey
The two terms are fundamental.

Predator
The organism that captures and consumes another organism.

Prey
The organism that is captured and consumed.
For example:
Ladybird beetle=Predator   
Aphid=Prey   
Relationship:
Ladybird→Aphid→Aphid mortality 

## 4. Predation
Predation is the biological interaction in which one organism captures and consumes another.
In agriculture:
Predation=Search+Detection+Capture+Consumption  +
  +   +    
The predator may repeat this process many times.

5. Predators in IPM
Predators are part of the natural-enemy component of IPM.
The general IPM framework is:
Identify→Monitor→Assess→Decide→Manage 
Predators can contribute to the manage stage through natural biological control.

6. Major Characteristics of Predatory Insects
Predatory insects commonly have:
- Active searching behavior
- Specialized prey-detection abilities
- Strong movement ability
- Grasping or piercing mouthparts
- Good visual or chemical sensing
- Rapid response to prey
- Ability to consume multiple prey
A generalized predator cycle is:
Search→Detect→Capture→Kill→Consume→Search again 

7. Predators vs Parasitoids
This is one of the most important distinctions.
Feature
Predator
Parasitoid
Number of prey/hosts Usually many
Usually one host per developing parasitoid
Feeding
Direct consumption
Develops on/in host
Host normally killed During/after predation
Usually required for successful development
Searching
Actively searches for prey Searches for suitable host
Example
Ladybird beetle
Trichogramma
Role
Natural biological control Natural biological control
Memory:
Predator = Many prey  
Parasitoid = One host for development  

8. Important Predatory Insects
Major groups include:
1. Ladybird beetles
## 2. Lacewings
3. Syrphid fly larvae

4. Assassin bugs
5. Predatory stink bugs
6. Ground beetles
7. Rove beetles
## 8. Mantises
9. Predatory earwigs
10.Some dragonflies and damselflies
11.Some predatory wasps
12.Some predatory thrips

9. Ladybird Beetles
Ladybird beetles are among the best-known agricultural predators.
They can prey on:
- Aphids
- Scale insects
- Mealybugs
- Whiteflies
- Other small soft-bodied insects
Both larvae and adults are predatory in many species.
Life cycle:
Egg→Larva→Pupa→Adult 

10. Ladybird Larvae
Ladybird larvae are particularly important because they can consume large numbers of prey during
development.
They often appear:
- Elongated
- Dark-colored
- Spiny or segmented

They may be mistaken for pests.
Therefore:
Correct identification is essential before control   

## 11. Lacewings
Lacewings are important predatory insects.
Their larvae are often called:
Aphid lions  
because many species prey heavily on aphids.
They can attack:
- Aphids
- Whiteflies
- Thrips
- Mites
- Small insect eggs
- Other soft-bodied insects

12. Green Lacewing Larvae
The larval stage is particularly predatory.
A typical sequence is:
Egg→Larva→Pupa→Adult 
The adult may feed on nectar, pollen or honeydew depending on species, while the larvae of many
species are active predators.
This is an important example of different ecological roles at different life stages.

13. Syrphid Fly Larvae
Syrphid flies are also known as hoverflies.
The adults commonly visit flowers and can contribute to pollination.

Their larvae of many species prey on:
- Aphids
- Small soft-bodied insects
Therefore:
Adult→Pollination  Larva→Predation 

14. Assassin Bugs
Assassin bugs are predatory true bugs.
They have piercing-sucking mouthparts adapted for capturing prey.
They may attack:
- Caterpillars
- Aphids
- Beetle larvae
- Other insects
Their feeding mechanism generally involves:
Capture→Piercing→Feeding on prey 

15. Predatory Stink Bugs
Not all stink bugs are plant pests.
Some species are predators of other insects.
They can attack:
- Caterpillars
- Larvae
- Eggs
- Other soft-bodied insects
This is an important identification lesson:
Stink bug≠necessarily plant pest   
The species and feeding behavior must be determined.

16. Ground Beetles
Ground beetles are mainly associated with the soil surface.
Many species prey on:
- Insect eggs
- Caterpillars
- Larvae
- Small insects
- Other arthropods
Some also consume weed seeds.
They can therefore contribute to both:
Pest suppression+Potential weed seed suppression 

17. Rove Beetles
Rove beetles are another important group of predators.
They can occur in:
- Soil
- Crop residues
- Organic matter
- Plant surfaces
Many species feed on:
- Small insects
- Eggs
- Larvae
- Other small organisms

18. Praying Mantises
Praying mantises are generalist predators.

They use specialized grasping forelegs to capture prey.
They may eat:
- Flies
- Grasshoppers
- Caterpillars
- Bugs
- Other insects
However:
Generalist predator   
means that they can consume both pests and beneficial insects.
Therefore, mantises should not automatically be regarded as the most suitable biological-control agent
for every pest.

19. Dragonflies and Damselflies
Dragonflies and damselflies are predators.
Adults commonly capture flying insects.
Their immature aquatic stages also prey on aquatic organisms.
They contribute to natural food webs, especially around:
- Wetlands
- Ponds
- Irrigation areas
- Other aquatic habitats

20. Predatory Earwigs
Some earwig species are omnivorous or predatory.
They may consume:
- Aphids
- Eggs
- Small larvae

- Other small insects
Their ecological role depends strongly on species and environment.

21. Predatory Thrips
Not all thrips are plant pests.
Some species are predators of:
- Mites
- Thrips larvae
- Small insects
- Other arthropods
This demonstrates again:
Identification→Behavior→Classification 
rather than judging an insect solely by its common name.

22. Predatory Wasps
Some adult wasps and other wasp groups can capture insect prey.
Depending on species, they may:
- Hunt caterpillars
- Capture flies
- Collect prey for larvae
- Kill prey during hunting
However, predatory wasps should be distinguished from parasitoid wasps.

23. What Do Predators Eat?
Predatory insects may consume:

Eggs
Predator→Pest eggPredator Pest\ egg

Larvae
Predator→Pest larvaPredator Pest\ larva

Nymphs
Predator→Pest nymphPredator Pest\ nymph

Adults
Predator→Pest adultPredator Pest\ adult
The preferred prey stage depends on predator species and prey species.

24. Predator Feeding Strategies
Predators may use different hunting strategies.

Active searching
The predator moves through the crop looking for prey.

Ambush predation
The predator waits and attacks when prey comes nearby.

Web-associated predation
Some predators exploit structures created by other organisms, although spiders themselves are not
insects.

Surface searching
Predators search leaves, stems and soil surfaces.

25. Predator Detection of Prey
Predators may locate prey using:
- Vision
- Odors
- Chemical cues
- Vibrations
- Movement
- Honeydew

- Plant-emitted signals associated with herbivore attack
For example, plants damaged by herbivores can release volatile chemicals that influence interactions
with natural enemies.
Conceptually:
Herbivore feeding→Plant chemical signals→Natural enemy attraction 

26. Predator Search Behavior
Predator efficiency depends on its ability to locate prey.
The general process is:
Search→Detect→Approach→Capture→Consume 
If prey density is very low, searching may require more time and energy.

27. Predator-Prey Relationship
Predator populations are influenced by prey populations.
A simplified relationship is:
Prey↑→Food availability↑→Predator reproduction/survival potential↑ 
Then:
Predator↑→Prey mortality↑ 
Eventually, prey populations may decline.
This is one mechanism behind natural population regulation.

28. Functional Response
A predator's functional response describes how the number of prey consumed changes as prey density
changes.
In general:
Prey density↑→Prey consumed per predator↑ 
until the predator reaches limitations such as:

- Handling time
- Digestive capacity
- Searching time
- Saturation
Therefore, prey consumption does not increase indefinitely.

29. Numerical Response
A numerical response refers to changes in predator abundance or reproduction as prey availability
changes.
Simplified:
Prey availability↑→Predator survival/reproduction↑→Predator population potential↑ 
Functional and numerical responses together influence biological control.

30. Predator-Prey Balance
A healthy agroecosystem may contain:
Pests+Predators+Parasitoids+Other organisms 
The objective of IPM is not necessarily to eliminate every pest.
Instead:
Keep pest population below economically damaging levels 
while conserving beneficial organisms.

31. Economic Injury Level and Predators
The Economic Injury Level (EIL) is the pest population density at which the cost of pest damage
becomes equivalent to the cost of control.
A common conceptual equation is:
EIL=CV×I×D×K  V I D K   
where:

- CC = cost of control
- VV = crop value
- II = injury per pest
- DD = damage per unit injury
- KK = proportion of pest population controlled
Predators can reduce the effective pest population and therefore influence whether pest density reaches
economically damaging levels.

32. Economic Threshold Level
The Economic Threshold Level (ETL) is generally the pest density at which management should be
initiated to prevent the pest from reaching the EIL.
Conceptually:
ETL<EIL 
Natural predators can affect pest population growth between these points.

33. Predators in Conservation Biological
Control
Conservation biological control aims to protect naturally occurring predators.
Important practices can include:
- Avoiding unnecessary broad-spectrum insecticide applications
- Maintaining suitable habitat
- Maintaining flowering resources where appropriate
- Providing shelter/refuge
- Reducing unnecessary disturbance
- Using selective pest-management approaches
Goal:
Predator survival↑→Natural control↑ 

34. Predator Enhancement
Predators can sometimes be supported by providing:

Food
- Nectar
- Pollen
- Honeydew
- Alternative prey

Shelter
- Vegetation
- Ground cover
- Field margins
- Crop residues where appropriate

Reduced pesticide exposure
- Selective products
- Correct timing
- Avoiding unnecessary applications

35. Generalist vs Specialist Predators
Generalist predator
Feeds on many different prey species.
Examples can include:
- Mantises
- Some ground beetles
- Some assassin bugs
Advantages:
- Can survive when one pest is absent
- Can consume several pest species
Limitations:

- May consume beneficial insects
- May switch prey

Specialist or prey-restricted predator
Feeds primarily on a narrow group of prey.
Advantages:
- More targeted interaction
Limitations:
- May decline when its preferred prey is absent

36. Predator and Crop Environment
Predator effectiveness depends on:
- Temperature
- Humidity
- Rainfall
- Wind
- Crop architecture
- Vegetation density
- Prey abundance
- Alternative food
- Pesticide exposure
- Habitat availability
Therefore:
Predator effectiveness=f(Prey+Environment+Habitat+Management) 

37. Predator Life Stage
Different life stages of the same predator can have different ecological roles.
For example:
Egg→Larva/Nymph→Pupa→Adult 

Depending on species:
- Larvae may be highly predatory
- Adults may also be predatory
- Adults may additionally consume nectar/pollen
Therefore, field scouting should record the life stage.

38. Predator Density
A simple measure is:
Predator density=Number of predatorsSampling unit  Sampling\ unit   
Examples:
- Predators/plant
- Predators/m²
- Predators/100 leaves
- Predators/100 sweeps

39. Predator : Pest Ratio
A useful descriptive measure is:
Predator:Pest ratio=Number of predatorsNumber of pests  Number\ of\ pests   

Example
Suppose a field sample contains:
- 20 predators
- 100 aphids
Then:
Predator:Pest=20100=1:5Predator:Pest=  100 =1:5
This means there are 1 observed predator for every 5 observed pests in that sample.
Important: There is no universal predator:pest ratio that guarantees successful control. Interpretation
depends on species, crop, life stage, environment and established local thresholds.

40. Predator Monitoring
Useful methods include:

Visual counting
Inspect leaves, stems, flowers and shoots.

Sweep net
Useful for mobile insects.

Beat sampling
Shake or tap plants over a collection surface.

Sticky traps
Can monitor certain flying insects.

Soil sampling
Useful for ground-dwelling predators.

Pitfall traps
Useful for many ground-active arthropods.

41. Predator Identification
Identification should follow:
Observe→Identify→Determine life stage→Observe feeding behavior→Confirm 
Record:
- Species/group
- Life stage
- Location
- Number
- Prey species
- Feeding behavior
- Crop stage

- Weather
- Pesticide history

42. Predator vs Pest Identification
A common agricultural mistake is:
"There is an insect on my crop, so I should kill it."
Correct approach:
Insect found→Identify→Determine what it is eating→Assess whether it is harmful or beneficial   Insect\ found  Identify  Determine\ what\ it\ is\ eating  Assess\
whether\ it\ is\ harmful\ or\ beneficial  
For example:

Insect A
Eating crop leaves:
→Potential pest Insect B
Eating aphids:
→Predator 43. Predator and Pesticide Use
Broad-spectrum insecticides can reduce predator populations.
This can lead to:
Pesticide→Predator mortality→Natural control↓ 
If the pest survives or recovers faster than the predator population:
Pest resurgence risk↑ 
Therefore, pesticide decisions should account for natural enemies.

44. Predator Conservation in IPM
A good IPM program attempts to maintain:

Pest suppression+Natural enemy conservation 
Rather than:
Kill every insect   
This distinction is fundamental to sustainable pest management.

45. Predators and Biological Control
Biological control can be represented as:
Predator→Pest mortality→Pest population↓→Crop damage↓ 
When predators occur naturally:
Natural biological control
When predators are deliberately released:
Augmentative biological control

46. Common Predatory Insects — Quick Table
Predator
Common prey/food
Important role
Ladybird beetles
Aphids, scales, mealybugs
Pest predation

Pest predation

Pest predation

47. Predators and Parasitoids Together
Predators and parasitoids often work together.
For example:

Pest Predator attacksParasitoid attacks→Pest mortality  l 
 \\      .  Pest\ mortality  
This is called natural-enemy complex or natural-enemy community.
The combined effect can contribute substantially to pest regulation.

48. Important Concept: Do Not Destroy Natural
Enemies
When scouting a crop, recognize beneficial predators before applying control measures.
Especially watch for:
- Ladybird larvae
- Lacewing larvae
- Hoverfly larvae
- Assassin bugs
- Predatory stink bugs
- Ground beetles
- Other known natural enemies
These may be providing free biological control.

49. Common Misconceptions
Misconception 1: All beetles are pests
False.
Many beetles are predators.

Misconception 2: All bugs are pests
False.
Some true bugs are predators.

Misconception 3: Every wasp is harmful
False.
Many wasps are parasitoids or predators.

Misconception 4: A predator will eliminate every pest
False.
Predators regulate populations; they do not necessarily eliminate them.

Misconception 5: More predators always means complete control
Not necessarily.
Predator effectiveness depends on:
Prey+Environment+Habitat+Timing+Predator behaviorPrey+Environment+Habitat+Timing+Predator\
behavior

50. Practical Field Example
Imagine a soybean field with aphids.
You observe:
- 500 aphids
- 30 ladybird larvae
- 10 adult ladybirds
- Several lacewing larvae
A simple pest-only approach sees:
500 aphids500\ aphids
An IPM approach sees:
Aphids+Ladybirds+Lacewings 
The natural enemies may already be suppressing the aphid population.
Therefore the correct decision should be based on:
Pest density+Damage+Crop stage+Predator abundance+Predator activity+Threshold 
rather than aphid count alone.

51. Predator Role in the Pest Population Cycle
A simplified cycle is:

Pest↑→Food availability↑→Predator response↑→Pest mortality↑→Pest↓ 
This contributes to natural population regulation.

52. Predator–Pest–Crop Relationship
The complete agricultural relationship can be represented as:
Crop←Pest←Predator 
More accurately:
Crop→Pest herbivory→Predator response→Pest mortality→Reduced crop damage 
The crop, pest and natural enemy form an interconnected biological system.

53. Predator Importance in Sustainable
Agriculture
Predators can:
- Reduce pest populations
- Reduce crop damage
- Reduce dependence on insecticides
- Support biodiversity
- Improve ecological stability
- Provide free natural pest control
- Contribute to IPM
Thus:
Predators→Natural pest regulation→Sustainable crop protection 

54. Final One-Read Summary
Predatory insects are beneficial insects that capture and consume other organisms, particularly insect
pests.
The key relationship is:
Predator→Many prey→Pest mortality    
Major predatory insects include:
- Ladybird beetles
- Lacewings
- Syrphid fly larvae
- Assassin bugs
- Predatory stink bugs
- Ground beetles
- Rove beetles
- Mantises
- Dragonflies
- Predatory earwigs
- Predatory thrips
Their importance comes from their role in natural biological control.
The most important distinction is:
Predator = consumes many prey   
while:
Parasitoid = develops on/in a host and normally kills that host   
Predator effectiveness depends on:
Predator abundance+Prey abundance+Environment+Habitat+Timing+Management 
And the central IPM principle is:
## Identify → Monitor → Conserve Natural Enemies → Manage When Necessar
Y   
Ultimate memory line
Predators are nature's active pest hunters: they search for, capture and consume
multiple prey, helping keep pest populations below economically damaging levels.

## Module 15 — Insect & Pest
## Management
## 11. Parasitoids
## 1. Introduction
Parasitoids are among the most important natural enemies used in agricultural pest management. They
are insects whose immature stages develop on or inside another arthropod, called the host, and their
development ultimately kills the host.
They are especially important in biological control and Integrated Pest Management (IPM).
The basic relationship is:
Parasitoid→Host→Development→Host death     
The most important distinction to remember is:
Predator = many prey  
Parasitoid = generally one host per developing individual  

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
A parasitoid is an insect whose immature stage develops on or inside a host insect and eventually kills
that host.

Exam-ready definition
A parasitoid is an insect whose immature developmental stage lives on or within a
single host, obtaining nutrients from the host and ultimately causing the host's death.
Parasitoids are therefore different from ordinary parasites because the host generally does not survive
successful parasitoid development.

3. Parasitoid vs Parasite
These terms should not be confused.
Feature

Parasite

Host

Usually survives

Relationship

Long-term association

Food source
Common agricultural
example
Role in biological
control
Memory:

Host resources
Plant-parasitic nematodes are parasites,
but not insects
Some parasites can affect pests

Parasitoid
Usually killed
Development eventually causes
host death
Host resources
Parasitoid wasps
Major natural enemies

Parasite→Host usually survives 
Parasitoid→Host ultimately dies 

4. Parasitoid vs Predator
This is one of the most important concepts in insect pest management.
Feature
Predator
Parasitoid
Number of prey/hosts Usually many
Usually one host per developing individual
Feeding pattern
Consumes prey directly Immature develops on/in host
Host/prey
Often killed quickly
Host usually dies after parasitoid development
Immature stage
Usually independent
Associated with host
Adult
May be predatory
Often free-living
Example
Ladybird beetle
Trichogramma
Biological control
Yes
Yes

Memory trick
Predator = Eat many  
Parasitoid = Develop in one  

5. Why Parasitoids Are Important
Parasitoids naturally regulate pest populations.
They can attack:
- Pest eggs
- Larvae

- Nymphs
- Pupae
- Adults
Their importance is:
Parasitoid→Host mortality→Pest population↓→Crop damage↓ 
They are therefore valuable components of:
- Biological control
- IPM
- Sustainable pest management
- Conservation biological control
- Augmentative biological control

6. Major Parasitoid Groups
Many agricultural parasitoids belong to the order Hymenoptera, particularly parasitoid wasps.
Important families/groups include:
## 1. Trichogrammatidae
## 2. Braconidae
## 3. Ichneumonidae
## 4. Aphelinidae
## 5. Encyrtidae
## 6. Eulophidae
There are also parasitoid flies, especially some members of Tachinidae.

## 7. Trichogramma
Trichogramma are very small egg parasitoid wasps.
They attack the eggs of various insect pests, especially some moths and butterflies.
Basic process:

Trichogramma female→Pest egg→Oviposition→Parasitoid development→Host egg fails 
This is particularly valuable because the pest can be killed before the damaging larval stage
develops.

8. Importance of Egg Parasitoids
Consider a caterpillar pest:
Egg→Larva→Pupa→AdultEgg Larva Pupa Adult
The larval stage may cause major crop damage.
If a parasitoid attacks the egg:
Egg parasitism→No damaging larva→Potential crop damage↓ 
Thus egg parasitoids can interrupt the pest life cycle at an early stage.

9. Braconid Parasitoids
The family Braconidae contains many important parasitoids.
Different braconid species attack:
- Caterpillars
- Aphids
- Beetle larvae
- Other insect hosts
Many develop internally within their hosts.
General process:
Adult parasitoid→Host detection→Oviposition→Larval development→Host death→Adult parasitoid\
boxed  Adult\ parasitoid  Host\ detection  Oviposition  Larval\
development  Host\ death  Adult\ parasitoid  

10. Ichneumonid Parasitoids
Ichneumonidae is another major parasitoid family.
Species can attack:

- Caterpillars
- Larvae
- Pupae
- Other insect stages
They are important components of natural ecosystems and biological-control systems.

11. Aphid Parasitoids
Several parasitoid wasps attack aphids.
A characteristic result of successful parasitism is an:
Aphid mummy  
The parasitoid develops inside the aphid.
Eventually:
Parasitized aphid→Mummy→Adult parasitoid emergence 
Therefore, seeing aphid mummies during field scouting can indicate parasitoid activity.

12. Parasitoid Life Cycle
A generalized parasitoid life cycle can be represented as:
Adult→Host searching→Host recognition→Oviposition→Egg→Larva→Pupa→Adult 
The immature parasitoid develops using resources from the host.

13. Host Searching
Female parasitoids generally have to locate suitable hosts.
They may use:
- Visual cues
- Chemical cues
- Host odors

- Plant odors
- Herbivore-induced plant volatiles
- Host movement
- Host feeding damage
- Honeydew
- Other environmental signals
A simplified process is:
Host presence→Chemical/visual cues→Parasitoid searching 

14. Host Recognition
Finding a host does not necessarily mean the parasitoid will attack it.
The parasitoid may assess:
- Host species
- Host life stage
- Host size
- Host condition
- Host location
- Previous parasitization
- Suitability for offspring development
Therefore:
Host detection≠Host acceptance 

## 15. Oviposition
Oviposition means the laying or deposition of eggs.
A female parasitoid may use an ovipositor to place eggs:
- Inside the host
- On the host
- Near the host, depending on species and strategy
The exact mechanism varies among parasitoid species.

## 16. Endoparasitoids
An endoparasitoid develops inside the host.
Conceptually:
Host→Parasitoid egg→Internal larval development→Host death 
Many parasitoids are endoparasitoids.

## 17. Ectoparasitoids
An ectoparasitoid develops externally on the host.
Conceptually:
Host→Parasitoid attached externally→Feeding/development→Host death 
Thus the main distinction is:
Endo=inside    Ecto=outside   

18. Solitary Parasitoids
A solitary parasitoid generally develops with one successful parasitoid individual per host.
Simplified:
1 Host→1 Parasitoid 

19. Gregarious Parasitoids
A gregarious parasitoid can produce multiple parasitoid offspring from a single host.
For example:
1 Host→Multiple Parasitoid offspring 
This distinction is important when studying parasitoid biology and population dynamics.

20. Primary Parasitoids
A primary parasitoid directly attacks a herbivorous or otherwise target host.

Example relationship:
Primary parasitoid→Pest 
These are particularly important in biological pest control.

## 21. Hyperparasitoids
A hyperparasitoid is a parasitoid that develops on or within another parasitoid.
Relationship:
Pest→Primary parasitoid→Hyperparasitoid 
Hyperparasitoids can reduce the effectiveness of biological control by attacking beneficial parasitoids.

22. Parasitoid Host Specificity
Different parasitoids have different host ranges.
Some are relatively:
- Host-specific
- Narrow-spectrum
Others attack a broader range of related hosts.
Host specificity matters because it determines:
- Targeting of pests
- Non-target risks
- Effectiveness
- Suitability for biological control

23. Parasitoid Host Stages
Parasitoids may specialize in particular host stages.

Egg parasitoids
Attack:
Egg  
Example:

Trichogramma

Larval parasitoids
Attack:
Larva  

Pupal parasitoids
Attack:
Pupa  

Nymphal parasitoids
Attack:
Nymph  

Adult parasitoids
Some attack adult insects.
Therefore:
Parasitoid species→Specific host stage 

24. Parasitoid-Induced Host Death
The parasitoid develops using host resources.
This can interfere with:
- Growth
- Feeding
- Metabolism
- Development
- Reproduction
- Immune function
Eventually:
Host resources→Parasitoid growth→Host mortality 

25. Host Defense Against Parasitoids
Hosts are not passive.
Insects may defend themselves through:
- Immune responses
- Encapsulation
- Melanization
- Behavioral responses
- Movement
- Hiding
- Dropping from plants
Therefore parasitoid-host interactions are complex.
Some parasitoids have evolved mechanisms to overcome host defenses.

26. Parasitoid Adaptations
Parasitoids may have adaptations for:
- Finding hosts
- Recognizing hosts
- Penetrating host tissues
- Overcoming host immunity
- Manipulating host physiology
- Synchronizing development with host development
This makes parasitoid biology an important area of biological-control research.

27. Parasitoid and Host Plant Interaction
Parasitoids may indirectly use the plant to locate herbivorous hosts.
The sequence can be:
Herbivore feeding→Plant volatile release→Parasitoid attraction→Host location 

This is an important example of a tritrophic interaction.

28. Tritrophic Interaction
A simplified three-level relationship is:
Plant→Herbivore→Parasitoid 
The plant is attacked by the herbivore, and the herbivore is attacked by the parasitoid.
Therefore:
Plant–Pest–Natural Enemy interaction   
is central to agricultural ecology.

29. Parasitoid Population Dynamics
Parasitoid populations depend on:
- Host availability
- Host density
- Temperature
- Humidity
- Crop environment
- Alternative hosts
- Nectar resources
- Pesticide exposure
- Habitat
- Parasitoid reproductive capacity
A simplified relationship:
Host density↑→Potential parasitoid reproduction↑ 
followed by:
Parasitoid abundance↑→Host mortality↑ 

30. Parasitism Rate
One important field measure is the parasitism percentage.
Parasitism(%)=Number of parasitized hostsTotal hosts examined×100   Total\ hosts\ examined   100  

Example
Suppose:
- Total hosts examined = 500
- Parasitized hosts = 75
Then:
Parasitism=75500×100Parasitism=   500  100 Parasitism=15% 

31. Emergence Percentage
If parasitoid emergence is being studied:
Emergence(%)=Hosts producing parasitoid adultsParasitized hosts×100   Parasitized\ hosts   100  
This helps distinguish parasitization from successful parasitoid development.

32. Sex Ratio
For parasitoid populations:
Sex ratio=Number of femalesTotal parasitoids   Total\
parasitoids   
Female parasitoids are particularly important because they locate hosts and lay eggs.

33. Parasitoid Density
A basic field measure is:
Parasitoid density=Number of parasitoidsSampling unit   Sampling\ unit   
Examples:
- Parasitoids/plant

- Parasitoids/m²
- Parasitized hosts/100 plants
- Parasitized eggs/100 eggs

34. Biological Control Using Parasitoids
Parasitoids can be used through several approaches.

Natural biological control
Naturally occurring parasitoids suppress pests.

Conservation biological control
Farm practices protect existing parasitoids.

Augmentative biological control
Parasitoids are mass-produced and released.

Classical biological control
A suitable natural enemy may be introduced against an invasive pest, subject to regulatory and
ecological assessment.

35. Conservation Biological Control
The objective is:
Protect existing parasitoids 
Important considerations include:
- Avoid unnecessary broad-spectrum insecticides
- Maintain suitable habitat
- Provide nectar resources where appropriate
- Preserve field margins where beneficial
- Reduce unnecessary disturbance
- Avoid destroying parasitized hosts unnecessarily

36. Augmentative Biological Control
The basic process is:
Mass production→Quality control→Release→Host parasitism→Pest suppression 
Parasitoids can be released:
- Inoculatively
- Inundatively
depending on the biological-control program.

37. Trichogramma Release Concept
A simplified biological-control system is:
Mass rear Trichogramma→Release in crop→Locate pest eggs→Parasitize eggs→Reduce pest larvae - xed  Mass\ rear\ Trichogramma  Release\ in\ crop  Locate\ pest\ eggs
 Parasitize\ eggs  Reduce\ pest\ larvae  
The actual release rate, timing and suitability depend on the target crop, pest and locally validated
recommendations.

38. Parasitoids and Pest Life Cycle
Parasitoids can interrupt different points of the pest life cycle.
For example:
Egg→Larva→Pupa→AdultEgg Larva Pupa Adult
A parasitoid may attack:
Egg 
- r:
Larva 
- r:
Pupa 
Thus:

Parasitoid timing→Host stage→Control effectiveness 

39. Importance of Timing
Parasitoids are effective only when suitable host stages are available.
Therefore:
Correct parasitoid+Correct host+Correct host stage+Correct timing 
is important.
If the pest is at a stage unsuitable for a particular parasitoid, control may be limited.

40. Parasitoid Monitoring in the Field
Look for:

Parasitized eggs
May change:
- Color
- Shape
- Appearance

Mummies
Especially important for aphid parasitoids.

Emergence holes
A parasitoid may emerge from the host through a characteristic opening.

Adult parasitoids
Observe small wasps or flies near host populations.

41. Field Sampling
Methods can include:
- Visual inspection
- Collection of pest eggs

- Collection of larvae
- Collection of mummies
- Laboratory rearing
- Sweep net sampling
- Sticky traps
- Emergence monitoring
A particularly useful method is:
Collect suspected parasitized host→Keep under observation→Record parasitoid emergence 
This can help confirm parasitism.

42. Parasitoid Identification
Identification should consider:
1. Host species
2. Host stage
3. Parasitoid morphology
4. Emergence pattern
5. Body size
6. Wing characteristics
## 7. Antennae
## 8. Ovipositor
## 9. Behavior
10.Taxonomic keys
Advanced identification may use:
- Microscopy
- Molecular methods
- DNA barcoding

43. Parasitoids and Pesticides
Parasitoids can be sensitive to insecticides.
Pesticides may cause:
Direct mortality+Sublethal effects+Reduced reproduction+Reduced host searching 
Therefore:
Broad−spectrum pesticide use→Parasitoid populations↓ 
can occur under some circumstances.

44. Selective Pest Management
A major IPM goal is:
Pest suppression+Parasitoid conservation 
This can involve selecting pest-management tactics that minimize unnecessary effects on natural
enemies, always following the registered label and local recommendations.

45. Parasitoids and Insecticide Resistance
Management
Parasitoids provide a non-chemical mortality mechanism.
Therefore:
Chemical control+Biological control 
can contribute to a diversified pest-management program.
However, biological control should not be treated as a substitute for correct identification, monitoring
and threshold-based decisions.

46. Hyperparasitoids and Biological Control
Hyperparasitoids can attack primary parasitoids:
Pest→Primary parasitoid→Hyperparasitoid 

This can reduce the population of beneficial parasitoids.
Therefore, biological control involves complex food webs rather than a simple pest-versus-naturalenemy relationship.

47. Parasitoids vs Predators — Quick Revision
Predator
Parasitoid
Captures prey
Finds a host
Usually eats many prey Generally develops using one host
Prey is consumed
Host supplies resources for development
Often kills prey quickly Host typically dies after parasitoid development
Ladybird beetle
Trichogramma
Lacewing
Braconid wasp
Memory:
PREDATOR=EAT 
PARASITOID=DEVELOP 

48. Common Misconceptions
Misconception 1: Parasitoids are the same as parasites
Not exactly.
The defining distinction is that parasitoid development generally ends in host death.

Misconception 2: All wasps are pests
False.
Many wasps are valuable parasitoids or predators.

Misconception 3: Every small wasp is a parasitoid
False.
Correct identification is required.

Misconception 4: Parasitized insects immediately die
Not necessarily.
The parasitoid may require substantial time to develop.

Misconception 5: Seeing a parasitoid means the pest is under control
Not automatically.

The effectiveness depends on:
Host density+Parasitism+Timing+Environment+Parasitoid survivalHost\
density+Parasitism+Timing+Environment+Parasitoid\ survival

49. Practical Field Example — Aphids
Suppose a crop contains aphids.
During scouting you observe:
- Live aphids
- Aphid mummies
- Adult parasitoid wasps
Interpretation:
Aphid=Pest    Parasitoid=Natural enemy    Aphid mummy=Evidence of parasitism   
The farmer should therefore consider the level of natural parasitism along with aphid density, crop
stage, damage and applicable thresholds before making a management decision.

50. Practical Field Example — Caterpillar
Suppose a crop has caterpillars.
You find:
- Caterpillars
- Small parasitoid cocoons near dead hosts
- Adult parasitoid wasps
The interpretation may be:
Caterpillar→Host 
Parasitoid→Natural enemy  Parasitoid\ development
 Host\ mortality  
This means the parasitoid community may already be contributing to pest suppression.

51. Parasitoid Role in Sustainable Agriculture
Parasitoids provide an ecosystem service:

Natural parasitism→Pest mortality→Reduced pest pressure 
This can help:
- Reduce unnecessary pesticide use
- Support biodiversity
- Improve IPM
- Reduce selection pressure from repeated chemical use
- Increase ecological stability

52. Complete Parasitoid Classification
A useful study framework is:
PARASITOIDS  

By host location
Endoparasitoid=inside host   
Ectoparasitoid=outside host   

By number of offspring
Solitary=1 parasitoid/host 
Gregarious=multiple parasitoids/host 

By host relationship
Primary parasitoid→Pest 
Hyperparasitoid→Parasitoid 

By host stage
Egg+Larva+Nymph+Pupa+Adult 

53. Master Biological-Control Chain
The complete process can be remembered as:
Pest population→Host detection→Parasitoid attack→Oviposition→Parasitoid development→Host dea
th→Adult parasitoid→New host search 
This creates a natural pest-regulation cycle.

54. Final One-Read Summary
Parasitoids are insects whose immature stages develop on or inside a host and ultimately kill that
host. They are among the most important natural enemies used in agricultural biological control.
The central concept is:
Parasitoid→Host→Development→Host death     
Major parasitoid groups include:
- Trichogramma — important egg parasitoids
- Braconidae — many important parasitoids
- Ichneumonidae — many parasitoids of larvae/pupae
- Aphelinidae
- Encyrtidae
- Eulophidae
- Some Tachinidae flies
Important classifications:
Endoparasitoid=inside host   
Ectoparasitoid=outside host   
Solitary=one developing parasitoid   
Gregarious=multiple developing parasitoids 
  Primary parasitoid=attacks the pest   
Hyperparasitoid=attacks another parasitoid   
A useful field measure is:
Parasitism(%)=Parasitized hostsTotal hosts×100 
 Total\ hosts   100  
The most important IPM principle is:
## Identify → Monitor → Conserve Parasitoids → Manage Pests When Necessa
RY   
Ultimate memory line
Predators eat many prey; parasitoids develop on or inside a host and ultimately kill it.
Parasitoids are therefore powerful natural regulators of agricultural pest populations
and important biological-control agents.

## Module 15 — Insect & Pest
## Management
## 12. Economic Threshold Level (Etl)
## 1. Introduction
In agriculture, the presence of a pest does not automatically mean that insecticide should be
applied.
A few insects may be present without causing economically important damage. Farmers therefore need
a decision point that tells them when pest management should begin.
That decision point is called the Economic Threshold Level (ETL).
Pest population increases→Monitor→ETL reached→Management action      
The purpose of ETL is to provide early warning and a management decision point before economic
injury becomes unacceptable.

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
Economic Threshold Level (ETL) is the pest population or pest damage level at which control
measures should be started to prevent the pest from reaching the Economic Injury Level (EIL).

Exam-ready definition
Economic Threshold Level is the pest population density at which management action
should be initiated to prevent the pest population from reaching the Economic Injury
Level and causing economically unacceptable crop loss.

3. Why ETL Is Needed
Without a threshold:
See insect→Spray immediately  This can lead to:
- Unnecessary pesticide use

- Higher production costs
- Killing natural enemies
- Pest resurgence
- Selection for insecticide resistance
- Environmental contamination
- Unnecessary residues/exposure
With ETL:
Monitor→Measure pest→Compare with threshold→Decide      
Thus ETL is a fundamental concept in Integrated Pest Management (IPM).

4. ETL and EIL
ETL and EIL are closely related but not the same.

Economic Injury Level — EIL
The pest population at which the cost of crop damage becomes equal to the cost of controlling the
pest.

Economic Threshold Level — ETL
The level at which management action should begin to prevent the pest from reaching the EIL.
Therefore:
ETL<EIL 
Conceptually:
Pest population
↑
│

EIL

│

/

│

/

│

ETL ----

│

/

│

/

│ / → Time

The exact relationship is dynamic rather than a universal fixed distance.

5. EIL — Economic Injury Level
The standard conceptual formula is:
EIL=CV×I×D×K  V I D K   
where:
- CC = Cost of control per unit area
- VV = Market value of the crop per unit yield
- II = Injury caused per pest
- DD = Yield loss per unit injury
- KK = Proportion of pest population controlled by the management tactic
The units of the variables must be compatible.

6. Relationship Between ETL and EIL
The sequence is:
Pest density↑→ETL→Management→EIL prevented 
If action is delayed:
Pest density↑→EIL→Economic loss 
So:
ETL is an action threshold; EIL is an economic damage threshold.

7. ETL Is Not a Universal Number
There is no single ETL for all insects or all crops.
ETL depends on:
- Pest species
- Crop species
- Crop variety
- Crop growth stage

- Pest life stage
- Pest density
- Type of damage
- Crop value
- Control cost
- Expected yield
- Environmental conditions
- Natural enemies
- Management effectiveness
Therefore:
ETL=f(Crop+Pest+Stage+Environment+Economics+Management)
 

8. ETL Is Crop-Specific
The same pest population can have different economic consequences in different crops.
For example:
Pest density=X =X
may cause little economic damage in one crop but substantial damage in another.
Therefore:
Same pest density≠same economic impact everywhere   

9. ETL Is Growth-Stage Specific
Crop susceptibility changes during development.
For example:
- Seedling stage
- Vegetative stage
- Flowering
- Fruiting
- Grain filling

may have different sensitivity to pest damage.
Therefore:
ETLseedling≠ETLflowering  ETL  flowering   
in many practical pest-management situations.

10. ETL Depends on Pest Life Stage
Different pest stages can cause different amounts of damage.
For example:
- Egg
- Larva
- Nymph
- Adult
may have very different feeding rates.
Therefore monitoring must record:
Pest species+Life stage+Population 
rather than simply counting "insects."

11. ETL and Pest Density
Pest density can be expressed in different ways.
Examples:
Insects/plant   Larvae/m2  2 
Eggs/leaf   Percent damaged plants  
Percent leaf damage   Insects/100 sweeps  
The appropriate measurement depends on the pest and crop.

12. ETL and Damage
ETL does not always have to be expressed as the number of insects.
It can sometimes be based on:

- Leaf damage
- Defoliation
- Infested shoots
- Damaged fruits
- Damaged pods
- Damaged tillers
- Wilted plants
- Percentage infestation
Thus:
ETL=Pest density OR measurable damage level   
depending on the validated management system.

13. ETL and Monitoring
ETL only works when pest populations are monitored properly.
Basic system:
Sampling→Pest counting→Threshold comparison→Decision 
Monitoring methods may include:
- Visual inspection
- Sweep nets
- Beat sampling
- Sticky traps
- Pheromone traps
- Light traps
- Plant counts
- Soil sampling
- Damage assessment

14. Sampling Before ETL
A farmer cannot make a reliable threshold decision without representative sampling.
Suppose a field contains 10,000 plants.
Inspecting only one plant may produce a misleading estimate.
Therefore:
Representative sampling→Better threshold decision 
Sampling should consider:
- Field size
- Spatial distribution
- Pest behavior
- Crop stage
- Sampling method
- Number of sampling points

15. ETL and Natural Enemies
This is extremely important in IPM.
Suppose a crop has:
- High pest population
- High predator population
- High parasitoid activity
The natural enemies may already be suppressing the pest.
Therefore:
Pest density+Natural enemy activity 
should be considered before management decisions.
The practical threshold for action can depend on the broader pest-management situation rather than pest
count alone.

16. ETL and Predators
For example:
Aphids↑  but:
Ladybird beetles↑  and:
Aphid parasitoids↑  Natural control may be substantial.
Therefore:
Pest count alone is not always sufficient for an IPM decision   

17. ETL and Parasitoids
Parasitoids can also influence pest population growth.
For example:
Aphids→Parasitism→Mummies    If parasitism is increasing, future pest growth may be reduced.
Therefore, monitoring can include:
Pest density+Parasitism rate 

18. ETL and Weather
Weather affects both pests and crops.
Important factors include:
- Temperature
- Humidity
- Rainfall
- Wind
- Solar radiation

For example:
Temperature→Pest development→Population growthTemperature  Pest\ development
 Population\ growth
and:
Weather→Crop susceptibility→DamageWeather  Crop\ susceptibility  Damage
Therefore ETLs may be interpreted differently under different environmental conditions.

19. ETL and Economics
ETL is fundamentally an economic decision concept.
The decision considers:
Cost of controlvsExpected crop loss 
If expected loss is very small:
Control may not be economically justified 
If expected loss is increasing:
Management becomes more justified 

20. Factors That Lower EIL
From:
EIL=CV×I×D×KEIL=  V I D K 
EIL tends to decrease when:

Control cost CC decreases
C↓→EIL↓C  EIL Crop value VV increases
V↑→EIL↓V  EIL Pest causes greater injury II
I↑→EIL↓I  EIL Injury causes greater yield loss DD
D↑→EIL↓D  EIL Control effectiveness KK increases
K↑→EIL↓K  EIL This means economically valuable crops or highly damaging pests may justify action at lower pest
densities.

21. Factors That Increase EIL
Conversely:
C↑→EIL↑C  EIL V↓→EIL↑V  EIL I↓→EIL↑I  EIL D↓→EIL↑D  EIL K↓→EIL↑K  EIL So a costly and poorly effective control method may not be economically justified at low pest density.

22. ETL and Crop Value
Suppose two crops have the same pest.

Crop A
Low market value.

Crop B
High market value.
Damage to Crop B may have a greater economic consequence.
Therefore:
Crop value↑→Economic injury threshold generally↓ 
This is a conceptual relationship; actual thresholds require crop- and pest-specific data.

23. ETL and Control Cost
If a control method becomes cheaper:
Control cost↓Control\ cost then the economics of controlling the pest change.
Similarly, if control becomes very expensive:

Control cost↑Control\ cost a higher pest density may be required before control becomes economically justified.

24. ETL and Control Effectiveness
Suppose a management method controls 90% of a pest population.
K=0.90K=0.90
If another method controls only 50%:
K=0.50K=0.50
The economic consequences can differ.
Therefore:
Control effectiveness matters 

25. ETL and Preventive Action
ETL is generally used as a decision threshold, not a target population.
The goal is not:
"Allow the pest to reach ETL."
The goal is:
Detect trend→Act appropriately→Prevent EIL 

26. ETL in IPM
ETL fits into the complete IPM system:
## Identify→Monitor→Assess→Compare With Etl→Decide→Manage→Reassess
 
This is much better than calendar-based spraying.

27. ETL vs Calendar Spraying
Calendar spraying
Date arrives→Spray  regardless of actual pest population.

Threshold-based management
Monitor→Pest reaches decision threshold→Consider action   The second approach is more closely aligned with IPM principles.

28. ETL vs Preventive Pesticide Use
ETL does not mean that all pest management must wait until severe damage occurs.
Preventive measures may include:
- Resistant varieties
- Crop rotation
- Sanitation
- Biological control
- Habitat management
- Seed treatment where appropriate
- Cultural practices
ETL primarily helps determine when intervention is warranted based on pest risk.

29. ETL Example
Suppose a validated crop-specific threshold states that management should be considered when:
5 aphids/plant 
are observed.
During scouting:

Observation 1
2 aphids/plant2\ aphids/plant
Below threshold.

Observation 2
4 aphids/plant4\ aphids/plant
Still below threshold.

Observation 3
6 aphids/plant6\ aphids/plant
Threshold exceeded.
The decision would then be to evaluate appropriate management, taking into account crop stage,
damage, natural enemies, weather and the locally recommended control method.

30. ETL Is Not "Spray Immediately"
This is a very important distinction.
Reaching ETL means:
Management decision should be considered   
It does not necessarily mean:
Immediately spray insecticide   
Possible management options include:
- Biological control
- Mechanical control
- Cultural control
- Physical control
- Selective chemical control
- Combination of methods

31. ETL and IPM Decision Tree
Pest detected
↓
Identify pest
↓
Monitor
↓
Measure pest/damage
↓

Check crop growth stage
↓
Check natural enemies
↓
Compare with threshold

↙

↘

Below ETL

≥ ETL

↓

↓

Continue

Evaluate

monitoring

management
↓
Choose suitable
IPM method
↓
Re-monitor

32. ETL and Natural Enemy Conservation
Suppose:
Pest population=moderatePest\ population=moderate
but:
Natural enemy population=highNatural\ enemy\ population=high
Immediate insecticide use may unnecessarily destroy natural enemies.
Therefore:
ETL+Natural enemy assessment 
is more useful than pest counting alone.

33. ETL and Pest Resurgence
Unnecessary pesticide application can kill natural enemies.
Then:
Natural enemies↓→Pest suppression↓→Pest resurgence risk↑ 

Threshold-based IPM can help reduce unnecessary applications.

34. ETL and Insecticide Resistance
Repeated unnecessary pesticide use creates selection pressure.
Repeated pesticide use→Selection pressure→Resistance risk 
Threshold-based decision-making can reduce unnecessary applications and support resistance
management.
However, resistance management also requires:
- MOA diversification
- Correct rates
- Correct timing
- Integrated tactics
- Prevention of resistant survivors reproducing

35. ETL and Damage Assessment
Pest density alone may not always predict economic loss.
Therefore, depending on the pest, monitoring may include:
Pest density+Damage intensity+Crop stage 
For example:
- Number of insects
- Percentage damaged leaves
- Percentage infested fruits
- Number of damaged shoots
- Defoliation percentage

36. ETL vs Action Threshold
The terms are sometimes used differently in pest-management literature.
In practical IPM usage:

- ETL = pest level at which management action should begin.
- Action threshold = broader term for a decision point at which action is initiated.
They are closely related, but exact terminology can vary among extension programs and pestmanagement systems.

37. ETL vs EIL — Important Table
Feature
ETL
EIL
Full form
Economic Threshold Level
Economic Injury Level
Purpose
Decide when to begin management Define economically damaging pest level
Timing
Before EIL
At economic injury
Function
Action/decision point
Economic damage point
Relationship Usually lower
Usually higher
IPM role
Very important
Basis for threshold economics
Memory:
ETL=ACT    EIL=ECONOMIC DAMAGE   
38. Critical Difference: ETL vs Economic Injury
Consider:
Low pest density
↓
No significant economic loss
↓
ETL ← management decision point
↓
Pest continues increasing
↓
EIL ← economic injury begins
↓
Economic loss

Thus:
ETL comes before EIL   

39. ETL and Pest Forecasting
ETL becomes even more useful when combined with forecasting.
For example:
Weather→Pest development→Population prediction→Threshold assessmentWeather  Pest\
development  Population\ prediction  Threshold\ assessment
Degree-day models can sometimes help predict pest development:
GDD=∑max⁡(0,Tmean−Tb) -T b)  
This can help predict when a pest stage is likely to appear, although pest-specific models and validated
parameters are required.

40. ETL and Sampling Error
A threshold decision can be wrong if the pest is not sampled properly.
Potential problems:
- Too few samples
- Poorly distributed samples
- Sampling only field edges
- Ignoring hotspots
- Wrong sampling method
- Wrong pest stage
- Misidentification
Therefore:
Good threshold decision=Correct identification+Representative sampling+Correct threshold 

41. ETL and Spatial Distribution
Pests may occur in:
- Uniform distribution
- Random distribution
- Aggregated/clustered distribution

If pests are clustered, average field density may hide hotspots.
Therefore scouting should consider spatial distribution.

42. ETL and Field Scouting
A good scouting record should include:
- Date
- Field
- Crop
- Variety
- Crop stage
- Pest species
- Life stage
- Pest density
- Damage level
- Natural enemies
- Weather
- Previous management
- Threshold
- Decision
This makes ETL-based management more reliable.

43. ETL Example With Natural Enemies
Suppose:
Aphids=8/plantAphids=8/plant
A simplistic system might say:
"Above threshold, spray."
An IPM system asks:
- Are aphids increasing?
- What crop stage is it?

- How much damage exists?
- Are ladybirds present?
- Are aphid parasitoids present?
- What is the weather forecast?
- What management options are registered?
- What are the economic consequences?
Therefore:
ETL→Decision framework, not automatic spray 

44. Limitations of ETL
ETL is extremely useful, but it has limitations.

1. Thresholds may be location-specific
A threshold developed elsewhere may not automatically apply.

2. Weather changes pest growth
Rapid pest growth may require earlier action.

3. Pest distribution may be uneven
Field averages can hide hotspots.

4. Natural enemies change the situation
Biological control may reduce future pest growth.

5. Crop value changes
Market prices affect economic calculations.

6. Control costs change
Input prices affect economics.

7. Crop stage matters
Young plants and reproductive stages may have different susceptibility.
Therefore:
ETL is a decision tool, not an immutable universal number   

45. Practical ETL Workflow
The complete practical process is:
1. Identify pest  2. Determine crop stage 
3. Monitor pest population  4. Measure damage  5. Monitor natural enemies 
6. Compare with validated threshold 
7. Evaluate economics  8. Select suitable IPM tactic  9. Re−monitor 

46. ETL in the Complete IPM Framework
Prevention→Monitoring→ETL→Decision→Management→Evaluation 
      
This helps transform pest management from reaction-based spraying into evidence-based decisionmaking.

47. Key Formulas
Economic Injury Level
EIL=CV×I×D×K  V I D K   

Parasitism
Parasitism(%)=Parasitized hostsTotal hosts×100 
 Total\ hosts   100  

Predator:pest ratio
Predator:Pest=Number of predatorsNumber of pests  Number\ of\ pests   

Pest density
Pest density=Number of pestsSampling unit  Sampling\
unit   
These measurements can support threshold-based decisions.

48. Important Exam Questions
Q1. What is ETL?
Answer: ETL is the pest population or damage level at which management action should be initiated to
prevent the pest from reaching the Economic Injury Level.

Q2. What is the relationship between ETL and EIL?
ETL<EIL 
ETL is the action point; EIL is the economic injury point.

Q3. Is ETL the same for every crop?
No. It varies with crop, pest, crop stage, environment, economics and management conditions.

Q4. Does reaching ETL automatically mean insecticide spraying?
No. It means management should be evaluated. IPM may use biological, cultural, mechanical, physical
- r chemical methods.

Q5. Why are natural enemies important when using ETL?
Because predators and parasitoids can suppress pest populations and influence whether intervention is
actually necessary.

49. Final One-Read Summary
Economic Threshold Level (ETL) is the decision point at which pest management should begin to
prevent the pest from reaching the Economic Injury Level (EIL).
The central relationship is:
ETL<EIL <   
Think of it as:
ETL = ACT    EIL = ECONOMIC INJURY   
ETL depends on:
Crop+Pest+Crop stage+Pest density+Damage+Environment+Natural enemies+Economics 
The basic IPM decision is:

## Identify→Monitor→Measure→Compare With Etl→Decide→Manage→Re−Mo
NITOR 
And the most important practical point is:
Reaching ETL does not automatically mean “spray.” It means the pest situation has
reached a level where appropriate management should be evaluated to prevent
economically significant injury.

Ultimate memory line
ETL = the “when should I act?” point; EIL = the “economic damage has begun” point.
   

## Module 15 — Insect & Pest
## Management
## 13. Economic Injury Level (Eil)
## 1. Introduction
The Economic Injury Level (EIL) is one of the most important concepts in Integrated Pest
Management (IPM).
A pest does not necessarily cause economic loss merely because it is present in a field. Economic injury
- ccurs when pest damage becomes large enough that the value of the crop loss equals the cost of
controlling the pest.
The central concept is:
Pest density↑→Crop injury↑→Economic loss↑       
The EIL identifies the pest density at which this economic relationship becomes significant.

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
Economic Injury Level (EIL) is the lowest pest population density that causes economic damage
equal to the cost of controlling the pest.

Exam-ready definition
Economic Injury Level is the lowest population density of a pest that will cause
economic damage equal to the cost of preventing or controlling that damage.
Mathematically:
Value of crop loss=Cost of control =   
at the EIL.

3. Why EIL Is Important
EIL answers an important question:
At what pest density does the economic loss become equal to the cost of controlling the
pest?
It helps determine whether pest control is economically justified.
Without this concept:
Pest detected→Automatic spraying  With economic decision-making:
Pest detected→Monitor→Estimate damage→Assess economics→Manage if justified       

4. Core Concept
Imagine pest density increasing:
Economic impact
↑
│

/

│

/

│

/

│

/

│

● EIL

│

/

│

/

│ / → Pest density

Before EIL:

Economic loss<Cost of control < 
At EIL:
Economic loss=Cost of control =   
Beyond EIL:
Economic loss>Cost of control > 

5. EIL Formula
A commonly used economic formula is:
EIL=CV×I×D×K  V I D K   
where:
Symbol
Meaning
CC
Cost of control per unit area
VV
Market value of crop per unit yield
II
Injury caused per pest
DD
Damage/yield loss per unit injury
KK
Proportion of pest population controlled by the management method
The variables must be expressed in compatible units.

6. Understanding the Formula
The formula can initially look complicated, but its logic is simple.
EIL=Cost of controlEconomic loss caused by one unit of pest pressure        
The denominator represents the economic damage associated with pest pressure.
Therefore:

High control cost
C↑⇒EIL↑C  EIL More pests may be required before control becomes economically justified.

High crop value
V↑⇒EIL↓V  EIL Economic damage becomes important at a lower pest density.

High pest injury
I↑⇒EIL↓I  EIL High yield loss per injury
D↑⇒EIL↓D  EIL Highly effective control
K↑⇒EIL↓K  EIL 7. Meaning of Each Component
7.1 Cost of Control — CC
This is the cost associated with controlling the pest.
It may include:
- Pesticide
- Labor
- Machinery
- Application
- Fuel
- Biological-control costs
- Other management expenses
If control becomes more expensive:
C↑C then:
EIL↑EIL 8. Crop Value — VV
VV represents the economic value of the crop.
For example, crop value may be expressed as:
₹/kg₹/ 
- r another compatible unit.
If crop value increases:

V↑V then even relatively small yield losses may become economically important.
Therefore:
Crop value↑→EIL↓ 

9. Injury per Pest — II
Not every pest causes the same amount of injury.
For example:
- One sucking insect may remove a certain amount of plant sap.
- One caterpillar may consume a certain amount of leaf tissue.
- One borer may damage a stem.
- One fruit pest may destroy a fruit.
Thus:
I=injury caused by one pest or unit of pest pressureI= 
If each pest causes more injury:
I↑I then:
EIL↓EIL 10. Damage per Unit Injury — DD
Injury and damage are not exactly the same.

Injury
The physiological or physical effect caused by the pest.
Examples:
- Leaf area removed
- Sap extracted
- Root tissue damaged
- Stem tissue bored

Damage
The resulting economic/yield consequence.
For example:
Leaf feeding→Reduced photosynthesis→Lower yield    So:
Injury→Damage→Economic loss 

11. Control Effectiveness — KK
KK represents the proportion of pest pressure that the control method can effectively suppress.
For example:
K=0.80K=0.80
means approximately 80% effective under the assumptions of the model.
Higher effectiveness means:
K↑K and therefore:
EIL↓EIL 12. ETL vs EIL
This is one of the most important exam distinctions.

Economic Injury Level
EIL=Economic damage point   

Economic Threshold Level
ETL=Action point before EIL   
Therefore:
ETL<EIL 
Conceptually:
Pest density

│

EIL

│

●

│

/

│

ETL

/

│

●

/

│

/

/

│ / / → Time

ETL gives time to act before the pest population reaches EIL.
