14. EIL Is Not a Fixed Universal Number
There is no single EIL applicable to all agricultural situations.
It varies with:
EIL=f(Crop+Pest+Environment+Economics+Management)
 
Important factors include:
- Pest species
- Crop species
- Crop variety
- Crop growth stage

- Crop price
- Expected yield
- Pest density
- Pest feeding rate
- Pest distribution
- Control cost
- Control effectiveness
- Weather
- Natural enemies

15. EIL Depends on Crop Value
Suppose two crops are affected by the same pest.

Crop A
Low-value crop.

Crop B
High-value crop.
The same amount of pest damage may produce very different economic losses.
Therefore:
V↑⇒EIL↓ 
High-value crops can have lower economic injury levels under otherwise comparable conditions.

16. EIL Depends on Control Cost
Suppose control is cheap:
C↓C Then economically justified intervention may occur at a lower pest density.
Thus:
C↓⇒EIL↓ 
If control becomes expensive:
C↑⇒EIL↑C  EIL 17. EIL Depends on Pest Damage Potential
Consider two pests:

Pest A
Causes little damage per individual.

Pest B
Causes severe damage per individual.
For Pest B:
I×DI D
is larger.
Therefore:
Severe pest damage→Lower EIL 

18. EIL Depends on Control Effectiveness
Suppose a management method has high effectiveness:
K↑K Then the economics of controlling the pest change, generally resulting in:
EIL↓ 
A poorly effective control method may not justify intervention at low pest densities.

19. EIL and Natural Enemies
Natural enemies can reduce pest growth.
Important natural enemies include:

Predators
- Ladybird beetles
- Lacewing larvae
- Assassin bugs
- Ground beetles

Parasitoids
- Trichogramma
- Braconids
- Ichneumonids
- Aphid parasitoids
Therefore:
Natural enemies→Pest mortality→Pest population growth↓ 
Natural biological control can influence whether the pest population reaches an economically damaging
level.

20. EIL and IPM
EIL forms part of the economic foundation of IPM.
The complete process is:
Identify→Monitor→Assess→Compare risk→Use ETL/EIL→Manage→Evaluate 
The objective is not to eliminate every pest.
The objective is to keep pest populations at economically acceptable levels while minimizing
unnecessary intervention.

21. EIL and Pest Population
A simplified relationship:
Pest density↑→Injury↑→Damage↑→Economic loss↑ 
When the economic loss reaches the cost of control:
EIL 

22. EIL and Yield Loss
The chain is:

Pest→Injury→Physiological disruption→Yield loss→Economic loss 
Examples:

Caterpillar
Leaf feeding→Leaf area loss→Photosynthesis↓→Yield↓Leaf\ feeding  Leaf\ area\ loss
 Photosynthesis  Yield Stem borer
Stem boring→Transport disruption→Plant damage→Yield↓Stem\ boring  Transport\
disruption  Plant\ damage  Yield Sucking pest
Sap removal→Physiological stress→Growth↓→Yield↓Sap\ removal  Physiological\ stress
 Growth  Yield 23. EIL and Crop Growth Stage
Crop susceptibility changes during development.
For example:
Seedling≠Vegetative≠Flowering≠Grain fillingSeedling Vegetative Flowering Grain\ filling
The same pest density may have different consequences at different stages.
Thus:
EIL=f(Pest density, Crop stage) 
in many practical situations.

24. EIL and Pest Life Stage
A pest's feeding intensity can vary by life stage.
For example:
Egg→Larva→Pupa→AdultEgg Larva Pupa Adult
The egg and pupa may cause little or no direct feeding damage, while larvae may cause substantial
damage.
Therefore the economically relevant measurement may be:
Specific damaging life stage 

rather than total insect numbers.

25. EIL and Pest Distribution
Pests may occur in:
- Random patterns
- Uniform patterns
- Aggregated/clustered patterns
A field with a low average pest density may still contain highly infested hotspots.
Therefore:
Field average≠always field reality 
Representative sampling is essential.

26. EIL and Weather
Weather can change:
- Pest reproduction
- Development rate
- Survival
- Feeding rate
- Crop growth
- Natural enemy activity
For example:
Temperature→Pest development→Population growthTemperature  Pest\ development
 Population\ growth
Therefore economic risk can change rapidly.

27. EIL and Pest Forecasting
Forecasting can help estimate whether pest density is likely to increase toward an economically
important level.
A simplified system:

Weather→Pest development→Population forecast→Economic risk 
Degree-day models may sometimes be used:
GDD=∑max⁡(0,Tmean−Tb) -T b)  
where:
- TmeanT  mean  = mean temperature
- TbT b = pest-specific developmental base temperature
The parameters must be specific to the pest and validated model.

28. EIL and Management Decisions
When pest density is below EIL:
Economic damage<Control cost 
under the assumptions of the model.
When pest density reaches EIL:
Economic damage=Control cost 
Above EIL:
Economic damage>Control cost 
This is the fundamental economic logic.

29. Example Calculation
Suppose the following hypothetical values are given:
C=₹1000/haC=₹1000/ha V=₹20/kgV=₹20/kg I=0.01I=0.01 D=2D=2 K=0.80K=0.80
Then:
EIL=100020×0.01×2×0.80EIL=   20 0.01 2 0.80 
First calculate the denominator:
20×0.01=0.220 0.01=0.2 0.2×2=0.40.2 2=0.4 0.4×0.80=0.320.4 0.80=0.32
Therefore:
EIL=10000.32EIL=  0.32  EIL=3125 
The exact units depend on how II and DD were defined.

Important: This is only a mathematical illustration; actual agricultural EIL calculations require
validated, compatible crop-pest parameters.

30. What Happens Below EIL?
Suppose:
Pest density<EILPest\ density<EIL
Then the expected economic loss is below the cost-equivalent injury level under the model.
Management may therefore be unnecessary, depending on the broader IPM situation.
Possible actions:
- Continue monitoring
- Conserve natural enemies
- Use cultural practices
- Improve crop health
- Prepare for future intervention

31. What Happens at EIL?
At EIL:
Economic loss=Cost of control 
This is the economic break-even point.
But practical management generally aims to act before this point, which is why ETL is used.

32. What Happens Above EIL?
When:
Pest density>EILPest\ density>EIL
the economic loss can exceed the cost of control.
This means intervention can become economically justified, subject to:
- Available control options
- Crop stage
- Weather

- Natural enemies
- Legal/label restrictions
- Harvest interval
- Resistance considerations

33. EIL Does Not Mean "Kill All Pests"
This is extremely important.
Agricultural ecosystems normally contain insects and other organisms.
The objective of IPM is:
Economic pest management 
not:
Zero insects 
Some pest presence may be economically acceptable.

34. EIL and Biological Control
Suppose:
Pest population=moderatePest\ population=moderate
and:
Parasitoid activity=highParasitoid\ activity=high
The parasitoids may reduce future pest growth.
Similarly:
Predator population↑→Pest mortality↑Predator\ population  Pest\
mortality Therefore biological control can contribute to keeping pest populations below economically damaging
levels.

35. EIL and Pesticide Resistance
Repeated pesticide use can select resistant individuals:

Pesticide→Susceptible pests die→Less susceptible pests survive→Reproduction→Resistance frequenc
y↑ 
Economic thresholds can help reduce unnecessary applications.
However:
EIL alone≠complete resistance management 
Resistance management also requires proper:
- Mode-of-action rotation
- Application timing
- Dose
- Integrated tactics
- Monitoring

36. EIL and Pest Resurgence
Unnecessary pesticide application can reduce natural enemies.
Therefore:
Broad−spectrum pesticide→Natural enemies↓→Natural control↓→Pest resurgence risk↑ 
Economic decision-making helps avoid unnecessary treatments.

37. EIL and ETL — Visual Memory
Think of the relationship like this:
## Economic Injury Level

EIL
/
/

/
●

│

38. Important Factors Affecting EIL
Factor
If it increases General effect on EIL
Control cost CC

These are relationships from the standard economic model; actual field thresholds require crop- and

39. EIL and Decision-Making
A complete economic decision should consider:
Pest density+Crop stage+Expected damage+Crop value+Control cost+Control effectiveness+Natural e
nemies+Weather 
Therefore:
EIL is an economic decision framework, not merely an insect-count number.   

40. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1: EIL and ETL are the same

❌ Incorrect.

ETL<EIL 
generally.

Mistake 2: EIL means spray immediately

❌ Not necessarily.

EIL is the economic injury reference point; practical management is generally initiated earlier using an
action threshold such as ETL.

Mistake 3: EIL is fixed for every crop

❌ Incorrect.

It depends on crop, pest, economics, environment and management.

Mistake 4: EIL means zero pest should be allowed

❌ Incorrect.

IPM aims for economically acceptable pest populations, not necessarily complete eradication.

Mistake 5: Pest number alone determines EIL

❌ Incorrect.

The economic effect of pest density depends on injury, crop value, yield response, control cost and
control effectiveness.

41. Exam-Ready Short Answer
Economic Injury Level (EIL) is the lowest pest population density at which the
economic value of crop loss caused by the pest is equal to the cost of controlling the
pest. It is expressed conceptually as EIL=C/(V×I×D×K)EIL=C/(V I D K). EIL varies with pest species, crop, crop stage, crop value, injury potential,
control cost, control effectiveness and environmental conditions. The Economic
Threshold Level (ETL) is generally set below EIL so that management can begin
before economic injury occurs.

42. Final One-Read Summary
The entire concept can be remembered as:
Pest density→Injury→Damage→Economic loss      
At the Economic Injury Level:
Crop-loss value=Cost of control =   
The standard model is:
EIL=CV×I×D×K  V I D K   
where:
- CC = cost of control
- VV = crop value
- II = injury per pest
- DD = damage/yield loss per unit injury
- KK = proportion controlled
And the most important relationship is:
ETL < EIL   
ETL tells you when to start considering management; EIL tells you the economic point where
pest injury equals the cost of control.

Ultimate memory line
EIL is the economic break-even pest level: below it, the modeled crop loss is less than
the cost of control; at it, they are equal; beyond it, crop loss can exceed the cost of
control.

## Module 15 — Insect & Pest
## Management
## 14. Control Methods — Cultural
## Control
## 1. Introduction
Cultural control is one of the oldest and most important methods of insect and pest management.
Instead of directly killing pests with pesticides, it involves modifying crop-growing practices so that
the crop becomes less favorable for pest establishment, multiplication, survival, or damage.
The central principle is:
Modify the cropping environment→Reduce pest survival/reproduction→Reduce pest population→Red
uce crop damage      
Cultural control is therefore a preventive and ecological pest-management approach and is an
important component of IPM.

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
Cultural control is the management of pests by modifying agricultural practices and the crop
environment to prevent or reduce pest populations and damage.

Exam-ready definition
Cultural control is a pest-management method in which crop production practices are
deliberately modified to make the environment unfavorable for pest establishment,
survival, reproduction, or spread while improving the crop's ability to compete with
- r tolerate pests.

3. Basic Principle
Cultural control does not necessarily attack the pest directly.
Instead:

Change farm practice→Change pest environment→Pest survival/reproduction↓ 
For example:
Crop rotation→Host availability interrupted→Pest buildup↓     4. Objectives of Cultural Control
The major objectives are to:
1. Prevent pest establishment
2. Reduce pest multiplication
3. Reduce pest survival
4. Break pest life cycles
5. Reduce pest movement
6. Reduce availability of host plants
7. Make the crop more competitive
8. Reduce pest damage
9. Conserve natural enemies
10.Reduce dependence on pesticides
11.Reduce pest carryover between seasons
12.Delay resistance development by reducing pest pressure

5. Cultural Control in IPM
Cultural control fits into the broader IPM system:
Prevention→Monitoring→Threshold assessment→Cultural management→Other suitable tactics   Prevention  Monitoring  Threshold\ assessment  Cultural\
management  Other\ suitable\ tactics  
It is particularly valuable because it can often be implemented before serious pest damage occurs.

6. Major Cultural Control Methods
Important cultural practices include:

1. Crop rotation
2. Proper sowing/planting time
3. Timely harvesting
4. Field sanitation
5. Destruction of crop residues when appropriate
6. Removal of volunteer plants
7. Removal of alternate hosts
8. Proper plant spacing
9. Optimum plant population
10.Balanced fertilization
11.Proper irrigation
12.Drainage and water management
13.Proper land preparation
14.Deep/planned tillage where appropriate
15.Clean cultivation
16.Trap crops
17.Border crops
18.Intercropping
19.Cover crops
20.Synchronization of crop planting
21.Use of resistant/tolerant varieties
22.Weed management
23.Adjusting crop architecture/density
24.Proper nursery management
25.Healthy planting material
26.Proper storage sanitation
27.Destruction of infested plant parts
28.Avoidance of continuous monocropping

7. Crop Rotation
Crop rotation means growing different crops sequentially on the same land according to a planned
sequence.
Example:
Crop A→Crop B→Crop C 
Instead of:
Crop A→Crop A→Crop A 

Pest-management principle
If a pest depends strongly on one crop:
Host crop removed→Food availability↓→Pest survival/reproduction↓ 
    8. How Crop Rotation Works
Crop rotation can:
- Break pest life cycles
- Interrupt host availability
- Reduce pest carryover
- Reduce soil-borne pest buildup
- Reduce disease buildup
- Improve soil health
- Increase crop diversity
Conceptually:
Continuous host→Pest buildup 
while:
Host rotation→Life cycle disruption→Pest pressure↓ 

9. Limitations of Crop Rotation
Rotation is not equally effective against every pest.

It is particularly useful when:
- The pest has a narrow host range
- The pest has limited mobility
- The pest has a soil-associated stage
- The pest cannot survive long without the host
It may be less effective when pests:
- Have many alternative hosts
- Migrate long distances
- Survive for several years
- Have broad host ranges

10. Timely Sowing
Changing planting time can help avoid periods of high pest pressure.
The principle is:
Crop phenology≠Peak pest activity 
If the crop reaches a vulnerable stage when pest populations are low:
Pest exposure during susceptible stage↓ 

11. Early or Delayed Planting
Depending on the pest, changing sowing time can:
- Avoid peak pest populations
- Avoid peak vector activity
- Escape favorable pest weather
- Allow crop establishment before pest buildup
However:
Optimal sowing time must consider both crop and pest 
Changing sowing time solely for pest control can create other agronomic problems.

12. Synchronization of Planting
In some cropping systems, coordinated planting across an area can reduce continuous availability of
suitable crop stages.
Conceptually:
Synchronized planting→Large crop cohort→Reduced continuous host availability 
This can be useful against pests whose populations depend strongly on crop stage.

13. Timely Harvesting
Leaving mature crops in the field longer than necessary can provide pests with additional food and
breeding opportunities.
Therefore:
Timely harvest→Host availability↓→Pest reproduction↓ 
It can also reduce field-to-storage pest movement in some systems.

14. Field Sanitation
Field sanitation means removing or managing materials that allow pests to survive, reproduce or
spread.
Examples:
- Removing heavily infested plant material
- Removing fallen fruits
- Removing diseased/infected residues where appropriate
- Removing volunteer plants
- Cleaning tools and equipment
- Managing crop residues
- Removing pest-infested storage material
Basic principle:
Remove pest habitat→Reduce pest survival 

15. Destruction of Infested Plant Parts
Some pests concentrate in:
- Shoots
- Leaves
- Fruits
- Pods
- Stems
- Roots
Removing heavily infested material can reduce pest populations.
For example:
Infested fruit removed→Larval development interrupted→Future adults↓ 
   Removed material should be handled safely so that the pest does not complete its life cycle.

16. Fallen Fruit Management
Some fruit pests use fallen fruit as breeding material.
Therefore:
Fallen fruit removal→Larval habitat↓→Adult emergence↓ 
This can be particularly important for pests that pupate or develop in fallen fruits.

17. Crop Residue Management
Crop residues can serve two opposite roles.
They can:

Benefit soil
Residue→Organic matter+Soil cover+Nutrient cyclingResidue Organic\ matter+Soil\
cover+Nutrient\ cycling
But they can also:

Shelter pests
Residue→Pest survival/carryoverResidue Pest\ survival/carryover
Therefore residue management should be pest- and cropping-system specific.
The goal is not automatically to remove or burn all residues.

18. Volunteer Plant Management
A volunteer plant is a crop plant that grows unintentionally from previous-season seed or other
propagules.
Examples:
- Volunteer wheat
- Volunteer maize
- Volunteer soybean
These plants can act as:
Host→Food+Shelter+Breeding site 
Removing problematic volunteers can break pest continuity.

19. Alternate Host Removal
Some pests can survive on:
- Weeds
- Wild plants
- Volunteer crops
- Nearby cultivated crops
If these plants serve as important alternate hosts:
Alternate host removal→Pest survival↓ 
However, this should be done carefully because some non-crop vegetation also provides habitat and
food for beneficial insects.

20. Weed Management as Cultural Control
Weeds may:
- Compete with crops
- Host insect pests
- Provide shelter
- Maintain vectors
- Bridge pest populations between crop cycles
Therefore:
Weed management→Host removal→Pest pressure↓ 
But:
Not every weed is equally important as a pest reservoir   
Identification and ecological knowledge are necessary.

21. Proper Plant Spacing
Plant spacing affects:
- Canopy density
- Humidity
- Temperature
- Air movement
- Light penetration
- Pest movement
- Spray penetration
Very dense crops may create favorable microclimates for some pests and diseases.
Therefore:
Optimum spacing→Healthy crop architecture→Pest/disease risk management 

22. Plant Population Management
Excessive plant density can cause:
- Competition
- Weak growth
- Dense canopy
- Poor air movement
- Difficult scouting
- Difficult spray coverage
Insufficient density may reduce crop competitiveness against weeds.
Therefore:
Optimum plant population 
is the objective rather than simply high or low density.

23. Balanced Fertilization
Nutrient management can influence pest susceptibility.
Excessive nitrogen, for example, can sometimes produce:
- Soft succulent growth
- Greater attractiveness to some sucking pests
- Changes in plant chemistry
Therefore:
Excess nutrient≠Better pest resistance 
The goal is:
Balanced nutrition→Healthy vigorous crop 

24. Nitrogen Management
A particularly important relationship is:
Excessive N→Succulent growth→Potential susceptibility to some pests↑ 

This is pest-specific, so nitrogen should not be reduced indiscriminately.
Correct principle:
Right nutrient+Right rate+Right time+Right place 

25. Irrigation Management
Irrigation can influence pest populations indirectly through:
- Plant vigor
- Humidity
- Canopy microclimate
- Root health
- Plant stress
Both excessive and insufficient water can stress crops.
Therefore:
Proper water management→Healthy crop→Improved tolerance/resilience 

26. Drainage and Water Management
Poor drainage can create:
- Waterlogging
- Weak roots
- Poor plant growth
- Favorable conditions for some pests/pathogens
Therefore:
Good drainage→Healthy root system→Better crop resilience 

27. Land Preparation
Proper land preparation can reduce some soil-associated pests.
Methods may include:

- Appropriate tillage
- Soil solarization where applicable
- Proper field leveling
- Removal of crop residues
- Exposure of certain soil stages to predators/environment
However, excessive tillage can harm:
- Soil structure
- Soil organisms
- Organic matter
- Beneficial arthropods
Therefore:
Use only the degree of disturbance justified by the cropping system 

28. Tillage as Cultural Pest Control
Tillage can sometimes expose:
- Pupae
- Larvae
- Eggs
- Soil-dwelling pests
to:
- Predators
- Desiccation
- Temperature
- Physical damage
Conceptually:
Tillage→Soil pest disturbance→Survival↓ 
But conservation agriculture requires careful balancing of pest control with soil health.

29. Deep Tillage
Deep tillage can sometimes disrupt pests that spend important life stages in soil.
However:
Deep tillage≠universally beneficial 
Repeated deep tillage may:
- Increase erosion
- Reduce soil organic matter
- Damage soil structure
- Disturb beneficial organisms
Use should therefore be pest- and system-specific.

30. Trap Cropping
A trap crop is a plant grown to attract a pest away from the main crop.
Concept:
Trap crop→Pest attraction→Main crop pressure↓ 
Example concept:
Trap crop → attracts pest
↓
Main crop → protected

The trap crop must be managed appropriately; otherwise it can become a pest breeding source.

31. Trap Crop vs Barrier Crop
Trap crop
Designed primarily to attract pests.
Attract  Barrier crop
Primarily acts as a physical/ecological barrier or modifies pest movement.

Barrier disruption 
They are not necessarily the same.

32. Border Crops
A border crop is planted around the main crop.
Possible functions:
- Pest interception
- Wind modification
- Diversification
- Habitat creation
- Physical barrier
- Supporting natural enemies
Effectiveness is highly pest- and system-specific.

## 33. Intercropping
Intercropping means growing two or more crops together in a planned spatial arrangement.
Crop diversity→Host finding complexity→Potential pest pressure↓ 
Possible mechanisms include:
- Host dilution
- Physical interference
- Odor masking
- Increased natural enemies
- Reduced continuous host availability
However, some intercrops can also favor certain pests.
Therefore:
Intercropping effect is crop−pest specific 

34. Crop Diversification
Diversification can include:
- Crop rotation
- Intercropping
- Multiple crops
- Cover crops
- Border crops
- Habitat strips
General principle:
Greater diversity→More complex pest environment 
This can reduce pest buildup in some systems.

35. Resistant or Tolerant Varieties
Although resistance is often considered a separate pest-management category, variety selection is also
a crop-management decision with cultural effects.
A resistant variety can:
Host resistance→Pest establishment/damage↓ 
Traits may include:
- Antixenosis
- Antibiosis
- Tolerance

## 36. Antixenosis
Antixenosis means the plant is less preferred by the pest.
Plant attractiveness↓→Pest colonization↓ 

## 37. Antibiosis
Antibiosis means the plant adversely affects pest biology after feeding.
It may reduce:
- Pest growth
- Survival
- Development
- Reproduction
Conceptually:
Host resistance→Pest fitness↓ 

## 38. Tolerance
A tolerant crop can withstand or compensate for pest injury without suffering proportionally large
economic yield loss.
Pest injury→Yield loss relatively limited 

39. Nursery Management
For transplanted crops, proper nursery management can reduce early pest establishment.
Important practices:
- Healthy seed
- Clean nursery
- Appropriate spacing
- Removal of severely infested seedlings
- Weed control
- Proper irrigation
- Monitoring
- Healthy transplant selection
The principle is:
Healthy seedlings→Strong crop establishment 

40. Healthy Planting Material
Pests can sometimes be transported through:
- Infested seedlings
- Cuttings
- Tubers
- Bulbs
- Rhizomes
- Grafts
- Other planting materials
Therefore:
Clean planting material→Initial pest pressure↓ 

41. Proper Harvest Management
Harvesting at the appropriate maturity stage can reduce:
- Field exposure
- Pest reproduction
- Fruit damage
- Pest movement into storage
Therefore:
Timely harvest→Field pest exposure↓ 

42. Post-Harvest Cultural Control
Cultural control does not end when the crop is harvested.
Post-harvest practices include:
- Cleaning storage areas
- Removing old infested grain
- Cleaning equipment

- Removing spilled grain
- Proper drying
- Proper storage hygiene
- Preventing pest carryover
This is particularly important for stored-product pests.

43. Crop Calendar Management
A crop calendar can be designed to avoid continuous availability of favorable host stages.
Concept:
Crop calendar→Pest life cycle disruption 
It may coordinate:
- Sowing
- Harvest
- Rotation
- Residue management
- Fallow periods
- Neighboring crop arrangements

44. Fallow Period
A properly managed fallow period can sometimes interrupt pests that depend on continuous crop hosts.
Host absent→Pest reproduction↓ 
However, many pests can survive on weeds or alternate hosts, so fallowing is not universally effective.

45. Clean Cultivation
Clean cultivation means minimizing unnecessary pest reservoirs such as:
- Volunteer crops
- Certain pest-host weeds
- Infested residues

- Fallen fruits
- Abandoned crop material
But "clean" does not mean eliminating all vegetation.
This distinction is important because vegetation may provide habitat for beneficial insects.

46. Habitat Management
Cultural practices can be designed to support beneficial organisms.
For example:
Flowering habitat→Nectar/pollen→Natural enemies→Potential pest suppressionFlowering\ habitat
 Nectar/pollen  Natural\ enemies  Potential\ pest\ suppression
Thus cultural control can work with biological control, rather than against it.

47. Cultural Control and Natural Enemies
Good cultural management can:
Pest pressure↓+Natural enemy habitat maintained 
Examples:
- Diversified cropping
- Flowering strips
- Reduced unnecessary pesticide use
- Appropriate field margins
- Habitat management
The goal is:
Pest suppression+Beneficial insect conservation 

48. Cultural Control and Weed Management
Weeds can function as:
- Competitors
- Pest hosts

- Vector hosts
- Shelter
Therefore cultural practices can include:
Crop competition+Timely weeding+Crop rotation+Mulching 
to reduce weed-mediated pest problems.

49. Cultural Control and Pest Life Cycle
One of the best ways to understand cultural control is to ask:
Where does the pest survive between crop cycles?
Possible locations:
- Soil
- Crop residue
- Volunteer plants
- Weeds
- Fallen fruit
- Storage
- Alternative hosts
Then:
Identify survival site→Modify/remove survival site→Break life cycle 

50. Example: Soil-Dwelling Pest
Suppose a pest spends part of its life in soil.
Possible cultural strategy:
Crop rotation+appropriate soil management+sanitation  +   +  
Then:
Host continuity↓→Soil pest survival↓ 

51. Example: Fruit Pest
Suppose a pest develops in fallen fruits.
Management:
Regularly remove fallen/infested fruits 
Then:
Breeding substrate↓→Adult emergence↓ 

52. Example: Sucking Pest
Suppose a sucking pest builds up under excessive nitrogen and dense crop growth.
Cultural management may include:
- Balanced nitrogen
- Appropriate plant population
- Proper irrigation
- Weed management
- Monitoring
This can modify the crop environment and reduce favorable conditions.

53. Advantages of Cultural Control
## 1. Preventive
It can act before pest populations become severe.

2. Environmentally compatible
It generally relies on crop-management changes rather than direct pesticide application.

3. Can reduce pesticide dependence
Cultural control→Pest pressure↓→Chemical intervention potentially↓Cultural\ control  Pest\ pressure  Chemical\ intervention\ potentially 4. Can be economical
Some practices require little additional input when incorporated into normal farm operations.

5. Compatible with IPM
It works with:
- Biological control
- Resistant varieties
- Mechanical control
- Chemical control

6. Can reduce pest carryover
Through:
- Rotation
- Sanitation
- Harvest management
- Residue management

54. Limitations of Cultural Control
Cultural control also has limitations.

## 1. Pest-specific
A method effective against one pest may be ineffective against another.

2. Requires planning
Crop rotation and planting schedules must be designed in advance.

3. May not provide immediate control
Severe infestations may require additional tactics.

4. May conflict with farm economics
Changing crop sequence may affect market opportunities.

5. Neighboring fields can influence pest pressure
Mobile pests can migrate into a managed field.

6. Some pests have broad host ranges
Rotation may be less effective.

7. Some pests survive for long periods
Long-lived soil stages can reduce rotation effectiveness.

55. Cultural Control vs Chemical Control
Cultural control
Chemical control
Modifies crop environment
Uses pesticide
Usually preventive
Often direct
Reduces pest establishment/survival
Directly affects pest
Generally slower
Often faster
Can support natural enemies
Some products can harm natural enemies
Lower resistance-selection pressure when used alone Can select for resistance
Requires planning
Requires correct product/timing/application
The best IPM program often combines compatible tactics.

56. Cultural Control vs Mechanical Control
Cultural
Changes the production system.
Examples:
- Rotation
- Planting time
- Plant density
- Sanitation
- Crop diversification

Mechanical
Physically removes or destroys pests.
Examples:
- Hand picking
- Traps
- Barriers
- Mechanical removal
Memory:

Cultural=Change the system 
Mechanical=Physically remove/control 

57. Cultural Control vs Biological Control
Cultural control
Modify crop environment 

Biological control
Use/conserve living natural enemies 
They can work together:
Cultural management→Natural enemy conservation→Biological control 

58. Four Major Principles to Remember
Cultural control can be remembered using four major objectives:
PREVENT   
Prevent pest establishment.
DISRUPT   
Disrupt the pest life cycle.
SUPPRESS   
Suppress pest multiplication.
PROTECT   
Protect the crop and beneficial organisms.

59. Master Classification
Before planting
- Crop rotation
- Variety selection
- Field sanitation
- Land preparation

- Removal of volunteers
- Weed management
- Planting-time planning

During planting
- Proper sowing time
- Proper spacing
- Appropriate plant population
- Healthy planting material
- Trap crops
- Intercropping

During crop growth
- Balanced fertilization
- Proper irrigation
- Weed control
- Removal of infested plant parts
- Crop monitoring
- Habitat management

At harvest
- Timely harvest
- Removal of heavily infested produce
- Avoiding unnecessary field carryover

After harvest
- Residue management
- Storage sanitation
- Removal of spilled grain
- Cleaning equipment
- Proper drying/storage

60. Cultural Control Decision Framework
When designing cultural control, ask:

Question 1
Where does the pest survive?

Question 2
When does it reproduce?

Question 3
What crop stage does it prefer?

Question 4
What environmental conditions favor it?

Question 5
Does it have alternate hosts?

Question 6
Can crop rotation interrupt it?

Question 7
Can planting time avoid its peak activity?

Question 8
Can sanitation remove its breeding sites?

Question 9
Can crop architecture be modified?

Question 10
Can natural enemies be conserved?
Then:
Pest biology→Cultural intervention→Pest suppression 

61. Important Formulas and Concepts
Pest density
Pest density=Number of pestsSampling unit  Sampling\
unit   

Economic Injury Level
EIL=CV×I×D×K  V I D K   

Predator:pest ratio
Predator:Pest=PredatorsPests  Pests   

Parasitism
Parasitism(%)=Parasitized hostsTotal hosts×100 
 Total\ hosts  100  
These help determine whether cultural control and other IPM measures are achieving the desired
reduction in pest pressure.

62. Important Exam Questions
What is cultural control?
Cultural control is the management of pests by modifying crop production practices and the
agricultural environment to make conditions unfavorable for pest establishment, survival,
reproduction or damage.

Give examples.
- Crop rotation
- Timely sowing
- Timely harvesting
- Field sanitation
- Residue management
- Weed management
- Proper plant spacing
- Balanced fertilization
- Proper irrigation

- Trap cropping
- Intercropping
- Removal of volunteer plants
- Use of resistant varieties

What is the main principle?
Make the crop environment unfavorable for the pest and favorable for the crop.   

63. Final One-Read Summary
Cultural control manages pests by changing the way crops are grown rather than directly relying on
pesticides.
Its basic mechanism is:
Change farming practice→Disrupt pest biology→Reduce pest survival/reproduction→Reduce crop da
mage      
The major methods are:
Crop rotationTimely sowingTimely harvestingField sanitationCrop-residue managementVolunteer/
alternate-host managementWeed managementProper spacing and plant populationBalanced fertilization
Proper irrigation and drainageTrap cropsBorder cropsIntercroppingCrop diversificationResistant/
tolerant varietiesHealthy planting materialProper nursery managementStorage sanitation  & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & 
   
The deepest concept is:
Cultural control = manage the crop and its environment so that the pest cannot easily survive, reproduc
e, spread or cause economic damage.   
And within IPM:
Prevention + Monitoring + Cultural Control + Biological Control + Other Appropriate Tactics   

Ultimate memory line
Cultural control does not primarily kill the pest—it changes the farming system so the
pest finds the environment less suitable and the crop becomes better able to escape,
tolerate, or withstand pest pressure.

## Module 15 — Insect & Pest
## Management
## Control Methods — Mechanical
## Control
## 1. Introduction
Mechanical control is a pest-management method in which pests are physically removed, destroyed,
trapped, blocked, excluded, or otherwise prevented from reaching or damaging the crop.
Unlike cultural control, which changes the farming system, mechanical control primarily involves a
direct physical action against the pest or its access to the crop.
The basic principle is:
Physical action→Pest removal/exclusion/destruction→Pest population↓→Crop damage↓        
Mechanical control is an important component of Integrated Pest Management (IPM) because it can
reduce pest populations without necessarily relying on chemical pesticides.

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
Mechanical control is the management of pests by physically removing, killing,
trapping, excluding, or destroying pests or their breeding sites.

Exam-ready definition
Mechanical control is a pest-management method that uses physical force, barriers,
traps, tools, or equipment to remove, destroy, immobilize, or prevent pests from
reaching and damaging crops.

3. Basic Principle
Mechanical control works through direct physical intervention:
Detect→Physically intervene→Pest removed/destroyed/excluded 
It can act on:
- Eggs
- Larvae
- Nymphs
- Pupae
- Adults
- Pest-infested plant parts
- Pest breeding sites
- Pest movement pathways

4. Objectives of Mechanical Control
The main objectives are:
1. Remove pests directly
2. Destroy pest eggs or immature stages
3. Prevent pests from reaching crops
4. Trap mobile adults
5. Destroy pest breeding sites
6. Reduce pest reproduction
7. Reduce pest migration into the crop
8. Reduce pest population before economic damage occurs
9. Reduce dependence on pesticides
10.Protect beneficial organisms where possible
11.Improve sanitation
12.Support IPM

5. Major Mechanical Control Methods
Important methods include:
1. Hand picking
## 2. Crushing
3. Collection and destruction of egg masses
4. Removal of infested plant parts
## 5. Pruning
## 6. Trapping
7. Sticky traps
8. Light traps
9. Pheromone traps
10.Pitfall traps
11.Water traps
12.Barrier methods
13.Netting
14.Insect-proof screens
15.Row covers
16.Physical exclusion
17.Soil solarization
18.Mechanical cultivation
19.Mechanical weed removal
20.Shaking/beating plants
21.Vacuum collection
22.Mechanical harvesting
23.Destruction of infested residues
24.Storage sanitation
25.Rodent trapping and exclusion
26.Physical destruction of pest shelters

6. Hand Picking
Hand picking is one of the simplest mechanical methods.
Large, visible pests are manually collected from plants.
Examples include:
- Caterpillars
- Beetles
- Large larvae
- Egg masses
- Snails/slugs in suitable situations
Process:
Scout→Identify→Collect→Destroy/remove 
It is particularly useful when pests are:
- Large
- Easily visible
- Localized
- Relatively immobile
- Present at low population density

## 7. Crushing
Some egg masses or exposed pests can be physically crushed.
For example:
Egg mass→Crushing→Egg mortality↑Egg\ mass  Crushing  Egg\
mortality This prevents:
Egg→Larva/Nymph→AdultEgg Larva/Nymph Adult
and therefore reduces future pest generations.

8. Egg Mass Collection
Some insects lay eggs in easily visible groups.
These egg masses can be:
## 1. Identified
## 2. Collected
3. Removed from the field
4. Destroyed appropriately
The principle is:
Egg removal→Hatching↓→Future pest population↓ 

Important caution
Do not destroy every egg-like structure automatically.
Some eggs may belong to beneficial insects or parasitoids.
Correct approach:
Identify→Decide→Remove 

9. Removal of Infested Plant Parts
Heavily infested portions of plants can sometimes be removed.
Examples:
- Infested shoots
- Leaves
- Fruits
- Pods
- Branches
- Stems
Conceptually:
Infested plant part→Removal→Pest biomass/breeding sites↓ 
Removed material must be managed so that surviving pests do not return to the field.

## 10. Pruning
In perennial crops, pruning can remove pest-infested:
- Branches
- Shoots
- Twigs
- Leaves
It may simultaneously improve:
- Canopy aeration
- Light penetration
- Crop architecture
Thus:
Pruning→Infested tissue removal+Canopy modification 
Pruning must be crop- and pest-specific.

11. Shaking or Beating Plants
Some pests can be dislodged by:
- Shaking
- Beating
- Mechanical vibration
A sheet or collection surface can be placed below the crop.
Concept:
Plant shaking→Pest dislodgement→Collection→RemovalPlant\ shaking  Pest\
dislodgement  Collection  Removal
This can be particularly useful for relatively mobile or easily dislodged insects.

## 12. Trapping
Trapping is one of the most important mechanical pest-control methods.
A trap physically captures or kills pests.

General process:
Pest attracted/encounters trap→Capture→Removal 
Traps can use:
- Light
- Color
- Pheromones
- Food
- Water
- Physical pit
- Sticky surfaces
- Sex attractants
- Aggregation signals

13. Sticky Traps
Sticky traps contain an adhesive surface that captures insects when they land on it.
Common colors include:
- Yellow
- Blue
Their effectiveness depends on pest species and behavior.
General mechanism:
Visual attraction→Trap landing→Adhesion→Pest capture 
They can be used for:
- Monitoring
- Mass trapping in appropriate systems
- Population assessment

14. Yellow Sticky Traps
Yellow is attractive to several flying insects.
They can help monitor pests such as:
- Whiteflies
- Aphids
- Leafhoppers
- Some flies
But:
Trap catch≠Automatic need for pesticide 
Trap catches should be interpreted together with:
- Pest density
- Crop stage
- Damage
- Natural enemies
- Economic thresholds

15. Blue Sticky Traps
Blue traps are commonly used for monitoring some thrips.
Concept:
Blue trap→Visual attraction→Thrips captureBlue\ trap  Visual\ attraction  Thrips\ capture
Effectiveness varies with species and environment.

16. Light Traps
Light traps attract insects that respond to light.
General mechanism:
Light→Insect attraction→Collection/trapping 
They can be useful for monitoring and, in certain situations, population suppression.

However, light traps are not automatically selective for pests.
They may also attract:
- Beneficial insects
- Non-target insects
Therefore, their use should be evaluated carefully.

17. Pheromone Traps
Pheromone traps use chemical signals produced or mimicked from insect communication systems.
Many are based on sex pheromones.
Mechanism:
Pheromone→Adult attraction→Trap capture 
Uses include:
- Monitoring
- Detection
- Flight/activity monitoring
- Population estimation
- Mass trapping in suitable systems
- Mating disruption when deployed as a specialized management tactic

18. Pheromone Traps and Monitoring
A pheromone trap is particularly useful for determining:
- Whether a pest is present
- When adults appear
- Population trends
- Peak flight periods
For example:
Trap catch / timeTrap\ catch\ /\ time
can be plotted to determine pest activity.

But:
High trap catch≠Automatically high crop damage 
The relationship must be calibrated for the crop-pest system.

19. Pitfall Traps
Pitfall traps capture insects and other arthropods moving on the soil surface.
Basic principle:
Walking pest→Falls into trap→Cannot easily escape 
They are commonly useful for monitoring ground-active organisms.
However, pitfall traps may also capture beneficial predators such as ground beetles.
Therefore:
Target pest identification+Non−target assessment 
is important.

20. Water Traps
Water or liquid-filled traps can capture certain flying insects.
The trap may combine:
- Color
- Visual attraction
- Water
- Attractant
with:
Attraction  21. Barrier Methods
A barrier physically prevents pests from reaching the crop.

Examples:
- Nets
- Screens
- Row covers
- Physical fences
- Tree guards
- Insect-proof structures
Basic principle:
Barrier→Pest movement blocked→Crop protected 

22. Insect-Proof Netting
Fine mesh can prevent insects from entering a crop area.
It can be particularly useful in:
- Nurseries
- Protected cultivation
- High-value vegetable production
- Seed production
- Insect-vector exclusion
The mesh size must be appropriate for the target pest.

23. Row Covers
Row covers physically separate crops from insects.
Concept:
Crop→Physical covering→Pest access↓ 
They can reduce contact between crops and:
- Flying insects
- Some crawling pests
- Some vectors

But the covering must be properly managed.

24. Physical Exclusion
Physical exclusion means preventing pest entry into the crop environment.
Examples:
- Screening
- Net houses
- Insect-proof doors
- Sealed nursery structures
- Protective covers
The principle is:
Pest entry↓→Initial infestation↓ 
This is especially important for pests that migrate into crops from outside.

25. Soil Solarization
Soil solarization uses transparent plastic to trap solar energy and increase soil temperature.
Concept:
Solar radiation→Plastic covering→Soil temperature↑→Some soil pests/pathogens/weed propagules m
- rtality↑Solar\ radiation  Plastic\ covering  Soil\ temperature  Some\ soil\ pests/pathogens/weed\ propagules\ mortality It is a physical/environmental control technique rather than a conventional pesticide treatment.
Its effectiveness depends on:
- Solar radiation
- Temperature
- Duration
- Soil moisture
- Soil depth
- Target organism

26. Mechanical Cultivation
Mechanical cultivation can physically disturb pests and their habitats.
It may:
- Destroy pest stages in soil
- Expose pests to predators/environment
- Remove weeds serving as pest hosts
- Disturb pest shelters
Conceptually:
Mechanical disturbance→Pest habitat disruption→Survival↓ 
However, excessive cultivation can damage soil health.

27. Mechanical Weed Removal
Mechanical removal includes:
- Hoeing
- Cultivation
- Mowing
- Inter-row cultivation
- Mechanical weeders
Weeds can serve as:
- Alternate hosts
- Pest reservoirs
- Shelter
Thus:
Mechanical weed control→Alternate host availability↓→Some pest pressure↓ 

28. Vacuum Collection
Specialized vacuum equipment can physically remove insects from crops.
Mechanism:
Air suction→Insect removal→Population↓ 
It may be useful in high-value crops or controlled environments.
The main challenge is avoiding excessive damage to plants and beneficial organisms.

29. Mechanical Destruction of Pest Breeding
Sites
Some pests depend on specific breeding materials.
Examples include:
- Fallen fruits
- Infested residues
- Old crop material
- Pest-infested storage material
Removing and destroying these sites can reduce future pest generations.
Breeding site removal→Reproduction↓→Next generation↓ 

30. Storage Sanitation as Mechanical/Physical
Management
After harvest, physical measures become important.
Examples:
- Cleaning grain stores
- Removing old grain
- Cleaning machinery
- Removing spilled grain

- Screening openings
- Repairing cracks and entry points
- Physical separation of infested material
Principle:
Sanitation+Exclusion→Stored pest establishment↓ 

31. Mechanical Control of Rodents
Mechanical methods can include:
- Traps
- Physical barriers
- Sealing entry points
- Removing food sources
- Removing nesting sites
Concept:
Food/entry/nesting opportunity↓+Trapping→Rodent pressure↓ 

32. Mechanical Control of Snails and Slugs
Where relevant, mechanical methods include:
- Hand collection
- Trapping
- Removal of hiding places
- Physical barriers
Because these organisms often remain close to the soil surface, targeted physical removal can
sometimes be effective in small or high-value plots.

33. Mechanical Control of Large Caterpillars
For large, visible caterpillars:

Scouting→Hand picking→Removal 
This can be particularly useful when:
- Pest density is low
- Plants are small
- Pests are visible
- Farm scale permits labor-intensive control

34. Mechanical Control of Egg → Adult Cycle
Mechanical control can target different life stages:
Egg→Larva/Nymph→Pupa→Adult 

Eggs
- Collect
- Crush
- Remove

Larvae/nymphs
- Hand pick
- Shake off
- Vacuum
- Trap

Pupae
- Disturb soil/residues where appropriate

Adults
- Traps
- Nets
- Physical exclusion
Thus:
Targeting multiple life stages→Greater opportunity for population suppression 

35. Mechanical Control and Pest Life Cycle
Understanding the life cycle is essential.
For example:
Egg→Larva→Pupa→AdultEgg Larva Pupa Adult
If the larva is exposed and easily collected:
Larval removal→Pupation↓→Adult emergence↓ 
Therefore:
Pest biology→Correct life stage→Mechanical intervention 

36. Mechanical Control and IPM
Mechanical control fits into IPM as follows:
## Identify→Monitor→Assess→Decide→Mechanically Control→Re−Monitor\
boxed  IDENTIFY  MONITOR  ASSESS  DECIDE  MECHANICALLY\ CONTROL  RE-MONITOR  
For example:
Pest detected
↓
Identify species
↓
Count population
↓
Check crop stage and damage
↓
Check ETL/natural enemies
↓
Use hand picking/traps/barriers if appropriate
↓

Monitor again

37. Mechanical Control and Economic
Threshold
Mechanical control should not necessarily be performed simply because one insect is seen.
The decision should consider:
- Pest density
- Crop stage
- Pest stage
- Damage
- Economic threshold
- Labor cost
- Natural enemies
- Expected pest growth
Remember:
ETL<EIL 
and:
EIL=CV×I×D×K  V I D K   
Mechanical control may be especially attractive when the cost of physical intervention is lower than the
expected economic loss.

38. Advantages of Mechanical Control
1. No conventional pesticide residue from the control action
Physical removal itself does not leave a chemical pesticide residue.

2. Can be highly targeted
For example:
Remove one visible egg mass 
instead of treating the entire field.

3. Useful in small areas
Especially:
- Nurseries
- Kitchen gardens
- High-value crops
- Protected cultivation

4. Can conserve natural enemies
When carefully targeted, it can avoid broad-spectrum pesticide exposure.

5. Useful for pesticide-resistant pests
Physical removal is not affected by biochemical herbicide/insecticide resistance mechanisms.

6. Useful in organic systems
Many physical methods can be compatible with organic production standards, subject to applicable
standards.

7. Can reduce pesticide dependence
Mechanical control→Pest population↓→Chemical requirement potentially↓Mechanical\ control
 Pest\ population  Chemical\ requirement\ potentially 39. Limitations of Mechanical Control
1. Labor intensive
Hand collection requires substantial labor.

2. Time consuming
Large fields may be difficult to manage manually.

3. Less practical for highly mobile pests
Flying insects can escape easily.

4. Difficult at very high pest densities
Once populations become extremely large, physical removal may become impractical.

5. Equipment costs
Some methods require:
- Traps

- Netting
- Vacuum equipment
- Specialized machinery

6. Non-target capture
Some traps can capture beneficial organisms.

7. Crop damage risk
Improper mechanical operations can injure plants.

## 8. Reinfestation
Pests can migrate from surrounding fields.
Therefore:
Mechanical control is often most effective when used early, selectively, and repeatedly where necessary
. 

40. Mechanical vs Cultural Control
Feature
Cultural control
Mechanical control
Main approach Modify farming system
Physical intervention
Main target
Pest environment/life cycle Pest or access directly
Examples
Rotation, planting time
Traps, hand picking
Nature
Mostly preventive
Direct physical
Labor
Variable
Often high
Speed
Often gradual
Can be immediate
Best use
Prevention
Removal/exclusion
IPM role
Major
Major

Memory:
Cultural = change the system  
Mechanical = physically act on the pest  

41. Mechanical vs Chemical Control
Mechanical
Physical action
No pesticide needed for the control action
Usually highly targeted

Chemical
Chemical action
Requires pesticide
May cover large areas

Mechanical
Chemical
Often labor intensive
Often faster over large areas
No chemical resistance mechanism
Resistance can develop
Usually lower residue concern from control action Residue and environmental concerns possible
Can conserve beneficials if selective
Non-target toxicity depends on product

42. Mechanical vs Biological Control
Mechanical
Human physical action 

Biological
Living organisms/natural enemies 
Examples:
Mechanical:
Trap→Pest captureTrap Pest\ capture
Biological:
Ladybird→Aphid predationLadybird Aphid\ predation

43. Mechanical Control vs Physical Control
These terms are sometimes used differently across agricultural texts.
Mechanical control generally emphasizes physical human intervention such as:
- Hand picking
- Trapping
- Barriers
- Mechanical removal
- Cultivation
Physical control may include environmental manipulation such as:
- Heat
- Cold
- Solarization
- Radiation

- Physical exclusion
There is some overlap.
Therefore, terminology can vary by textbook.

44. Important Practical Rule
A mechanical method should be evaluated using:
Effectiveness+Cost+Labor+Crop safety+Non−target effects+Scalability 
A method that works in a kitchen garden may not be practical on 100 hectares.

45. Mechanical Control and Beneficial Insects
This is extremely important.
Some traps can capture both:
Pests+BeneficialsPests+Beneficials
Therefore:
Trap selectivity matters 
Before using a trap extensively, determine:
- Target species
- Non-target species
- Trap location
- Trap density
- Trap timing
- Crop stage

46. Mechanical Control and Pheromone Traps
Pheromone trapping can serve different purposes:

Monitoring
Trap catch→Pest activity informationTrap\ catch Pest\ activity\ information

Mass trapping
Many traps→Adult removal→Matingopportunities↓Many\ traps  Adult\ removal
 Mating opportunities The actual effectiveness depends strongly on pest biology, trap density, immigration and surrounding
pest populations.

47. Mechanical Control and Mating Disruption
Mating disruption is usually classified as a behavioral/chemical communication-based management
tactic, rather than simple mechanical control.
It works by saturating the environment with synthetic pheromone signals so males have difficulty
locating females.
Therefore:
Pheromone trap≠Mating disruption 
Both use insect communication signals but operate differently.

48. Mechanical Control and Sanitation
A useful distinction:

Sanitation
Removes pest sources:
Infested material→RemovalInfested\ material Removal

Mechanical trapping
Captures mobile pests:
Pest→Trap→RemovalPest Trap Removal

Physical exclusion
Prevents entry:
Pest→Barrier→Crop protectedPest Barrier Crop\ protected
All can work together.

49. Practical Example
Suppose a vegetable field contains a low population of large caterpillars.

Step 1 — Identify
Crop→Damage→CaterpillarCrop Damage Caterpillar

Step 2 — Monitor
Count caterpillars per plant.

Step 3 — Assess
Consider:
- Crop stage
- Damage
- Natural enemies
- Pest density
- ETL/local recommendations

Step 4 — Mechanical control
If practical:
Hand pick→Remove caterpillarsHand\ pick Remove\ caterpillars

Step 5 — Re-monitor
Before control→After controlBefore\ control After\ control
This is much more rational than automatically spraying simply because caterpillars were observed.

51. Master Classification of Mechanical
Methods
Mechanical Control=Removal+Destruction+Trapping+Exclusion+Physical Disturbance 

Removal
- Hand picking
- Infested-part removal
- Pruning
- Vacuuming

Destruction
- Crushing eggs
- Destroying breeding material
- Mechanical cultivation

Trapping
- Sticky traps
- Light traps
- Pheromone traps
- Pitfall traps
- Water traps

Exclusion
- Netting
- Screens
- Row covers
- Barriers

Physical disturbance
- Shaking
- Beating
- Cultivation
- Soil disturbance

52. Key Formulas for Mechanical/IPM Decisions
Pest density
Pest density=Number of pestsSampling unit  Sampling\
unit   

Trap catch
Trap catch=Number capturedTrap×time  Trap time   

Egg viability
Egg viability(%)=Viable eggsTotal eggs×100  Total\
eggs  100  

Hatching percentage
Hatching(%)=Eggs hatchedEggs observed×100  Eggs\
- bserved  100
