EIL=CV×I×D×K  V I D K   

Economic Threshold
ETL<EIL 
These concepts help determine whether mechanical intervention is economically justified.

53. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1
Thinking every insect should be removed.
Correct:
Identify→Assess→Act 

Mistake 2
Assuming every trap is a control method.
Some traps are primarily monitoring tools.

Mistake 3
Assuming trap catch equals crop damage.
Trap catch≠Economic damage 

Mistake 4
Ignoring beneficial insects.
Some traps can capture predators and pollinators.

Mistake 5
Using physical control after pest populations become extremely high.
Mechanical methods are often most practical when pest populations are:
- Low
- Localized
- Accessible

- Easily identifiable

Mistake 6
Using barriers without considering pollination.
In crops requiring insect pollination:
Insect exclusion→Pollination problem 
unless pollination is managed appropriately.

54. Exam-Ready Short Answer
Mechanical control is a pest-management method that uses physical means such as
hand picking, crushing, trapping, barriers, netting, removal of infested plant parts,
mechanical cultivation, and other physical interventions to remove, destroy,
immobilize, or exclude pests. It is an important IPM component and is especially
useful for visible, localized, low-density pests and in nurseries, protected cultivation,
and high-value crops.

55. Final Conceptual Summary
The complete mechanism is:
Detection→Identification→Physical intervention→Pest removal/exclusion/destruction→Population re
duction→Reduced crop damage        
The five major categories are:
REMOVE + DESTROY + TRAP + EXCLUDE + DISTURB   
Final memory line
Mechanical control = physically remove, destroy, trap, block, or disturb the pest or its
breeding site.
And the most important IPM principle remains:
Identify first → Monitor → Assess → Choose the appropriate control method → Remonitor   

## Module 15 — Insect & Pest
## Management
## Control Methods — Biological
## Control
## 1. Introduction
Biological control is a major component of Integrated Pest Management (IPM) in which living
- rganisms or their natural activities are used to suppress pest populations and keep them below
economically damaging levels.
Instead of directly killing pests through physical force or synthetic chemicals, biological control uses
natural ecological relationships such as:
- Predation
- Parasitism/parasitoidism
- Pathogenicity
- Competition
- Antagonism
- Herbivory
The central idea is:
Natural enemy/biological agent→Pest mortality or suppression→Pest population↓→Crop damage↓ xed  Natural\ enemy/biological\ agent  Pest\ mortality\ or\ suppression  Pest\
population  Crop\ damage  

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
Biological control is the suppression of pests by using living organisms or their
biological activities.

Exam-ready definition
Biological control is the use, conservation, or enhancement of natural enemies and
- ther biological agents to reduce pest populations and prevent them from reaching
economically damaging levels.

3. Basic Principle
Every agricultural ecosystem contains interactions between:
Crop↔Pest↔Natural Enemies 
For example:
Aphid→Ladybird beetle  The ladybird consumes aphids, reducing their population.
Another example:
Pest egg→Trichogramma  The parasitoid develops inside the pest egg, preventing the pest from completing its development.

4. Main Biological Control Agents
Biological control agents can broadly include:
## 1. Predators
## 2. Parasitoids
## 3. Pathogens
4. Microbial biocontrol agents
5. Entomopathogenic fungi
6. Entomopathogenic bacteria
7. Entomopathogenic viruses
8. Entomopathogenic nematodes
9. Antagonistic microorganisms
10.Competitive organisms
11.Herbivorous biological control agents for weeds
12.Natural enemies conserved in the ecosystem

5. Major Categories of Biological Control
There are three major classical categories:

Natural/Conservation+Augmentative+Classical Biological Control 
They differ mainly in how the biological agent is managed or introduced.

6. Natural Biological Control
Natural biological control occurs when naturally occurring enemies suppress pests without deliberate
mass release.
For example:
Aphid population→Ladybird + Lacewing + Parasitoids→Aphid suppression 
  Natural enemies may already be present in:
- Crop fields
- Field margins
- Hedgerows
- Soil
- Crop residues
- Natural vegetation

7. Conservation Biological Control
Conservation biological control means protecting and enhancing naturally occurring beneficial
- rganisms.
The objective is:
Protect natural enemies→Natural enemy survival↑→Pest suppression↑ 
Practices include:
- Avoiding unnecessary broad-spectrum pesticide use
- Maintaining flowering plants
- Maintaining suitable habitat
- Providing shelter
- Maintaining crop diversity

- Providing nectar and pollen
- Protecting overwintering sites
- Reducing unnecessary disturbance

8. Why Conservation Matters
A field may already contain:
- Predators
- Parasitoids
- Pollinators
- Decomposers
Unnecessary pesticide application can kill both:
Pests+Beneficials 
Therefore IPM attempts to achieve:
Maximum pest suppression+Minimum natural enemy disruption 

9. Augmentative Biological Control
Augmentative biological control involves deliberately increasing the population of a beneficial
- rganism, usually through release.
General process:
Mass production→Quality control→Release→Natural enemy population↑→Pest suppression 
It can involve:

Inoculative release
Small numbers are released with the expectation that they will reproduce.

Inundative release
Large numbers are released to provide rapid suppression.

10. Classical Biological Control
Classical biological control generally involves introducing and establishing a natural enemy against an
invasive or introduced pest where appropriate.
General concept:
Pest introduced→Natural enemy identified→Safety/testing→Approved introduction→Establishment→
Long−term suppression 
Because introduced organisms can create ecological risks, modern classical biological control requires
careful:
- Host-range testing
- Risk assessment
- Regulatory approval
- Quarantine
- Ecological evaluation

## 11. Predators
A predator is an organism that attacks and consumes multiple prey during its lifetime.
Core relationship:
1 Predator→Many prey 
Examples of predatory insects include:
- Ladybird beetles
- Lacewings
- Syrphid fly larvae
- Assassin bugs
- Predatory stink bugs
- Ground beetles
- Rove beetles
- Mantises
- Predatory earwigs

- Dragonflies and damselflies
- Some predatory wasps
- Some predatory thrips

12. Ladybird Beetles
Ladybird beetles are important predators in many agricultural systems.
They may feed on:
- Aphids
- Scale insects
- Mealybugs
- Whiteflies
- Other soft-bodied arthropods
Both larvae and adults can be predatory in many species.
Basic interaction:
Ladybird→Aphid→Aphid mortality 

## 13. Lacewings
Lacewing larvae are commonly called aphid lions.
They can prey on:
- Aphids
- Whiteflies
- Thrips
- Mites
- Insect eggs
- Other small arthropods
Concept:
Lacewing larva→Multiple prey→Pest suppression 

14. Syrphid Flies
Syrphid fly larvae can prey on aphids.
Adults often visit flowers and can contribute to pollination.
Therefore one insect group can have different ecological roles at different life stages:
Larva=Predator 
Adult=Flower visitor/potential pollinator 

15. Ground Beetles
Ground beetles are important soil-surface predators.
They may attack:
- Insect eggs
- Larvae
- Caterpillars
- Other arthropods
- Some weed seeds
Their importance can be enhanced by suitable ground habitat and reduced unnecessary disturbance.

16. Assassin Bugs
Assassin bugs are predatory true bugs.
They capture and feed on other insects using piercing-sucking mouthparts.
Conceptually:
Assassin bug→Pest insect→Predation 

## 17. Parasitoids
A parasitoid is an organism, usually an insect, whose immature stage develops on or inside a single
host and eventually kills that host.
Core relationship:
Parasitoid→Host→Development→Host death 

18. Predator vs Parasitoid
Predator
Parasitoid
Usually consumes many prey Usually develops on/in one host
Kills prey by consumption
Host generally dies due to parasitoid development
Often free-living predator
Immature parasitoid depends on host
Example: ladybird
Example: Trichogramma
Memory:
PREDATOR=EAT 
PARASITOID=DEVELOP 

19. Important Parasitoid Groups
Important parasitoid groups include:
- Trichogrammatidae
- Braconidae
- Ichneumonidae
- Aphelinidae
- Encyrtidae
- Eulophidae
- Tachinidae
Their host range and developmental biology vary greatly.

## 20. Trichogramma
Trichogramma are very small parasitoid wasps that commonly parasitize insect eggs.
Basic mechanism:
Pest egg→Trichogramma oviposition→Parasitoid development→Pest embryo destroyed 
Therefore the pest may never reach the damaging larval stage.

21. Aphid Parasitoids
Some parasitoids attack aphids.
A characteristic sign is an aphid mummy.
Concept:
Aphid→Parasitoid development→Aphid mummy→Adult parasitoidAphid  Parasitoid\
development  Aphid\ mummy  Adult\ parasitoid
Finding mummified aphids can therefore indicate natural biological control activity.

22. Pathogens as Biological Control Agents
Microorganisms can cause disease in pests.
Important groups include:
## 1. Bacteria
## 2. Fungi
## 3. Viruses
## 4. Nematodes
5. Other microbial agents
General mechanism:
Pathogen→Infection→Disease→Pest mortality 

23. Entomopathogenic Bacteria
These are bacteria capable of infecting and killing insects.
One important example is:
Bacillus thuringiensis (Bt)
Certain Bt strains produce insecticidal proteins that affect susceptible insect larvae.
General mechanism:
Bt→Larval ingestion→Toxin activation→Gut damage→Larval mortalityBt  Larval\
ingestion  Toxin\ activation  Gut\ damage  Larval\ mortality
Bt activity is highly dependent on the particular strain/toxin and target insect.

24. Entomopathogenic Fungi
Examples include:
- Beauveria bassiana
- Metarhizium spp.
- Lecanicillium spp.
- Isaria / related taxa used in biocontrol contexts
General mechanism:
Spore→Cuticle attachment→Germination→Penetration→Internal infection→MortalitySpore
 Cuticle\ attachment  Germination  Penetration  Internal\
infection  Mortality
Unlike many microbial agents that must be ingested, entomopathogenic fungi can infect insects through
the cuticle.

25. Entomopathogenic Viruses
Some viruses specifically infect insects.
An important group is:
Baculoviruses
Examples include:
- Nucleopolyhedroviruses (NPVs)
- Granuloviruses (GVs)
General mechanism:
Virus→Ingestion→Replication→Tissue damage→Insect deathVirus  Ingestion  Replication  Tissue\ damage  Insect\ death
They can be highly host-specific.

26. Entomopathogenic Nematodes
Some nematodes can infect insects.
Important genera include:
- Steinernema

- Heterorhabditis
They commonly work in association with symbiotic bacteria.
Simplified mechanism:
Nematode→Host entry→Symbiotic bacteria→Septicemia→Host deathNematode  Host\
entry  Symbiotic\ bacteria  Septicemia  Host\ death
They can be useful against certain soil-dwelling or cryptic insect stages.

27. Microbial Biological Control
Microbial control can be represented as:
Microorganism→Host infection→Disease→Pest mortality 
Important groups:
Agent
Example
General target
Bacteria Bacillus thuringiensis Susceptible insect larvae
Fungi
Beauveria
Various insects
Fungi
Metarhizium
Various insects/soil-associated pests
Virus
NPV
Specific susceptible insect larvae
Nematode Steinernema
Some soil/cryptic pests

28. Biological Control of Plant Diseases
Biological control is not limited to insects.
Beneficial microorganisms can suppress plant pathogens.
Examples include:
- Trichoderma spp.
- Bacillus spp.
- Pseudomonas spp.
Possible mechanisms:
Competition+Antibiosis+Mycoparasitism+Predation+Induced resistance 

## 29. Antagonism
An antagonist is an organism that suppresses another organism through mechanisms such as:
- Competition
- Antibiotic production
- Parasitism
- Predation
- Resource exclusion
For example:
Trichoderma→pathogenic fungus→suppression    ## 30. Competition
Beneficial organisms can compete with pests/pathogens for:
- Food
- Space
- Nutrients
- Infection sites
- Other resources
General principle:
Beneficial organism→Resource competition→Pest/pathogen establishment↓ 

## 31. Mycoparasitism
Some beneficial fungi can attack other fungi.
Concept:
Biocontrol fungus→Pathogenic fungus→Parasitism/enzymatic attack→Pathogen suppression 
This is one mechanism associated with some Trichoderma species.

32. Herbivory as Biological Control
Biological control can also be used against weeds.
Certain insects or pathogens may specifically attack weeds.
Concept:
Biological agent→Target weed→Weed suppression 
This is called biological control of weeds.
Host specificity is particularly important because non-target plants must be protected.

33. Biological Control Agents vs Biofertilizers
These should not be confused.

Biofertilizers
Primarily improve nutrient availability or plant nutrient acquisition.
Microbe→Nutrient availability→Plant nutritionMicrobe Nutrient\ availability Plant\ nutrition

Biocontrol agents
Primarily suppress pests or pathogens.
Biocontrol agent→Pest/pathogen suppressionBiocontrol\ agent Pest/pathogen\ suppression
Some microorganisms may have multiple functions, but the purposes are conceptually different.

34. Biological Control and Host–Natural Enemy
Relationship
A simplified food chain is:
Plant→Herbivore→Predator/Parasitoid 
For a parasitoid:
Plant→Herbivore→Parasitoid 
For a microbial pathogen:

Host→Pathogen→Disease 
These interactions regulate pest populations.

35. Tritrophic Interaction
A tritrophic interaction involves three trophic levels:
Plant→Herbivore→Natural enemy 
For example:
Crop→Caterpillar→Parasitoid    The plant may influence the herbivore, and the herbivore can influence the parasitoid.

36. Natural Enemy Searching
Natural enemies need to locate their prey or hosts.
The process can involve:
Search→Detection→Recognition→Attack→Consumption/Parasitism 
They may respond to:
- Pest movement
- Plant odors
- Herbivore-induced plant volatiles
- Visual cues
- Chemical cues
- Host/prey odors

37. Pest Suppression by Predators
Predator population can influence pest mortality.
Conceptually:
Predator density↑→Predation pressure↑→Pest population↓ 

But the relationship is not always linear because predator behavior, prey density, environment and prey
defenses matter.

38. Functional Response
A predator's functional response describes how the number of prey consumed changes with prey
density.
Conceptually:
Prey density↑→Prey consumed per predator↑Prey\ density  Prey\ consumed\ per\
predator until limitations such as handling time produce saturation.
Three classical forms are:

Type I
Approximately linear at lower densities, followed by saturation.

Type II
Rapid increase followed by deceleration due to handling time.

Type III
Sigmoidal response with stronger increase at intermediate prey densities.

39. Numerical Response
A numerical response refers to changes in predator/natural-enemy population as prey availability
changes.
Prey↑→Food availability↑→Natural enemy reproduction/survival↑ 
This can increase natural-enemy abundance.

40. Predator–Pest Balance
The objective is not to eliminate every pest.
Instead:
Pest population<Economic damage level 
Natural enemies help maintain pest populations below economically important levels.

41. Predator:Pest Ratio
A simple monitoring measure is:
Predator:Pest=Number of predatorsNumber of pests  Number\ of\ pests   
For example:
20 predators and 200 pests:
Predator:Pest=20200=1:10Predator:Pest=  200 =1:10
However:
There is no universal predator:pest ratio that guarantees control.   
It depends on:
- Predator species
- Pest species
- Crop
- Crop stage
- Predator life stage
- Pest life stage
- Environment
- Predator efficiency
- Immigration
- Natural enemy diversity

42. Parasitism Percentage
For parasitoids:
Parasitism(%)=Parasitized hostsTotal hosts×100 
 Total\ hosts  100  
Example:
100 pest larvae examined and 30 are parasitized:
Parasitism=30100×100=30%Parasitism=  100  100=30\%

43. Parasitoid Emergence
Emergence(%)=Hosts producing parasitoid adultsParasitized hosts×100   Parasitized\ hosts   100  
Example:
40 parasitized hosts produce 34 parasitoid adults:
Emergence=3440×100=85%Emergence=   40  100=85\%

44. Biological Control and Pest Life Cycle
Knowledge of pest development is essential.
For example:
Egg→Larva→Pupa→AdultEgg Larva Pupa Adult
Different biological agents may attack different stages.

Egg parasitoid
Parasitoid→EggParasitoid Egg

Larval parasitoid
Parasitoid→LarvaParasitoid Larva

Predator
Predator→Egg/Larva/Nymph/AdultPredator Egg/Larva/Nymph/Adult

Entomopathogenic nematode
Often targets susceptible soil-associated stages.
Thus:
Correct agent+Correct pest stage=Better biological control opportunity 

45. Conservation of Natural Enemies
Natural enemies require suitable conditions.
Important resources include:
- Nectar

- Pollen
- Shelter
- Alternative prey
- Suitable microclimate
- Water
- Reproduction sites
Therefore:
Habitat→Natural enemy survival→Natural enemy population→Pest suppression 

46. Flowering Plants and Natural Enemies
Some adult parasitoids and predators require nectar or pollen.
Flowering plants can provide:
Nectar/Pollen→Energy→Adult longevity/fecundityNectar/Pollen  Energy  Adult\ longevity/fecundity
Therefore appropriately designed flowering habitats can support beneficial insects.
However, plant selection must consider:
- Pest attraction
- Competition
- Invasiveness
- Crop compatibility
- Local ecology

47. Pesticides and Biological Control
Pesticides can affect biological control agents through:
- Direct toxicity
- Residues
- Contaminated prey
- Habitat changes

- Reduced food availability
Therefore:
Broad−spectrumpesticide→Pest↓+Natural enemies↓ 
This may cause problems such as:
- Pest resurgence
- Secondary pest outbreaks
- Loss of natural regulation

48. Selective Chemical Use in IPM
When chemical control is necessary, IPM attempts to choose options that minimize disruption to
natural enemies where feasible.
The goal is:
Pest suppression+Natural enemy conservation 
This may involve consideration of:
- Selectivity
- Application timing
- Dose/rate according to label
- Application location
- Persistence
- Natural-enemy sensitivity

49. Biological Control and Pest Resurgence
Suppose a pesticide kills:
Pests+Natural enemiesPests+Natural\ enemies
If natural enemies recover more slowly:
Natural enemies↓Natural\ enemies and pest reproduction resumes:
Pest population↑Pest\ population Thus:
Loss of natural enemies→Reduced biological regulation→Potential pest resurgence 

50. Biological Control and Secondary Pests
A broad-spectrum intervention may suppress one pest while also removing natural enemies that
normally regulate another organism.
This can allow a secondary pest to become economically important.
Therefore:
IPM considers the entire pest−natural enemy community 

51. Biological Control Production
Commercial biological-control agents generally require:
Selection→Mass production→Quality control→Formulation→Storage→Transport→Application d  Selection  Mass\ production  Quality\ control  Formulation
 Storage  Transport  Application  
Quality is critical because biological agents are living organisms or biologically active materials.

52. Quality Control
Important considerations include:
- Correct species/strain
- Viability
- Purity
- Population/concentration
- Biological activity
- Contamination
- Shelf life
- Storage conditions
- Host specificity where relevant
A product containing the correct organism but poor viability may provide inadequate control.

53. Application of Biological Control Agents
Successful application depends on:
- Target pest
- Pest stage
- Natural enemy stage
- Temperature
- Humidity
- UV exposure
- Crop canopy
- Timing
- Compatibility with pesticides
- Application method
Therefore:
Agent+Timing+Environment+Target stage→Biological control success 

54. Environmental Factors
Biological control agents are strongly influenced by environmental conditions.
Important factors include:

Temperature
Affects:
- Development
- Reproduction
- Activity
- Pathogen growth

Humidity
Especially important for many fungal pathogens.

Rain
Can:
- Wash away agents
- Affect spores
- Change insect behavior

UV radiation
Can reduce the persistence of some microbial agents.

Wind
Can affect dispersal.
Therefore:
Biological agent×Environment→Control effectiveness 

55. Host Specificity
Some biological agents have a narrow host range.
Advantages:
High specificity→Lower non−target risk 
But it can also mean:
Narrow host range→Limited target spectrum 
This is why correct pest identification is essential.

56. Advantages of Biological Control
1. Environmentally compatible
It uses biological interactions rather than relying solely on synthetic chemicals.

2. Can be self-sustaining
Established natural enemies can reproduce.
Natural enemy→Reproduction→Next generationNatural\ enemy  Reproduction  Next\ generation

3. Can provide long-term suppression
Especially when natural enemies establish successfully.

4. Can reduce pesticide dependence
Biological control→Pest suppression→Chemical applications potentially↓Biological\ control
 Pest\ suppression  Chemical\ applications\ potentially 5. Can be selective
Some agents have narrow host ranges.

6. Resistance can be less straightforward
Pests may evolve resistance to biological agents, but the selection dynamics can differ from single-site
chemical pesticides.

7. Supports ecological pest regulation
It works with natural food webs.

57. Limitations of Biological Control
1. Often slower than chemical control
Biological control may require time for natural enemies to act.

2. Environmental dependence
Temperature, humidity and other conditions affect performance.

3. Host specificity
An agent may control only certain pests/stages.

4. Quality and storage issues
Living agents can be sensitive to poor storage.

5. Establishment may fail
Released organisms may not survive or reproduce adequately.

6. Non-target risks
Some introduced organisms can affect non-target species.

7. Requires correct identification
A biological agent must be matched to the correct target.

58. Biological Control vs Chemical Control
Biological control
Uses living organisms/biological
agents
Often slower
Environment-dependent
Can reproduce/establish
Can be highly selective
Can support natural regulation
Resistance dynamics differ
Requires biological knowledge

Chemical control
Uses chemical pesticides
Often faster
Also environment-dependent, but often more immediately
predictable
Does not reproduce
Selectivity varies by pesticide
Some products can disrupt natural enemies
Resistance is a major concern
Requires pesticide/product knowledge

59. Biological vs Mechanical Control
Biological
Mechanical
Uses living agents
Uses physical methods
Predators, parasitoids, pathogens
Traps, barriers, hand picking
Can reproduce
Does not reproduce
Often slower
Often immediate after physical removal
Environment dependent
Labor/equipment dependent
May provide long-term suppression Often requires repeated physical action

60. Biological vs Cultural Control
Cultural
Modify farming system 

Biological
Use/conserve living control agents 
They complement one another.
For example:
Crop diversification→Natural enemy habitat→Predator/parasitoid population↑→Pest suppressionCrop\
diversification  Natural\ enemy\ habitat  Predator/parasitoid\ population  Pest\ suppression

61. Biological Control and IPM
Biological control should not be treated as an isolated method.
The IPM process is:
## Identify→Monitor→Assess→Compare With Threshold→Conserve/Release N
ATURAL ENEMIES→RE−MONITOR 
If biological control is insufficient, other compatible tactics may be added.

62. Integrated Biological Control Example
Suppose a crop has aphids.

Step 1 — Identify
Crop→AphidCrop Aphid

Step 2 — Monitor
Count aphids per plant.

Step 3 — Look for natural enemies
Search for:
- Ladybird beetles
- Lacewing larvae
- Syrphid larvae
- Aphid parasitoids
- Mummies

Step 4 — Assess
Determine:
- Aphid density
- Crop stage
- Damage
- Natural enemy activity
- ETL/local recommendations

Step 5 — Conserve
Avoid unnecessary broad-spectrum pesticide use.

Step 6 — If appropriate
Use an approved biological-control approach.

Step 7 — Re-monitor
Pest↓?Natural enemies↑ 

63. Biological Control Decision Framework
Ask:

1. What is the pest?
IdentifyIdentify

2. Which life stage is present?
Egg/Larva/Nymph/Pupa/AdultEgg/Larva/Nymph/Pupa/Adult

3. Are natural enemies already present?
Predators+Parasitoids+PathogensPredators+Parasitoids+Pathogens

4. Is pest density economically important?
Pest density vs ETLPest\ density\ vs\ ETL

5. Are environmental conditions suitable?
Temperature+Humidity+Rain+Crop canopyTemperature+Humidity+Rain+Crop\ canopy

6. Can natural enemies be conserved?
7. Is augmentation appropriate?
8. Is the biological agent compatible with other IPM tactics?

64. Important Biological Control Formulas
Predator density
Predator density=Number of predatorsSampling unit  Sampling\ unit   

Predator:pest ratio
Predator:Pest=Number of predatorsNumber of pests  Number\ of\ pests   

Parasitism
Parasitism(%)=Parasitized hostsTotal hosts×100 
 Total\ hosts  100  

Parasitoid emergence
Emergence(%)=Hosts producing parasitoid adultsParasitized hosts×100   Parasitized\ hosts   100  

Pest population change
Populationnext=Populationcurrent+Births−Deaths+Immigration−Emigration  = Population  current  + Births - Deaths + Immigration - Emigration  
Biological control primarily increases the mortality component and can also affect reproduction or
dispersal.

65. Important Distinctions
Natural biological control
Naturally occurring enemies suppress pests.

Conservation biological control
Protect and enhance existing natural enemies.

Augmentative biological control
Release additional natural enemies.

Classical biological control
Introduce and establish an appropriate natural enemy, typically against an introduced pest, under
regulatory and ecological safeguards.

66. Predator, Parasitoid and Pathogen
Agent
Predator
Parasitoid

Main action
Eats prey
Develops on/in host and kills it

Example
Ladybird
Trichogramma

Agent
Main action
Example
Bacterial pathogen
Infects host
B. thuringiensis
Fungal pathogen
Infects through/into host
Beauveria
Viral pathogen
Replicates in host
NPV
Entomopathogenic nematode Infects host, often with symbiotic bacteria Steinernema

67. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1: Calling all beneficial insects biological-control agents
Not necessarily.
A honey bee is beneficial primarily as a pollinator, but it is not normally a pest-control agent.

Mistake 2: Confusing predator and parasitoid
Remember:
Predator=Many prey 
Parasitoid=Usually one host per developing individual 

Mistake 3: Assuming biological control means only insects
It also includes:
- Fungi
- Bacteria
- Viruses
- Nematodes
- Other organisms

Mistake 4: Assuming biological control always gives immediate control
Biological control is often slower than chemical control.

Mistake 5: Releasing natural enemies without identification
Wrong agent + wrong pest = poor control.

Mistake 6: Ignoring natural enemies before spraying
This can destroy valuable biological control already operating in the field.

Mistake 7: Assuming more natural enemies always means better control
Effectiveness depends on:
Agent+Pest+Environment+Timing+CropAgent+Pest+Environment+Timing+Crop

68. Master Flowchart
## Pest
↓

↓

↓

↓

↓

69. Complete Biological Control Framework
Natural control↓Conservation↓Augmentation↓Classical introduction↓Microbial control↓Integrated biol
- gical management  & \\ &  \\
& \\ &  \\ & \\ &  \\

& \\ &  \\ & \\
&  \\ &     

70. Final One-Read Summary
Biological control uses living organisms or biological processes to suppress pests.
Its major agents are:
Predators+Parasitoids+Pathogens+Other Biological Agents 
The major approaches are:
Natural+Conservation+Augmentative+Classical 
The major mechanisms are:
Predation+Parasitoidism+Pathogenicity+Competition+Antagonism 
The basic IPM sequence is:
## Identify→Monitor→Assess→Conserve/
RELEASE→SUPPRESS→RE−MONITOR 
The most important conceptual relationship is:
Natural enemy/biological agent→Pest mortality or suppression→Pest population↓→Economic damage
↓        

Ultimate memory line
Biological control means using, conserving, or enhancing living organisms and their
natural interactions to keep pest populations below economically damaging levels.
And the three most important examples to remember are:
Predator = eats the pest   
Parasitoid = develops on/in and kills the host    Pathogen = infects and causes disease   

## Module 15 — Insect & Pest
## Management
## Control Methods — Botanical
## Control
## 1. Introduction
Botanical control is a pest-management method in which plant-derived substances are used to
repel, deter, poison, inhibit feeding, disrupt development or reproduction, or otherwise suppress
pests.
The substances may come from:
- Leaves
- Seeds
- Flowers
- Bark
- Roots
- Fruits
- Oils
- Plant extracts
- Plant-derived active compounds
The basic concept is:
Plant→Bioactive compounds→Pest behavioral/physiological disruption→Pest suppression 
Botanical pesticides are often called botanicals, botanical insecticides, or plant-based pesticides,
depending on the target and formulation.

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
Botanical control is the management of pests using pest-suppressing substances
- btained from plants.

Exam-ready definition
Botanical control is a pest-management approach that uses plant-derived extracts,
- ils, powders, or active compounds to repel, deter, poison, inhibit feeding, disrupt
development or reproduction, or otherwise suppress pests.

3. Basic Principle
Plants naturally produce many secondary metabolites that protect them from herbivores and
pathogens.
Examples include:
- Alkaloids
- Terpenoids
- Limonoids
- Phenolics
- Flavonoids
- Essential oils
- Sulfur-containing compounds
These compounds can affect pests through different mechanisms.
Plant compound→Target process→Pest behavior/physiology affected→Suppression 

4. Why Plants Produce Defensive Compounds
Plants cannot move away from herbivores.
Therefore they have evolved chemical defenses.
Herbivore attack→Plant chemical defense→Feeding/survival/reproduction affected 

These natural chemical defenses form the basis of many botanical pest-control products.

5. Major Sources of Botanicals
Plant materials used for pest management can include:

Leaves
Examples:
- Neem leaves
- Chrysanthemum foliage

Seeds
Examples:
- Neem seed
- Pongamia/karanja seed

Flowers
Some plants contain insecticidal compounds concentrated in flowers.

Roots
Certain plants contain bioactive compounds in roots.

Bark
Some plant species contain defensive chemicals in bark.

Fruits
Certain plant fruits or fruit oils contain pest-active compounds.

Essential oils
Obtained from aromatic plants.

6. Important Botanical Pest-Control Plants
Important examples include:
- Neem
- Pyrethrum-producing Chrysanthemum species
- Karanja/Pongamia

- Tobacco
- Garlic
- Chilli
- Onion
- Custard apple
- Eucalyptus and other aromatic plants in some formulations
Their effectiveness and legal use vary by product, formulation, target pest and jurisdiction.

## 7. Neem
Neem is one of the most important plants associated with botanical pest management.
Scientific name:
Azadirachta indica  
Important plant parts include:
- Seeds
- Seed kernels
- Leaves
- Oil
The most famous bioactive compound is:
Azadirachtin  

## 8. Azadirachtin
Azadirachtin is a limonoid obtained primarily from neem.
It is particularly known for effects such as:
- Antifeedant activity
- Repellent activity
- Growth disruption
- Molting disruption
- Developmental effects
- Reproductive effects in some insects

It is therefore not simply a conventional "instant poison."
A simplified pathway is:
Azadirachtin→Feeding/
developmentaldisruption→Growth↓+Reproduction↓+Survival↓ 

9. Neem as an Antifeedant
An antifeedant reduces or prevents feeding.
Neem compound→Feeding behavior disrupted→Food intake↓→Crop damage↓ 
This can be particularly useful when the objective is to prevent feeding damage rather than
immediately kill every pest.

10. Neem as an Insect Growth Regulator
Some neem compounds interfere with insect development and molting.
Conceptually:
Neem→Developmental/hormonal disruption→Molting or maturation abnormal 
This can prevent insects from progressing normally through:
Larva→Pupa→AdultLarva Pupa Adult
- r:
Nymph→AdultNymph Adult
The exact effects depend on pest species, developmental stage and exposure.

11. Neem as a Repellent
Neem-derived compounds may make treated plants less attractive to certain pests.
Plant treatment→Repellent/behavioral effect→Pest colonization/feeding↓ 

12. Neem Oil
Neem oil is obtained from neem seeds/kernels.
It contains multiple plant-derived compounds, so its activity is not equivalent to a standardized pure
azadirachtin product.
Potential effects include:
- Repellency
- Antifeedant activity
- Insect growth disruption
- Some oviposition deterrence
- Some effects on pest development
Product formulation and concentration strongly influence performance.

13. Neem Extracts
Neem preparations may be made from:
- Seeds
- Kernels
- Leaves
Their composition can vary considerably depending on:
- Plant part
- Variety/genotype
- Maturity
- Extraction method
- Storage
- Solvent
- Processing
- Formulation
Therefore:
Neem extract≠Always identical chemical composition 

## 14. Pyrethrum
Pyrethrum is derived from certain Chrysanthemum species, particularly:
Chrysanthemum cinerariifolium  
The important active compounds are pyrethrins.
Pyrethrins act primarily on insect nervous systems.
General concept:
Pyrethrins→Nervous system disruption→Rapid knockdown 

15. Pyrethrins vs Pyrethroids
These terms should not be confused.

Pyrethrins
Naturally occurring insecticidal compounds from certain chrysanthemum flowers.

Pyrethroids
Synthetic chemicals designed to resemble or improve upon pyrethrin chemistry.
Therefore:
Pyrethrin≠Pyrethroid 
A product being synthetic or natural does not by itself determine its safety or environmental impact.

## 16. Tobacco
Tobacco contains nicotine, an alkaloid that has insecticidal activity.
Nicotine→Insect nervous system→Toxicity 
However, nicotine is highly toxic and tobacco-derived preparations can present significant human,
animal and environmental hazards.
Therefore unregulated homemade tobacco pesticide use should not be assumed safe.

17. Karanja / Pongamia
Karanja:

Pongamia pinnata  
is associated with plant-derived bioactive compounds and oil products.
Karanja-derived products may have:
- Insect-repellent activity
- Antifeedant effects
- Other pest-suppressing properties
Commercial formulations should be used according to their registered label.

## 18. Garlic
Garlic contains sulfur-containing compounds and volatile substances.
These may produce:
- Repellent effects
- Antifeedant effects
- Insect deterrence
- Some antimicrobial activity
Simplified:
Garlic→Volatile/sulfur compounds→Behavioral disruption→Pest pressure↓Garlic  Volatile/sulfur\ compounds  Behavioral\ disruption  Pest\ pressure ## 19. Chilli
Chilli contains capsaicinoids, particularly capsaicin.
These compounds can act as irritants or deterrents to some organisms.
Capsaicin→Irritant/deterrent effect→Feeding/interaction affected 
Effectiveness varies greatly by target pest and formulation.

20. Botanical Mode of Action
Botanicals can affect pests through multiple mechanisms.
Major mechanisms include:

## 1. Repellency
2. Antifeedant activity
3. Contact toxicity
4. Stomach toxicity
5. Fumigant action
6. Growth regulation
7. Molting disruption
8. Oviposition deterrence
9. Reproductive disruption
10.Behavioral modification
11.Enzyme/metabolic interference
12.Nervous-system effects

21. Repellent Action
A repellent makes the crop less attractive to the pest.
Botanical→Sensory/behavioral response→Pest avoids treated area 
This reduces:
- Colonization
- Feeding
- Oviposition

22. Antifeedant Action
Antifeedants reduce feeding without necessarily killing the pest immediately.
Antifeedant→Feeding↓→Plant injury↓ 
This is particularly important for botanical products such as neem-derived preparations.

23. Contact Toxicity
A botanical may enter through the insect's external body surface.
Contact→Cuticle interaction/absorption→Physiological disruption→Mortality 
Not every botanical works primarily by contact.

24. Stomach Toxicity
Some substances must be ingested.
Pest feeds→Botanical enters gut→Toxic/physiological effect 
The classic example is certain microbial toxins such as Bt, although Bt itself is generally classified as a
microbial biological-control agent rather than a botanical.

25. Fumigant Action
Some volatile plant compounds can act through the air.
Volatile botanical→Vapor→Respiratory/contact exposure→Pest effect 
Essential oils may have such activity against some pests.

26. Growth Regulation
Some botanicals interfere with insect development.
Possible effects:
- Delayed development
- Abnormal molting
- Reduced pupation
- Reduced adult emergence
- Abnormal metamorphosis
Conceptually:

Botanical→Developmental disruption→Adult emergence↓ 

27. Oviposition Deterrence
Some plant-derived compounds discourage females from laying eggs.
Botanical→Oviposition deterrence→Egg deposition↓→Future pest population↓ 
This can be especially valuable because:
Eggs↓→Larvae/Nymphs↓→Future adults↓Eggs  Larvae/Nymphs  Future\ adults 28. Reproductive Effects
Some botanicals can affect:
- Mating behavior
- Egg production
- Egg viability
- Sperm function
- Female fecundity
Therefore:
Reproduction↓→Next generation↓ 

29. Botanical Control and Pest Life Cycle
Botanicals can affect different stages:
Egg→Larva→Pupa→AdultEgg Larva Pupa Adult
- r:
Egg→Nymph→AdultEgg Nymph Adult
For example, neem-derived compounds can be particularly useful against certain immature stages
because of developmental effects.

30. Botanical Control and IPM
Botanical control fits into IPM as:
## Identify→Monitor→Assess→Threshold→Botanical Intervention→Re−Moni
TOR 
It should not automatically be applied simply because a pest is observed.

31. Botanical Control Is Not Automatically
"Harmless"
This is one of the most important concepts.
Natural≠Automatically Safe 
Plants can produce highly toxic substances.
Examples include:
- Nicotine
- Certain alkaloids
- Some essential-oil constituents
- Other potent secondary metabolites
Therefore botanical pesticides can affect:
- Humans
- Livestock
- Fish
- Pollinators
- Natural enemies
- Aquatic organisms
depending on the substance and exposure.

32. Botanical vs Synthetic Does Not Equal Safe
vs Unsafe
It is incorrect to assume:
Natural=SafeNatural=Safe
and:
Synthetic=DangerousSynthetic=Dangerous
A better principle is:
Risk=f(Hazard×Exposure) 
A highly hazardous substance may pose low practical risk under tightly controlled exposure, while a
moderately hazardous substance can become dangerous under high exposure.

33. Botanical Control and Beneficial Insects
Botanical pesticides may affect beneficial organisms.
Possible targets include:
- Predators
- Parasitoids
- Pollinators
- Soil organisms
Therefore:
Botanical≠Automatically selective 
Before application, consider:
- Target pest
- Natural enemies present
- Pollinator activity
- Application timing
- Product selectivity
- Label restrictions

34. Botanical Control and Pollinators
Special caution is needed when flowering crops or flowering weeds are present.
Pollinators may contact residues during:
- Foraging
- Pollen collection
- Nectar collection
Therefore:
Protect pollinators→Avoid unnecessary exposure 
Follow the registered product label for application timing and restrictions.

35. Botanical Control and Resistance
Botanical products can still exert selection pressure on pest populations.
Repeated exposure may select for reduced susceptibility.
Therefore:
Botanical≠Resistance proof 
Resistance-management principles still matter:
- Rotate effective modes of action where applicable
- Avoid unnecessary applications
- Use integrated methods
- Monitor control failure
- Follow label rates
- Prevent repeated selection pressure

36. Botanical Control and Pest Resistance
General evolutionary process:
Variation→Botanical selection→Less−sensitive individuals survive→Reproduction→Susceptibility shi
fts 

Therefore botanicals should be integrated with:
- Cultural control
- Mechanical control
- Biological control
- Host resistance
- Monitoring

37. Botanical Formulations
Plant-derived products can occur in different forms:
- Crude extracts
- Aqueous extracts
- Oil extracts
- Essential oils
- Powders
- Emulsions
- Standardized extracts
- Commercial formulations
- Purified active compounds
The formulation affects:
- Stability
- Solubility
- Persistence
- Application
- Efficacy
- Phytotoxicity

38. Crude Extract vs Standardized Product
Crude extract
Contains many plant compounds in variable proportions.

Standardized formulation
Designed to contain a more consistent concentration of active ingredient(s).
Therefore:
Crude botanical→Greater variability 
while:
Standardized formulation→More consistent active content 

39. Factors Affecting Botanical Efficacy
Effectiveness depends on:

Plant factors
- Species
- Variety
- Plant part
- Age
- Growth stage
- Chemical composition

Extraction factors
- Solvent
- Temperature
- Time
- Extraction technique

Product factors
- Active concentration
- Formulation
- Stability
- Storage

Pest factors
- Species

- Life stage
- Population density
- Feeding behavior

Environmental factors
- Temperature
- Rain
- Humidity
- UV radiation
- Wind
Thus:
Efficacy=f(Plant+Extraction+Formulation+Pest+Environment+Application)
 

40. UV Degradation
Many plant-derived compounds can be degraded by sunlight.
Conceptually:
UV exposure→Active compound degradation→Activity↓UV\ exposure  Active\ compound\
degradation  Activity Therefore persistence may be shorter than that of some conventional pesticides.

## 41. Rainfall
Rain can wash botanical residues from foliage.
Rain→Residue removal→Exposure↓ 
This can reduce effectiveness, although the exact effect depends on formulation and rainfall timing.

## 42. Temperature
Temperature can influence:
- Pest activity
- Plant uptake

- Volatilization
- Compound stability
- Degradation
- Botanical toxicity
Therefore:
Temperature→Botanical performance affected 

43. Botanical Control and Crop Phytotoxicity
A botanical product can sometimes injure the crop.
Possible symptoms include:
- Leaf burn
- Chlorosis
- Necrosis
- Leaf curling
- Reduced growth
Therefore:
Pest toxicity≠Crop safety 
Always follow the product's approved crop use and application rate.

44. Phytotoxicity Factors
Risk may increase with:
- Excess concentration
- Hot weather
- Sensitive crop stages
- Repeated applications
- Tank-mix incompatibility
- Poor formulation
- Certain cultivars

Thus:
Correct concentration+Correct timing+Correct crop 
are essential.

45. Botanical Extraction Concept
A simplified extraction process is:
Plant material→Drying/processing→Extraction→Filtration→Formulation→Application 
Different extraction methods recover different chemical compounds.

46. Water-Based Extract
A simplified process:
Plant material→Crushing→Water extraction→Filtration→ApplicationPlant\ material  Crushing  Water\ extraction  Filtration  Application
The resulting chemical composition can vary considerably.
Therefore homemade extracts should not be assumed to have a standardized concentration.

47. Oil-Based Botanical Products
Plant oils may contain active compounds.
Potential actions include:
- Repellency
- Suffocation/contact effects for some soft-bodied pests
- Antifeedant effects
- Interference with pest behavior
The actual mechanism depends on the oil, formulation and target pest.

48. Essential Oils
Essential oils contain volatile compounds.
Examples of aromatic plants used as sources include:
- Neem-related products
- Eucalyptus
- Mentha
- Citronella
- Clove
- Garlic
- Many aromatic plants
Possible mechanisms:
Volatile compounds→Repellency/Fumigant/Contact effects 
Their volatility can provide both an advantage and a limitation.

49. Advantages of Botanical Control
1. Plant-derived origin
They are obtained from plants or plant-derived substances.

2. Multiple biological effects
A botanical may show:
- Repellent
- Antifeedant
- Growth-regulating
- Reproductive
- Toxic effects

3. Often biodegradable
Many plant-derived compounds degrade relatively quickly, although this varies widely.

4. Can fit IPM
They can complement:
- Cultural
- Mechanical
- Biological
- Host resistance

5. Useful for resistance management when integrated appropriately
They may provide additional modes of action, although this depends on the specific compound.

6. Can be useful for small and high-value systems
Especially where targeted management is practical.

50. Limitations of Botanical Control
1. Variable composition
Especially in homemade/crude extracts.

2. Short residual activity
Some compounds degrade rapidly.

3. Environmental sensitivity
Sunlight, rain and temperature can affect efficacy.

## 4. Phytotoxicity
Some botanicals can injure crops.

5. Non-target effects
Beneficial insects and other organisms can be affected.

6. Quality-control challenges
Poor-quality extracts can give inconsistent results.

7. Large-scale production challenges
Reliable supply and standardization may be difficult for some products.

8. Not every botanical works against every pest
Plant source≠Universal pesticide 

51. Botanical vs Biological Control
This distinction is extremely important.

Botanical control
Uses:
Plant−derived chemicals 

Biological control
Uses:
Living organisms 
Example:
Neem extract→BotanicalNeem\ extract Botanical Ladybird beetle→BiologicalLadybird\
beetle Biological Trichogramma→BiologicalTrichogramma Biological
Beauveria→BiologicalBeauveria Biological

52. Botanical vs Chemical Control
Botanical control
Chemical control
Plant-derived substances
Synthetic or other pesticide active ingredients
May have multiple biological effects
May have specific or broad modes of action
Often variable in crude preparations
Usually standardized commercial formulation
Some have short persistence
Persistence varies widely
Can affect non-target organisms
Can affect non-target organisms
Can develop resistance
Can develop resistance
Requires correct identification/application Requires correct identification/application
Important: "Botanical" and "chemical" are not absolute opposites. Botanicals are themselves chemical
substances; the distinction usually refers to their biological source.

53. Botanical vs Mechanical Control
Botanical
Plant−derived chemical action 

Mechanical
Physical action 
Example:

Neem extract→BotanicalNeem\ extract Botanical Hand picking→MechanicalHand\
picking Mechanical

54. Botanical vs Cultural Control
Cultural
Modify farming practices 

Botanical
Apply plant−derived pest−suppressing substance 
They can be combined:
Crop rotation+Neem−based intervention+Natural enemy conservationCrop\ rotation + Neem-based\
intervention + Natural\ enemy\ conservation

55. Botanical Control in IPM
The correct approach is:
## Identify→Monitor→Assess→Threshold→Select Botanical If Appropriate→
APPLY CORRECTLY→RE−MONITOR 
Botanical treatment should not automatically replace:
- Monitoring
- Threshold-based decisions
- Biological control
- Cultural management

56. Example — Neem Against a Pest
Suppose a crop has a growing population of a susceptible insect pest.

Step 1 — Identify
Crop+Damage+PestCrop+Damage+Pest

Step 2 — Monitor
Determine pest density and life stage.

Step 3 — Assess
Check:
- Crop stage
- Damage
- Natural enemies
- ETL/local recommendations

Step 4 — Select appropriate product
Choose a properly labeled neem/azadirachtin product if suitable.

Step 5 — Apply according to label
Consider:
- Correct concentration
- Application timing
- Coverage
- Weather
- Pollinators
- Compatibility

Step 6 — Re-monitor
Pest population before→Pest population afterPest\ population\ before  Pest\ population\
after

57. Botanical Control and Pest Stage
The same botanical may perform differently against:
- Eggs
- Larvae
- Nymphs
- Pupae
- Adults
Therefore:
Pest stage matters 

For growth-regulating botanicals, immature stages may be particularly important.

58. Botanical Control and Spray Timing
Timing should consider:
- Pest stage
- Crop stage
- Pest density
- Weather
- Natural enemy activity
- Flowering
- Rainfall
- UV exposure
A botanical application at the wrong time can give poor control even if the product itself is effective.

59. Botanical Control and Natural Enemies
A useful IPM objective is:
Botanical pest suppression+Natural enemy conservation 
Therefore avoid assuming that botanical products can be sprayed without considering:
- Ladybirds
- Lacewings
- Parasitoids
- Bees
- Other pollinators

60. Safety of Botanical Pesticides
Always consider:

Human safety
- Skin exposure

- Eye exposure
- Inhalation
- Accidental ingestion

Animal safety
- Livestock
- Pets
- Wildlife

Environmental safety
- Fish
- Aquatic organisms
- Bees
- Natural enemies
- Soil organisms
Therefore:
Natural origin does not remove the need for PPE, label compliance, and safe handling. 

## 61. Storage
Botanical products can lose activity during storage because of:
- Heat
- Light
- Oxygen
- Moisture
- Time
Therefore:
Proper storage→Active ingredient stability 
Follow the manufacturer's storage instructions.

62. Quality Control
A good botanical product should be evaluated for:
- Correct botanical source
- Active ingredient concentration
- Purity
- Stability
- Contaminants
- Formulation quality
- Shelf life
- Biological efficacy
For standardized products:
Consistent active ingredient→More predictable performance 

63. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1 — "Botanical means harmless."
Incorrect.
Natural≠Harmless 

Mistake 2 — "Neem kills every insect."
Incorrect.
Neem effects depend on:
- Pest species
- Life stage
- Concentration
- Formulation
- Timing

Mistake 3 — "Botanicals cannot cause resistance."
Incorrect.

Any biologically active substance that repeatedly selects pest populations can contribute to reduced
susceptibility.

Mistake 4 — "More concentration means better control."
Not necessarily.
Higher concentration can increase:
- Phytotoxicity
- Non-target effects
- Cost
- Residue/exposure
Use the registered label.

Mistake 5 — Confusing botanical and biological control.
Botanical=Plant−derived substance 
Biological=Living organism/biological agent 

Mistake 6 — Ignoring weather.
Rain and sunlight can strongly affect some botanical products.

64. Master Classification
BOTANICAL CONTROL   

Plant sources
Leaves+Seeds+Roots+Bark+Flowers+Fruits+OilsLeaves+Seeds+Roots+Bark+Flowers+Fruits+Oils
↓

Plant-derived compounds
Alkaloids+Terpenoids+Limonoids+Phenolics+Essential oilsAlkaloids+Terpenoids+Limonoids+Phenoli
cs+Essential\ oils
↓

Biological effects
Repellent+Antifeedant+Toxic+Growth disruption+Oviposition deterrence+Reproductive disruptionRep
ellent + Antifeedant + Toxic + Growth\ disruption + Oviposition\ deterrence + Reproductive\ disruption

↓

Outcome
Pest population↓→Crop damage↓ 

65. Important Examples to Remember
Botanical source Important compound/group
Major known effect
Neem
Azadirachtin/limonoids
Antifeedant, growth/development disruption
Pyrethrum
Pyrethrins
Rapid insecticidal/knockdown activity
Tobacco
Nicotine
Neurotoxic insecticidal activity
Karanja
Plant bioactive compounds/oil Repellent/antifeedant activity reported
Garlic
Sulfur compounds
Repellent/deterrent effects
Chilli
Capsaicinoids
Irritant/deterrent effects
Aromatic plants Essential oils
Repellent/fumigant/contact effects in some systems
Exact efficacy depends on product, pest, formulation and conditions.

66. Comparison of Pest-Control Methods
Method
Main principle
Example
Cultural
Change crop environment Crop rotation
Mechanical Physical action
Hand picking
Biological Living control agents
Ladybird
Botanical Plant-derived chemicals Neem
Chemical Pesticide active ingredient Registered insecticide
Memory:
Cultural=Change 
Mechanical=Physically remove 
Biological=Living agent  Botanical=Plant−derived chemical 
Chemical=Pesticide active ingredient 

67. Exam-Ready Short Answer
Botanical control is the management of agricultural pests using plant-derived
extracts, oils, powders or active compounds. Botanicals may act as repellents,
antifeedants, contact or stomach toxicants, fumigants, growth regulators, oviposition
deterrents or reproductive inhibitors. Important examples include neem, pyrethrum,
tobacco, karanja, garlic and other plant-derived products. Their effectiveness depends

- n the pest, life stage, active compound, formulation, dose, timing and environmental
conditions. Botanical products should be integrated with IPM and handled according
to approved safety and label requirements.

68. Final One-Read Summary
The complete mechanism is:
Plant→Extraction/processing→Bioactive compound→Pest exposure→Behavioral/physiological disrup
tion→Pest suppression 
Major effects:
Repellency+Antifeedant+Toxicity+Growth disruption+Oviposition deterrence+Reproductive disruption
 
Major examples:
Neem→Azadirachtin 
Pyrethrum→Pyrethrins 
Tobacco→Nicotine 
Karanja→Plant bioactive compounds 

Most important conceptual distinction
Botanical control = plant-derived substances used to suppress pests.   
It is not automatically harmless, not automatically residue-free, not automatically selective, and
not automatically free from resistance or non-target effects.

Ultimate memory line
Botanical control uses the natural chemical defenses of plants as pest-management
tools, with neem/azadirachtin being one of the most important examples, while
successful use depends on correct pest identification, appropriate
product/formulation, timing, environmental conditions, crop safety, natural-enemy
conservation and proper safety practices.

## Module 15 — Insect & Pest
## Management
## Control Methods — Chemical
## Control
## 1. Introduction
Chemical control is a pest-management method in which pesticide chemicals are used to prevent,
suppress, repel, regulate, or kill agricultural pests.
It is one component of Integrated Pest Management (IPM) and should be used based on:
Correct identification→Monitoring→Threshold−based decision→Correct pesticide→Correct applicati
- n→Re−monitoring 
Chemical control can provide relatively rapid pest suppression, but inappropriate or excessive use can
cause:
- Pesticide resistance
- Pest resurgence
- Secondary pest outbreaks
- Harm to beneficial organisms
- Residues
- Environmental contamination
- Human and animal exposure

## 2. Definition
Simple definition
Chemical control is the management of agricultural pests using chemical pesticides.

Exam-ready definition
Chemical control is the use of appropriately selected and applied chemical pesticides
to suppress pest populations and prevent economically unacceptable crop damage.

3. Basic Principle
A pesticide interacts with a pest through a particular mode of action (MOA).
Pesticide→Exposure→Absorption→Movement→Target site→Physiological disruption→Pest death/
suppression 
The exact pathway depends on the pesticide.

4. What Is a Pesticide?
A pesticide is a substance or mixture intended to prevent, destroy, repel, or control pests or regulate
unwanted organisms.
The term is broader than "insecticide."
Major categories include:
Pesticide
Main target
Insecticide Insects
Acaricide
Mites/ticks
Herbicide
Weeds
Fungicide
Fungi/fungal diseases
Bactericide Bacteria
Nematicide Nematodes
Rodenticide Rodents
Molluscicide Molluscs
Avicide
Birds in specific regulated applications
Fumigant
Pests through toxic gases/vapors

## 5. Insecticide
An insecticide is a pesticide specifically used to control insects.
Examples of insecticide target situations include:
- Aphids
- Whiteflies
- Thrips
- Caterpillars
- Beetles

- Borers
- Leafhoppers
- Bugs
The correct pesticide depends on:
Pest species+Life stage+Crop+Location+Resistance status 

6. Chemical Control in IPM
Chemical control should generally be considered one tool among several.
The IPM framework is:
## Identify→Monitor→Assess→Threshold→Select Management→Apply→Re−
MONITOR 
Chemical intervention is considered when other available measures are insufficient or when the pest
situation justifies it.

7. Why Chemical Control Is Used
Chemical pesticides can provide:
- Rapid pest suppression
- Effective control during severe outbreaks
- Control of pests difficult to remove mechanically
- Protection of high-value crops
- Management of pests in inaccessible locations
- Protection during critical crop stages
- Targeted treatment when properly selected
However:
Fast control≠Automatically best control 
The method must be economically and environmentally appropriate.

8. Classification by Target
Insecticides
Control insects.

Acaricides
Control mites.

Herbicides
Control weeds.

Fungicides
Control fungal pathogens.

Nematicides
Control plant-parasitic nematodes.

Rodenticides
Control rodents.

Molluscicides
Control snails and slugs.

9. Classification by Source
Chemical pesticides may broadly be:

Inorganic
Examples historically include certain compounds containing:
- Sulfur
- Copper
- Other inorganic elements

Organic synthetic
Many modern pesticides belong to synthetic organic chemical groups.

Natural-product-derived
Some pesticide active ingredients originate from natural substances but may be chemically modified or
formulated.

Therefore:
Natural origin and synthetic origin are not the same as safe and unsafe. 

10. Classification by Mode of Entry
Pesticides can be classified according to how they reach the target.

Contact action
The pesticide acts after contacting the pest.
Pesticide→Pest surface→Target effectPesticide  Pest\ surface  Target\ effect

Stomach action
The pest must ingest the pesticide.
Pesticide→Feeding→Gut→Toxic effectPesticide  Feeding  Gut  Toxic\ effect

Fumigant action
The pesticide enters as a gas or vapor.
Pesticide→Vapor→Pest exposure→ToxicityPesticide  Vapor  Pest\ exposure
 Toxicity

Systemic action
The pesticide is absorbed and moves within the plant or other treated system, depending on the product.
Plant uptake→Internal movement→Feeding pest→ExposurePlant\ uptake  Internal\
movement  Feeding\ pest  Exposure

11. Contact Insecticides
A contact insecticide can affect insects after contact with the pesticide.
Potential exposure routes include:
- Body surface
- Treated plant surface
- Spray droplets
- Residues

General mechanism:
Contact→Absorption→Target site→Toxic effect 
Good spray coverage can be important for contact products, depending on their label and mode of
action.

12. Stomach Insecticides
The insect must consume the treated material.
Treated plant→Pest feeding→Ingestion→Toxic effect 
They can be particularly relevant for chewing insects.

13. Systemic Insecticides
A systemic insecticide is absorbed and translocated within the plant to some extent, depending on its
chemistry.
General concept:
Application→Plant uptake→Translocation→Pest feeding→Exposure 
They can be useful where pests feed in locations difficult to reach with direct spray.
Systemicity is product-specific, not a property of all pesticides.

## 14. Fumigants
Fumigants produce a toxic gas or vapor that reaches pests through the surrounding atmosphere.
They may be used in specialized situations such as:
- Certain stored products
- Soil treatment
- Enclosed structures
Because of their hazards, fumigants require particularly strict:
- Label compliance
- Training

- PPE
- Ventilation/exclusion procedures
- Regulatory compliance

15. Classification by Selectivity
Selective pesticide
Designed or used in a way that targets particular pests while having comparatively less effect on certain
non-target organisms.

Broad-spectrum pesticide
Can affect a wide range of organisms.
Conceptually:
Broad spectrum→Pests↓+Natural enemies↓Broad\ spectrum  Pests + Natural\
enemies Therefore selective options can be valuable in IPM when suitable.

## 16. Selectivity
Chemical selectivity can result from:
1. Target-site differences
2. Metabolic differences
3. Application placement
## 4. Timing
## 5. Dose
6. Crop tolerance
7. Pest physiology
Conceptually:
Weed/pest sensitivity>Crop sensitivity 
for a selective treatment.
Selectivity is often condition-dependent.

17. Mode of Action (MOA)
Mode of action describes how a pesticide affects a biological target.
Examples of insecticide target systems include:
- Nervous system
- Muscle function
- Energy production
- Lipid synthesis
- Growth/development
- Respiration
- Feeding processes
For herbicides, target systems include:
- Amino-acid synthesis
- Photosynthesis
- Lipid synthesis
- Cell division
- Pigment formation
- Plant hormone pathways
For fungicides, target systems can include:
- Sterol biosynthesis
- Respiration
- Cell division
- Nucleic-acid synthesis
- Protein synthesis

18. Mode of Action vs Chemical Class
These are related but not identical.

Chemical class
Groups compounds based on chemical structure.

Mode of action
Groups pesticides based on biological target/process affected.
Therefore:
Chemical class≠MOA 
For resistance management, MOA grouping is particularly important.

19. Insecticide Action on the Nervous System
Many insecticides affect neural signaling.
Possible targets include:
- Sodium channels
- Acetylcholinesterase
- Nicotinic acetylcholine receptors
- GABA-related signaling
- Other neural targets
General pathway:
Pesticide→Neural target→Abnormal nerve signaling→Paralysis→DeathPesticide  Neural\
target  Abnormal\ nerve\ signaling  Paralysis  Death

20. Acetylcholinesterase Inhibition
Some insecticides inhibit acetylcholinesterase (AChE).
Normally:
Acetylcholine→AChEBreakdownAcetylcholine   Breakdown
If AChE is inhibited:
AChE inhibition→Acetylcholine accumulation→Continuous neural stimulation→Tremors/
paralysis→DeathAChE\ inhibition  Acetylcholine\ accumulation  Continuous\
neural\ stimulation  Tremors/paralysis  Death
This is a major example of a pesticide mode of action.

21. Sodium Channel Effects
Some insecticides interfere with voltage-gated sodium channels.

Normal:
Nerve impulse→Controlled sodium channel activityNerve\ impulse  Controlled\ sodium\
channel\ activity
Disruption:
Pesticide→Sodium channel dysfunction→Abnormal nerve firing→Knockdown/paralysisPesticide
 Sodium\ channel\ dysfunction  Abnormal\ nerve\ firing  Knockdown/paralysis

22. Growth-Regulating Insecticides
Some insecticides interfere with insect development rather than causing immediate poisoning.
Possible effects:
- Abnormal molting
- Failure to develop
- Abnormal metamorphosis
- Reduced adult emergence
- Reduced reproduction
Growth regulator→Development disruption→Population suppression 

23. Herbicide Mode of Action
Herbicides can interfere with critical plant processes.
Examples include inhibition of:
- ALS
- ACCase
## • Epsps
- PPO
- Photosystem II
- Glutamine synthetase
General concept:

Herbicide→Plant biochemical target→Essential process disrupted→Plant injury→DeathHerbicide
 Plant\ biochemical\ target  Essential\ process\ disrupted  Plant\ injury
 Death

24. Fungicide Mode of Action
Fungicides may disrupt:
- Cell membrane formation
- Respiration
- Cell division
- Protein synthesis
- Nucleic acid synthesis
- Spore germination
Concept:
Fungicide→Fungal target→Essential process disrupted→Disease development↓Fungicide  Fungal\ target  Essential\ process\ disrupted  Disease\ development 25. Preventive vs Curative Use
Preventive
Applied before significant infection or infestation becomes established, where the product is labeled for
preventive use.

Curative
Used after infection/infestation has begun, where the product has appropriate curative activity.
Not every pesticide has curative activity.
Preventive≠Curative 

26. Pre-Emergence and Post-Emergence
Herbicides
Pre-emergence
Applied before weeds emerge, depending on the product and crop.

Soil→Germinating weed→Herbicide exposure→ControlSoil  Germinating\ weed
 Herbicide\ exposure  Control

Post-emergence
Applied after weeds emerge.
Emerging weed→Foliar application→Absorption→ControlEmerging\ weed  Foliar\
application  Absorption  Control

27. Pesticide Formulation
The active ingredient is often formulated into a usable product.
A formulation may contain:
- Active ingredient
- Solvents/carriers
- Surfactants
- Stabilizers
- Adjuvants
- Other formulation components
The formulation affects:
- Mixing
- Stability
- Application
- Coverage
- Storage
- Absorption

28. Active Ingredient
The active ingredient (AI) is the component responsible for the pesticide's biological activity.
A product may contain:
Active ingredient+Formulation ingredientsActive\ ingredient+Formulation\ ingredients
The label specifies the concentration and approved uses.

29. Common Formulation Types
Depending on the pesticide, formulations can include:
- EC — Emulsifiable concentrate
- SC — Suspension concentrate
- WP — Wettable powder
- WG/WDG — Water-dispersible granules
- SL — Soluble liquid/concentrate
- GR — Granules
- CS — Capsule suspension
The exact meaning and regulatory classification should be taken from the product label.

## 30. Adjuvants
Adjuvants are substances added to improve pesticide performance or application characteristics.
They may improve:
- Wetting
- Spreading
- Sticking
- Penetration
- Droplet behavior
- Compatibility
But:
Adjuvant≠Automatically necessary 
Only use compatible, label-permitted adjuvants.

31. Chemical Control and Pest Stage
Pesticide effectiveness often depends strongly on pest life stage.
For example:
- Eggs may be difficult to control.

- Small larvae may be more susceptible to some products.
- Older larvae may be more difficult to control.
- Protected pupae may escape foliar sprays.
- Adults may respond differently from immature stages.
Therefore:
Correct pesticide+Correct pest stage 
is important.

32. Spray Timing
Timing depends on:
- Pest stage
- Pest density
- Crop stage
- Weather
- Natural enemies
- Product MOA
- Product residual period
- Rainfall
- Wind
- Temperature
Good timing can improve:
Efficacy↑Efficacy and reduce:
Unnecessary exposure↓Unnecessary\ exposure 33. Weather and Chemical Control
Important factors include:

Rain
Can wash pesticides from foliage.

Wind
Can cause drift.

Temperature
Can affect:
- Volatilization
- Pest activity
- Crop injury
- Pesticide degradation

Humidity
Can influence spray behavior and some pest/pathogen interactions.

Sunlight
Can degrade some pesticides.
Thus:
Weather→Pesticide performance+Safety 
Always follow product-specific weather restrictions.

34. Spray Coverage
Coverage is especially important for contact pesticides.
Conceptually:
Better target coverage→Greater probability of exposure 
But "more spray" does not necessarily mean "better."
Excessive application can increase:
- Cost
- Residues
- Drift
- Environmental contamination
- Crop injury

## 35. Calibration
Spraying equipment must be calibrated so the intended application rate is delivered.
General principle:
Correct dose=Correct concentration×Correct application volume 
Calibration considers:
- Nozzle type
- Pressure
- Travel speed
- Spray width
- Flow rate
- Tank volume

36. Chemical Dose and Concentration
These concepts are different.

Concentration
Amount of active ingredient per unit volume of spray mixture.

Dose/rate
Amount applied per unit area or target.
For example:
Rate=Amount of productAreaRate=  Area 
Always use the label-specified rate rather than improvising.

37. Pesticide Resistance
One of the biggest problems of chemical control is resistance.
Pesticide resistance is the inherited ability of a pest population to survive a pesticide exposure that
would normally control a susceptible population.
General evolution:

Variation→Pesticide selection→Survivors→Reproduction→Resistance frequency↑ 

38. Why Resistance Develops
Risk increases with practices such as:
- Repeated use of the same MOA
- Excessive applications
- Suboptimal control
- Poor timing
- Repeated exposure across generations
- Failure to integrate non-chemical methods

39. Resistance Management
A key principle is:
Rotate MOAs 
not merely:
Rotate brands 
Two different brand names may contain active ingredients with the same MOA.
Other strategies:
- Monitor pest populations
- Use thresholds
- Avoid unnecessary treatments
- Use effective registered rates
- Combine compatible IPM methods
- Avoid repeated use of one MOA
- Prevent survivors from producing large subsequent generations

## 40. Cross-Resistance
Cross-resistance occurs when resistance to one pesticide provides resistance to another pesticide, often
because they share a similar target or resistance mechanism.
Pesticide A→Resistance→Reduced sensitivity to BPesticide\ A  Resistance  Reduced\ sensitivity\ to\ B
when A and B are affected by the same resistance mechanism.

41. Multiple Resistance
Multiple resistance occurs when a pest population has resistance to pesticides from multiple unrelated
groups/MOAs.
Resistance to A+B+C 
This makes chemical management much more difficult.

42. Pesticide Resistance vs Pest Tolerance
Tolerance
Natural ability of a species/population to withstand a pesticide without having evolved resistance
through selection.

Resistance
An inherited reduction in susceptibility that has developed/been selected within a population.
Tolerance≠Resistance 

43. Pest Resurgence
Pest resurgence can occur when pesticide treatment reduces natural enemies along with the target pest.
Pesticide→Pest↓+Natural enemies↓Pesticide  Pest + Natural\
enemies Afterward:
Natural enemies↓→Pest recovery↑Natural\ enemies  Pest\ recovery Potentially:
Pest population→Above pre−treatment level 

44. Secondary Pest Outbreak
Chemical treatment may suppress one pest while natural enemies controlling another pest are also
reduced.
Then:
Natural enemy loss→Secondary pest increase 
This is why pest-management decisions should consider the whole crop ecosystem.

45. Pesticide Residues
A pesticide residue is pesticide-related material remaining on or in agricultural commodities or the
environment after application.
Residues depend on:
- Active ingredient
- Rate
- Application timing
- Number of applications
- Crop
- Environmental degradation
- Pre-harvest interval

46. Pre-Harvest Interval (PHI)
The pre-harvest interval is the minimum time that must pass between the final pesticide application
and harvest, according to the approved product label.
Application→PHI→Harvest 
Following the PHI is essential for residue management.

47. Re-Entry Interval
A re-entry interval (REI) is the period specified on a pesticide label during which workers should not
enter a treated area without following the required precautions.

Application→REI→Safe entry according to label 
PHI and REI are different.

48. PHI vs REI
PHI
REI
Concerned mainly with harvest
Concerned with worker entry
Application → harvest
Application → re-entry
Helps manage residues on harvested produce Helps protect workers

49. Environmental Effects
Improper pesticide use can contaminate:
- Soil
- Surface water
- Groundwater
- Air
- Sediments
Potential pathways:
Application→DriftApplication  Drift Application→RunoffApplication  Runoff
Application→LeachingApplication  Leaching Application→VolatilizationApplication
 Volatilization

50. Pesticide Drift
Drift occurs when pesticide droplets, vapors or particles move away from the intended application
area.
Application→Wind/other transport→Off−target movement 
Drift can affect:
- Neighboring crops
- Natural vegetation
- Pollinators

- Water bodies
- People
- Livestock

## 51. Runoff
Rain or irrigation can move pesticides from treated fields.
Field→Runoff→Drainage/waterway 
Risk depends on:
- Soil
- Slope
- Rainfall
- Application timing
- Chemical properties
- Vegetation
- Buffer areas

## 52. Leaching
Leaching is downward movement through soil with water.
Soil surface→Water movement→Lower soil→Potential groundwater 
The risk depends on pesticide properties and soil/environmental conditions.

## 53. Volatilization
Some pesticides can move from a liquid/solid phase into the atmosphere.
Pesticide→Vapor→Atmospheric movement 
Temperature, formulation, wind and chemical properties influence volatilization.

54. Effects on Beneficial Organisms
Chemical pesticides can affect:
- Predators
- Parasitoids
- Pollinators
- Earthworms
- Soil microorganisms
- Aquatic organisms
Therefore:
Pest control≠Only target pest 
The entire agroecosystem should be considered.

55. Chemical Control and Pollinators
Special care is needed around flowering crops.
Risk can result from:
- Direct spray
- Residues
- Contaminated nectar/pollen
- Drift
Therefore:
Protect pollinators→Follow label restrictions+Avoid unnecessary exposure 

56. Human Health and Chemical Control
Pesticide exposure can occur through:
## 1. Skin
## 2. Eyes
## 3. Inhalation
## 4. Ingestion
Therefore:
Exposure prevention>Treatment after exposure 

57. Personal Protective Equipment
PPE depends on the pesticide label and hazard classification.
Potential PPE includes:
- Chemical-resistant gloves
- Protective clothing
- Eye protection
- Appropriate footwear
- Respiratory protection where specifically required
The label/SDS determines what is required.

58. Safe Mixing and Loading
Important principles:
- Read the label first.
- Use the required PPE.
- Avoid skin contact.
- Avoid splashing.
- Do not eat, drink or smoke.
- Use appropriate measuring equipment.
- Do not use household utensils.
- Keep unauthorized people away.
- Avoid mixing near water bodies.
- Follow the product's mixing order and compatibility instructions.

## 59. Storage
Pesticides should be:

- Kept in original labeled containers
- Stored securely
- Kept away from food and animal feed
- Kept away from seed
- Protected from inappropriate heat/light
- Stored according to label requirements
- Kept inaccessible to children and unauthorized people
Never transfer pesticide into a food or beverage container.

## 60. Disposal
Never dispose of pesticides by:
- Pouring into drains
- Dumping into ponds
- Dumping into rivers
- Throwing into fields
- Burning containers unless specifically permitted and instructed
Follow:
Label+Local regulations+Approved disposal procedure 

61. Chemical Compatibility
Mixing pesticides can create problems.
Potential issues include:
- Precipitation
- Reduced efficacy
- Crop injury
- Increased toxicity
- Formulation instability
Therefore:

Compatibility must be verified before tank mixing 

62. Tank Mixing
A tank mixture combines multiple products in one spray tank.
Potential reasons include:
- Multiple target pests
- Multiple disease targets
- Resistance management
- Operational efficiency
But tank mixing should only be done when:
- Labels permit it
- Products are physically compatible
- Crops are tolerant
- Application is agronomically justified

63. Chemical Control and 4R Principle
A useful framework is:
Right Product+Right Rate+Right Time+Right Place 

Right product
Correct target and registered use.

Right rate
Label-approved rate.

Right time
Correct pest/crop/weather stage.

Right place
Correct target and application location.

64. Chemical Control and Thresholds
Chemical treatment should ideally be connected to pest monitoring.
Pest density<ETL→Usually continue monitoring  Pest density≥ETL→Evaluate management 
The exact threshold depends on crop, pest, location, crop stage and locally validated recommendations.

65. EIL and Chemical Control
Economic Injury Level:
EIL=CV×I×D×K  V I D K   
where:
- CC = cost of control
- VV = crop value
- II = injury per pest
- DD = damage per unit injury
- KK = proportion of pest controlled
And:
ETL<EIL 
Chemical control is generally considered before economic injury becomes unacceptable when the
threshold-based system indicates action.

66. Chemical Control Decision Example
Suppose aphids are found.

Do not immediately spray.
First:
Crop→Identify aphid→Count aphidsCrop Identify\ aphid Count\ aphids
Then assess:
- Crop stage
- Damage

- Natural enemies
- Weather
- ETL/local recommendations
If action is justified:
Select registered product→Check MOA→Check label→Apply correctly→Re−monitorSelect\
registered\ product  Check\ MOA  Check\ label  Apply\ correctly
 Re-monitor

67. Chemical Control and Biological Control
Chemical and biological control can sometimes be compatible.
The goal is:
Chemical pest suppression+Natural enemy conservation 
This requires consideration of:
- Pesticide selectivity
- Timing
- Persistence
- Natural-enemy sensitivity
- Application location

68. Advantages of Chemical Control
1. Rapid action
Many pesticides can suppress pests relatively quickly.

2. Effective during outbreaks
Useful when pest populations rise rapidly.

3. Large-area applicability
Mechanized spraying can treat large areas efficiently.

4. Target-specific products exist
Some pesticides have relatively specific targets.

5. Multiple formulations
Products can be adapted to different application systems.

6. Useful for difficult-to-reach pests
Systemic and soil-applied products can reach pests in protected locations when labeled for such use.

69. Limitations of Chemical Control
## 1. Resistance
Repeated selection can produce resistant pest populations.

2. Non-target effects
Beneficial insects and other organisms may be affected.

## 3. Residues
Improper use can cause unacceptable residues.

4. Environmental contamination
Runoff, leaching and drift can occur.

5. Pest resurgence
Natural enemies may be disrupted.

6. Secondary pest outbreaks
Natural pest regulation can be disturbed.

7. Human health risks
Improper exposure can cause poisoning or other health effects.

## 8. Cost
Repeated applications increase production costs.

70. Chemical Control vs Other Control Methods
Method
Main principle
Cultural
Modify crop environment
Mechanical Physical removal
Biological Living organisms
Botanical Plant-derived chemicals

Example
Crop rotation
Hand picking
Ladybird
Neem

Method
Main principle
Example
Chemical Pesticide active ingredients Registered insecticide
The strongest IPM system combines compatible methods rather than depending permanently on one
method.

71. Common Mistakes
Mistake 1: Spraying whenever pests are seen
Presence does not automatically mean economic damage.
Pest presence≠Automatic treatment 

Mistake 2: Increasing the dose for faster control
Higher dose can increase:
- Crop injury
- Residues
- Environmental risk
- Selection pressure
Use the approved label rate.

Mistake 3: Rotating brands instead of MOAs
Different brands may contain the same or similar MOA.
Rotate MOA, not just brand names 

Mistake 4: Ignoring pest life stage
A pesticide may work much better against one stage than another.

Mistake 5: Ignoring weather
Rain, wind, temperature and sunlight can strongly affect performance and safety.

Mistake 6: Assuming systemic means permanent protection
Systemic activity does not mean unlimited or permanent pest protection.

Mistake 7: Ignoring natural enemies
Chemical treatment can remove beneficial predators and parasitoids.

74. Important Formulas
