75. Exam-Ready Short Answer
Chemical control is the management of agricultural pests through the use of chemical
pesticides. Pesticides can be classified according to target organism, mode of entry,
selectivity, formulation and mode of action. They may act through contact, ingestion,

fumigation or systemic exposure and can affect processes such as nervous-system
function, growth, respiration, photosynthesis or other essential biochemical pathways.
Chemical control is an important component of IPM but should be based on pest
identification, monitoring and economic thresholds. Correct product selection, dose,
timing, application method, PPE, resistance management, PHI and environmental
precautions are essential.

76. Final One-Read Summary
The complete concept is:
Identify→Monitor→Threshold→Select pesticide→Select MOA→Apply correctly→Re−monitor d  Identify  Monitor  Threshold  Select\ pesticide  Select\
MOA  Apply\ correctly  Re-monitor  
Chemical pesticides can enter or affect pests through:
Contact+Stomach+Systemic+Fumigant 
They can act on:
Nervous system+Growth+Respiration+Photosynthesis+Other essential biochemical processes 
The major risks are:
Resistance+Residues+Drift+Runoff+Leaching+Non−target effects+Resurgence+Human exposure d  Resistance+Residues+Drift+Runoff+Leaching+ Non-target\ effects+Resurgence+Human\
exposure  
The key resistance principle is:
Rotate effective modes of action, not merely pesticide brands.   
The key safety principle is:
Follow the registered label, including rate, crop, target, PPE, PHI, REI and application restrictions.
   
And the most important IPM principle is:
Chemical control is a tool for pest management—not a substitute for identification, monitoring and inte
grated management.   

## Module 15 — Insect & Pest
## Management
## Integrated Pest Management (Ipm)
## 1. Introduction
Integrated Pest Management (IPM) is a comprehensive, decision-based approach to managing
agricultural pests by combining multiple compatible pest-management methods while considering
pest biology, crop health, economics, environmental conditions, and human safety.
The central idea is not to eliminate every pest. Instead, the objective is to keep pest populations below
economically damaging levels while minimizing unnecessary pesticide use and protecting beneficial
- rganisms and the environment.
IPM=Prevention+Monitoring+Decision making+Compatible Control Methods+Evaluation 
The fundamental decision cycle is:
IDENTIFY→MONITOR→ASSESS→DECIDE→MANAGE→RE−MONITOR 
2. Definition of IPM
Simple definition
Integrated Pest Management is the coordinated use of different pest-management
methods to keep pest populations below economically damaging levels with minimum
risk to people and the environment.

Exam-ready definition
Integrated Pest Management is an ecosystem-based, decision-oriented approach that
integrates compatible cultural, mechanical, biological, botanical, chemical, genetic
and other pest-management techniques to maintain pest populations below
economically unacceptable levels while minimizing risks to human health, beneficial
- rganisms and the environment.

3. Why IPM Is Needed
Depending entirely on one control method can create problems.

For example:
Repeated pesticide use→ResistanceRepeated\ pesticide\ use  Resistance
- r:
Broad−spectrum pesticide→Natural enemies↓→Pest resurgenceBroad-spectrum\ pesticide  Natural\ enemies  Pest\ resurgence
- r:
Excessive pesticide→Residues+Environmental contaminationExcessive\ pesticide  Residues+Environmental\ contamination
IPM attempts to avoid these problems through diversification and decision-based management.

4. Central Philosophy of IPM
The central principle is:
Manage pests, not necessarily eliminate pests.   
A crop can tolerate some level of pest population without economically important yield loss.
Therefore:
Pest presence≠Automatic need for control 
The important question is:
Is the pest population likely to cause economically unacceptable damage, and what is
the least disruptive effective management option?

5. IPM and Economic Threshold
Two important concepts are:

Economic Injury Level — EIL
The lowest pest population density at which the economic value of crop loss equals the cost of control.
EIL=CV×I×D×K  V I D K   

Economic Threshold Level — ETL
The pest population level at which management action should begin to prevent the pest from reaching
the EIL.
ETL<EIL 

Thus:
Monitor→Compare with threshold→Decide 

6. Main Objectives of IPM
IPM aims to:
1. Prevent pest problems.
2. Detect pests early.
3. Monitor pest populations.
4. Identify pests correctly.
5. Understand pest biology.
6. Protect crop yield and quality.
7. Maintain pests below economically damaging levels.
8. Conserve natural enemies.
9. Reduce unnecessary pesticide use.
10.Delay pesticide resistance.
11.Reduce pesticide residues.
12.Protect pollinators.
13.Reduce environmental contamination.
14.Improve economic efficiency.
15.Maintain long-term agricultural sustainability.

7. Core Components of IPM
A useful IPM framework is:
1. Prevention→2. Identification→3. Monitoring→4. Decision making→5. Management→6. Evaluatio
n 

8. Component 1 — Prevention
The first principle is:

Prevent pest problems before they become economically important.
Prevention can include:
- Healthy planting material
- Resistant varieties
- Crop rotation
- Field sanitation
- Removal of infested residues
- Weed management
- Proper planting time
- Proper plant spacing
- Balanced nutrition
- Efficient irrigation
- Clean farm equipment
- Pest-free seed
- Quarantine measures
Conceptually:
Prevention→Pest establishment↓ 

9. Component 2 — Correct Pest Identification
Before deciding on a control method, determine:
- What organism is present?
- Which species/group?
- Which life stage?
- What crop is affected?
- Which plant part is affected?
- What type of damage is present?
The diagnostic chain is:
Crop→Damage→Plant part→Pest→Life stage→Confirmation 

10. Why Identification Is Critical
Different pests may produce similar symptoms.
For example:
Leaf yellowingLeaf\ yellowing
could result from:
- Insect feeding
- Nutrient deficiency
- Disease
- Drought
- Waterlogging
- Herbicide injury
Therefore:
Wrong identification→Wrong management→Wasted money+Potential crop/environmental damage xed  Wrong\ identification  Wrong\ management  Wasted\ money+Potential\
crop/environmental\ damage  

11. Component 3 — Monitoring
Monitoring means regularly observing and measuring pest populations, damage and natural enemies.
Monitoring answers:
- Is the pest present?
- How many are present?
- Where are they located?
- Are numbers increasing?
- What life stage is dominant?
- Is crop damage increasing?
- Are natural enemies present?

12. Pest Monitoring Methods
Visual inspection
Inspect:
- Leaves
- Stems
- Shoots
- Flowers
- Fruits
- Roots
- Soil
- Plant residues

Sweep net
Useful for sampling mobile insects in suitable crops.

Beat sampling
Plants are disturbed over a sheet/tray and insects are counted.

Sticky traps
Useful for monitoring certain flying insects.

Pheromone traps
Used for monitoring specific pest species and their activity.
Trap catch=Number capturedTrap×TimeTrap\ catch =   Trap Time 

Light traps
Can monitor certain nocturnal insects.

Soil sampling
Useful for soil-dwelling pests.

13. Monitoring Natural Enemies
IPM does not monitor only pests.
It also monitors:

- Predators
- Parasitoids
- Pathogens
- Other beneficial organisms
For example:
Aphids↑Aphids but simultaneously:
Ladybird larvae↑Ladybird\ larvae and:
Parasitoid mummies↑Parasitoid\ mummies The pest population may therefore be naturally regulated.

14. Component 4 — Decision Making
Monitoring data must be converted into a management decision.
The basic sequence is:
Pest count→Damage→Crop stage→Natural enemies→ETL/EIL→Decision 
Possible decisions:

No action
Continue monitoring.

Preventive/cultural action
Modify crop management.

Mechanical action
Physically remove or suppress pests.

Biological action
Conserve or release natural enemies.

Botanical action
Use an appropriate plant-derived product.

Chemical action
Use a registered pesticide when justified.

15. Component 5 — Management
IPM combines several methods.
The major methods are:
Cultural+Mechanical+Biological+Botanical+Chemical+Genetic+Physical+Behavioral+Regulatory ed  Cultural+ Mechanical+ Biological+ Botanical+ Chemical+ Genetic+ Physical+ Behavioral+
Regulatory  
Not every method is required in every crop.
The objective is to select compatible and effective combinations.

16. Cultural Control
Cultural control changes crop-management practices to make conditions less favorable for pests and
more favorable for the crop.
Examples:
- Crop rotation
- Timely sowing
- Proper spacing
- Balanced fertilizer application
- Irrigation management
- Field sanitation
- Removal of volunteer plants
- Weed management
- Resistant varieties
- Trap crops
- Cover crops
Concept:
Cultural practices→Crop competitiveness↑+Pest suitability↓ 

17. Mechanical Control
Mechanical control physically removes or suppresses pests.
Examples:
- Hand picking
- Removing egg masses
- Destroying infested plant parts
- Traps
- Barriers
- Netting
- Mechanical cultivation
- Pruning infested branches
Physical intervention→Pest population↓ 

18. Biological Control
Biological control uses living organisms to suppress pests.
Major agents:

Predators
- Ladybird beetles
- Lacewings
- Assassin bugs
- Ground beetles

Parasitoids
- Trichogramma
- Braconid wasps
- Ichneumonid wasps

Pathogens
- Beauveria
- Metarhizium

- Bacillus thuringiensis
General mechanism:
Natural enemy→Pest mortality→Pest population regulation 

19. Botanical Control
Botanical control uses plant-derived substances.
Examples:
- Neem
- Pyrethrum
- Karanja
- Garlic-derived products
- Other registered plant-derived pesticides
Potential effects include:
- Repellency
- Antifeedant action
- Toxicity
- Growth disruption
- Oviposition deterrence
Plant−derived compound→Pest behavioral/physiological disruption 
Botanicals are not automatically harmless and must be used appropriately.

20. Chemical Control
Chemical control uses registered pesticide active ingredients.
It can be highly effective when correctly selected and applied.
However:
Chemical control should be integrated, not automatically routine. 
Potential problems from inappropriate use include:

- Resistance
- Resurgence
- Secondary pests
- Residues
- Drift
- Non-target effects

21. Genetic Control
Genetic approaches include:

Resistant varieties
Plants are bred or selected to resist pests.
Resistant variety→Pest establishment/damage↓Resistant\ variety  Pest\
establishment/damage Host plant resistance
Can involve:
- Antixenosis
- Antibiosis
- Tolerance

## 22. Antixenosis
Antixenosis means the plant possesses characteristics that make it less attractive or suitable for pest
feeding, oviposition or colonization.
Plant trait→Pest preference↓Plant\ trait  Pest\ preference ## 23. Antibiosis
Antibiosis occurs when feeding on a plant adversely affects pest survival, growth, development or
reproduction.
Pest feeding→Plant resistance factor→Pest development/survival↓Pest\ feeding  Plant\
resistance\ factor  Pest\ development/survival ## 24. Tolerance
Tolerance means the plant can withstand pest injury with relatively limited reduction in yield or
performance.
Pest injury→Plant compensates→Yield loss relatively lowPest\ injury  Plant\ compensates
 Yield\ loss\ relatively\ low

25. Physical Control
Physical methods alter the environment or create barriers.
Examples:
- Insect-proof nets
- Mulches
- Solarization
- Temperature treatment
- Light barriers
- Physical barriers
- Soil flooding in suitable systems

26. Behavioral Control
Behavioral approaches manipulate pest behavior.
Examples include:
- Pheromone traps
- Mating disruption
- Attract-and-kill systems
- Repellents
- Trap crops
Basic concept:
Modify pest behavior→Reproduction/feeding/colonization↓ 

27. Regulatory Control
Regulatory measures prevent introduction or spread of pests.
Examples:
- Quarantine
- Movement restrictions
- Inspection
- Certification
- Pest-free areas
- Destruction of severely infested planting material where required
Concept:
Prevention of introduction/spread→Pest risk↓ 

28. IPM Is More Than "Using Many Methods"
Simply using many control methods does not automatically constitute IPM.
True integration requires:
Compatibility+Timing+Monitoring+Decision making 
For example:
Crop rotation+Natural enemies+Monitoring+Threshold−based pesticide useCrop\ rotation + Natural\
enemies + Monitoring + Threshold-based\ pesticide\ use
is more representative of IPM than randomly applying several pesticides.

29. Pest Monitoring and Threshold
Suppose:
Pest population<ETLPest\ population < ETL
The usual decision is:
Continue monitoring 
If:

Pest population≥ETLPest\ population  ETL
then:
Evaluate management options 
This does not automatically mean:
ETL exceeded→Spray immediatelyETL\ exceeded Spray\ immediately
Instead, consider all available compatible methods.

31. IPM and Pest Population Dynamics
Pest populations change through:
Pnext=Pcurrent+Births−Deaths+Immigration−Emigration  = P  current  + Births Deaths + Immigration - Emigration  
IPM attempts to increase natural mortality and reduce pest population growth while improving crop
resistance and resilience.

32. IPM and Natural Enemies
Natural enemies are an important part of pest regulation.
Pest↑→Natural enemies↑→Pest mortality↑→Pest population↓Pest  Natural\
enemies  Pest\ mortality  Pest\ population Therefore unnecessary broad-spectrum pesticide use can interfere with natural regulation.

33. Conservation Biological Control
Conservation biological control means protecting existing natural enemies.
Methods include:
- Reducing unnecessary pesticide applications
- Using selective pesticides when suitable
- Maintaining flowering resources
- Providing refuges
- Maintaining habitat diversity
- Avoiding destruction of beneficial insects
Goal:

Natural enemy survival↑→Natural pest control↑ 

34. Augmentative Biological Control
Here natural enemies are mass-produced and released.
Mass production→Release→Natural enemy population↑→Pest suppressionMass\ production
 Release  Natural\ enemy\ population  Pest\ suppression
Examples include releases of parasitoids such as Trichogramma in suitable systems.

35. Classical Biological Control
Classical biological control generally involves introducing and establishing a natural enemy against an
invasive pest after appropriate ecological and regulatory assessment.
Because introduced organisms can have ecological consequences, such programs require careful
evaluation and regulation.

36. IPM and Pesticide Resistance
One of IPM's major benefits is reducing unnecessary selection pressure.
Repeated chemical use can produce:
Pesticide→Susceptible pests die→Less−sensitive pests survive→Reproduction→ResistancePesticide
 Susceptible\ pests\ die  Less-sensitive\ pests\ survive  Reproduction
 Resistance
IPM reduces dependence on a single pesticide approach.

37. Resistance Management in IPM
Important principles:

1. Use pesticides only when justified.
2. Rotate effective MOAs.
Rotate MOA, not merely brands 

3. Use non-chemical methods.
4. Monitor control failure.
5. Avoid unnecessary repeated applications.
6. Prevent survivors from contributing to future generations.

38. IPM and Pest Resurgence
Broad-spectrum pesticides can reduce natural enemies.
Pesticide→Pest↓+Natural enemies↓Pesticide  Pest + Natural\
enemies If natural enemies recover more slowly:
Natural enemies↓→Pest reproduction succeeds→Pest resurgenceNatural\ enemies  Pest\ reproduction\ succeeds  Pest\ resurgence
IPM attempts to reduce this risk through selective and need-based management.

39. IPM and Secondary Pests
Suppose pest A and pest B occur together.
A broad-spectrum pesticide may kill:
Pest A↓Pest\ A but also:
Natural enemies of Pest B↓Natural\ enemies\ of\ Pest\ B Then:
Pest B↑Pest\ B Therefore IPM considers the whole pest complex, not just one pest.

40. IPM and Crop Stage
Pest importance varies with crop development.
The same pest density may have different consequences during:
- Seedling stage
- Vegetative stage

- Flowering
- Fruit formation
- Grain filling
- Maturity
Therefore:
ETL=f(Crop+Pest+Stage+Environment+Economics)
 

41. IPM and Weather
Weather affects:
- Pest development
- Reproduction
- Migration
- Disease development
- Natural enemy activity
- Pesticide performance
Temperature can be incorporated into pest forecasting through:
GDD=∑max⁡(0,Tmean−Tb) -T b)  
This can help predict developmental events for suitable pest species.

42. IPM and Pest Forecasting
Forecasting attempts to predict pest occurrence or population development before severe damage
- ccurs.
Inputs can include:
- Temperature
- Rainfall
- Humidity
- Crop stage
- Pest history
- Degree-days

- Trapping data
- Field scouting
Conceptually:
Weather+Crop stage+Pest biology+Monitoring→Risk forecastWeather+Crop\ stage+Pest\
biology+Monitoring  Risk\ forecast

43. IPM and Precision Agriculture
Modern IPM can use:
- GPS
- GIS
- Drones
- Remote sensing
- Smart traps
- AI image recognition
- IoT sensors
- Digital scouting
- Variable-rate spraying
For example:
Camera→Image→AI detection→Pest mapping→Targeted treatmentCamera  Image
 AI\ detection  Pest\ mapping  Targeted\ treatment
This can support site-specific pest management.

44. IPM and Economic Efficiency
IPM aims to optimize:
Crop protection+Yield+Profit−Unnecessary control cost 
A management decision should consider:
- Pest population
- Expected yield loss
- Crop price

- Control cost
- Control effectiveness
- Natural enemies
- Environmental risk

45. Cost-Benefit Principle
A simple decision concept is:
Expected benefit of control>Cost of control 
If the expected economic benefit is lower than the cost and risk, treatment may not be justified.

46. IPM and Sustainability
IPM supports sustainability by attempting to reduce:
- Unnecessary pesticide use
- Resistance development
- Non-target impacts
- Environmental contamination
- Production costs
while maintaining:
- Crop productivity
- Crop quality
- Farm profitability
- Natural pest regulation

47. IPM and Organic Agriculture
IPM and organic farming are not synonymous.
IPM is a pest-management decision framework.
Organic agriculture is a production system governed by specific standards and restrictions.
Organic farms can use IPM principles, and conventional farms can also use IPM.
IPM≠Organic farming 

48. IPM and Conventional Agriculture
IPM can be practiced in conventional systems.
For example:
Monitoring+ETL+Biological control+Selective pesticideMonitoring + ETL + Biological\ control +
Selective\ pesticide
is an IPM approach even if the farm uses synthetic fertilizers or registered synthetic pesticides.

49. Example — IPM for Aphids
Suppose aphids are observed on a crop.

Step 1 — Identify
Confirm that the insects are aphids.

Step 2 — Monitor
Count aphids on representative plants.

Step 3 — Check damage
Observe:
- Curling
- Yellowing
- Stunting
- Honeydew
- Sooty mold

Step 4 — Check natural enemies
Look for:
- Ladybird beetles
- Lacewing larvae
- Syrphid larvae
- Aphid parasitoid mummies

Step 5 — Compare with threshold
Use locally validated crop- and stage-specific recommendations.

Step 6 — Select management
Possible options:
- Cultural practices
- Mechanical removal in small areas
- Conservation of natural enemies
- Appropriate botanical product
- Selective registered insecticide if justified

Step 7 — Re-monitor
Determine whether aphid density and damage are declining.
This is IPM because the decision is based on identification + monitoring + thresholds + multiple
compatible methods.

50. Example — IPM for Caterpillars
Eggs→Larvae→Pupae→AdultsEggs  Larvae  Pupae  Adults
Management can include:

Prevention
- Crop rotation
- Field sanitation

Monitoring
- Pheromone traps
- Egg scouting
- Larval counts

Biological
- Parasitoids
- Predators
- Microbial control agents

Mechanical
- Hand removal of egg masses in small fields

Chemical
- Registered insecticide when threshold and local recommendations justify treatment
The timing should target the susceptible life stage specified by the management
recommendation/product label.

51. IPM Example — Integrated Combination
Consider:
Resistant variety+Crop rotation+Healthy planting material+Regular scouting+Natural enemy conservat
ion+Trap monitoring+Threshold based treatment 
This is substantially stronger than:
Routine calendar spraying 
because it combines prevention, monitoring, biological regulation and need-based intervention.

52. IPM Calendar vs IPM
Calendar spraying
Every X days→SprayEvery\ X\ days  Spray
regardless of actual pest population.

IPM
Monitor→Assess→Threshold→DecisionMonitor  Assess  Threshold  Decision
Therefore:
Calendar spraying≠ IPM 

53. Advantages of IPM
Agricultural advantages
- Better pest monitoring
- Reduced unnecessary control
- Protection of yield

- Better timing
- More sustainable pest management

Economic advantages
- Potential reduction in unnecessary pesticide expenditure
- Better use of inputs
- Reduced resistance-related costs

Ecological advantages
- Natural-enemy conservation
- Pollinator protection
- Reduced contamination
- Better ecosystem balance

Long-term advantages
- Resistance management
- Reduced dependence on a single control method
- Improved resilience

54. Limitations and Challenges of IPM
IPM is not effortless.
Challenges include:

1. Requires knowledge
Farmers need to identify pests and natural enemies.

2. Requires monitoring
Regular scouting takes time.

3. Thresholds are crop- and region-specific
A single threshold cannot be applied everywhere.

4. Decision making is more complex
Multiple factors must be considered.

5. Biological control can be variable
Weather and ecology affect natural enemies.

6. Initial knowledge/training requirements
Farmers may need extension support.

7. Some severe outbreaks require rapid intervention
IPM does not mean refusing chemical control when it is justified.

55. IPM and "Zero Pesticide" Concept
IPM does not necessarily mean:
No pesticides ever 
Instead:
Use pesticides only when justified, and use them responsibly. 
Chemical control remains one possible component.

56. IPM and "Zero Pest" Concept
IPM does not aim for:
Pest=0Pest=0
because complete eradication is often unnecessary, impractical or ecologically undesirable.
Instead:
Pest population<Economic damage level 

57. IPM Pyramid
A useful conceptual hierarchy is:
## Chemical Control
▲
│
## Biological / Botanical
▲
│

## Mechanical / Physical
▲
│
## Cultural Control
▲
│
## Prevention
The exact order is not rigid in every crop, but the concept emphasizes prevention and non-chemical
measures first where practical, with chemical control used when justified.

58. IPM Core Principles
The most important principles are:

Principle 1
Prevention before outbreak.

Principle 2
Identify before controlling.

Principle 3
Monitor before deciding.

Principle 4
Use thresholds.

Principle 5
Prefer compatible and least-disruptive effective methods.

Principle 6
Conserve natural enemies.

Principle 7
Use pesticides responsibly when needed.

Principle 8
Rotate modes of action to manage resistance.

Principle 9
Evaluate the result.

Principle 10
Continue monitoring after intervention.

59. IPM Master Formula
There is no single mathematical equation that completely defines IPM, but conceptually:
IPM=Prevention+Identification+Monitoring+Threshold−based Decisions+Integrated Control+Evaluati
- n 
Or:
IPM=Cultural+Mechanical+Biological+Botanical+Chemical+Genetic+Physical+Behavioral+Regulator
y 
where only the compatible and appropriate components are selected for a particular crop-pest system.

60. Complete IPM Workflow
1. PREVENT↓2. IDENTIFY↓3. MONITOR↓4. ASSESS DAMAGE↓5. CHECK ETL/EIL↓6. EVALU
ATE NATURAL ENEMIES↓7. SELECT COMPATIBLE CONTROL↓8. APPLY CORRECTLY↓9. RE
-MONITOR↓10. EVALUATE AND ADJUST  & \\

& \\ & \\ &

62. Key Terms Related to IPM
Term

Meaning
Pest
Organism causing economically important damage
Monitoring
Regular observation and measurement
ETL
Point at which management action should begin
EIL
Economic break-even pest density
Natural enemy
Predator, parasitoid or pathogen that suppresses pests
Biological control Use of living organisms for pest suppression
Cultural control Modification of crop practices
Botanical control Plant-derived pest suppression
Chemical control Use of pesticide chemicals
Resistance
Inherited reduced susceptibility
Resurgence
Pest population rebound following control
Secondary pest
Pest becoming important after disruption of its regulation
IPM
Integrated decision-based pest-management system

63. Common Misconceptions
"IPM means no pesticides."

❌ Incorrect.

Chemical control can be part of IPM.

"IPM means using every control method."

❌ Incorrect.

Only compatible and appropriate methods should be integrated.

"Seeing one pest means spraying."

❌ Incorrect.

Assess population, damage, crop stage, natural enemies and threshold.

"Biological control always eliminates pests."

❌ Incorrect.

Its objective is generally population suppression/regulation, not necessarily eradication.

"Botanical products are always safe."

❌ Incorrect.

Natural substances can be biologically active and toxic.

"IPM is only for organic farming."

❌ Incorrect.

IPM can be used in organic, conventional and integrated production systems.

64. Exam-Ready Answer
Integrated Pest Management (IPM) is an ecosystem-based and decision-oriented
approach to pest management that combines compatible cultural, mechanical,
biological, botanical, chemical, genetic, physical, behavioral and regulatory methods.
It begins with prevention and correct pest identification, followed by regular
monitoring and assessment of pest density and crop damage. Management decisions
are based on economic thresholds, pest biology, crop stage, environmental conditions
and natural-enemy activity. Chemical pesticides are used only when justified and are
applied according to proper label, safety and resistance-management principles. The
- verall objective is to maintain pest populations below economically damaging levels
while minimizing risks to humans, beneficial organisms and the environment.

65. Final One-Read Summary
The entire IPM concept can be remembered as:
PREVENT→IDENTIFY→MONITOR→THRESHOLD→DECIDE→INTEGRATE→REMONITOR         
And:
IPM=Cultural+Mechanical+Biological+Botanical+Chemical+Genetic+Physical+Behavioral+Regulator
y 

The most important principle is:
IPM does not try to eliminate every pest; it keeps pest populations below economically damaging levels
using the most appropriate combination of management methods.   

Ultimate memory line
IPM = Prevent first → identify correctly → monitor continuously → compare with
thresholds → conserve natural enemies → integrate compatible controls → use
chemicals only when justified → evaluate → monitor again.
